On build-5a43d4b's module the recovered call answered the unknown outcome for both harnesses; on this build the spawn answered `{"agent_id":1,...,"status":{"state":"running"}}` with one child in the directory, and the send answered `{"disposition":"queued","message_id":1,...}`, the first run's ID, with the message reaching the child once (one child request whose newest input carries it, before and after). `output/durability/restart.mjs` with `SCENARIO=nap` (an `exec` with a 120 s yield whose cell sleeps 8 s and prints the cell time it slept), killed 3 s in and then restarted three more times 3 s apart: the cell answered at +20.2 s on build-5a43d4b (the sleep restarted from scratch on each start) and at +8.1 s on this build (`woke after 8005 ms of cell time`, the clock read replayed). `SCENARIO=waityield` (an `exec` with a 300 ms yield whose cell sleeps 40 s, then a `wait` with a 20 s yield, killed 8 s into the wait): the recovered wait yielded at +29.5 s on build-5a43d4b (8 s plus a whole 20 s after the restart) and at +21.4 s here. In Miniflare (`output/durability/cf/run.mjs`, `SCENARIO=stuck`: a cell that sleeps 20 s, the instance disposed and recreated every 4 s four times) the turn completed after 37.2 s on build-5a43d4b and 20.3 s here; `nap` (6 s, one restart at 2.5 s) 9.3 s and 6.4 s; `progress` (six restarts) completed with 19 model calls on both. `restart.mjs` (`plain`, `sleep`, `compute`, `yield`, `yieldtext`: `lookup` run once with its real output; `sleep` answered 3.3 s after its request where it restarted a whole 3 s sleep before) and `mcp-resume.mjs` (`resume` and `lose` with the work run once, `plain` and `unknown` the unknown outcome) answered as recorded above. On production (test wallet, a 2 MACH key revoked after; build-041a91f deployed as 2650438, version 31ed1692 at 05:35 UTC), a Codex and a Claude agent on DeepSeek V4.1 Flash each ran one cell that spawned a subagent, sent it a deferred message and waited on it, while the subagent's cell slept 840 s; origin/main was deployed again unchanged from a clean worktree at 05:38:08 (version 9e7a9fb8), and at 05:43:11-12 both leads and both subagents were reset (each run started again with the subagent-recovery notice and `recovered: true` on the lead turns). The subagents' sleeps ended on time: `slept 840000 ms from 2026-11-08T05:35:29.836Z to 2026-11-08T05:49:29.836Z` (Codex) and `slept 840001 ms from 05:35:26.115Z to 05:49:26.116Z` (Claude), the recovered `exec` reporting 378 s of wall time since the reset; the leads' replayed cells answered `SPAWN {"agent_id":1,...}`, `SEND {"disposition":"queued",...,"message_id":1,...}` and the completed wait, no event of either agent or subagent mentioned an unknown outcome or a host crash, and the message reached each subagent once (delivered with disposition `started` after its result). The spawn and the send had completed before the reset, so production exercised their journaled results, not their new recovery, which the Node reproduction covers. The run cost 0.0081 USD of model calls.

A model call that a payer or gateway answers 429, 502, 503, 504 or 529 before its response starts keeps retrying for up to ten minutes (2026-11-08, SDK 9a695d182). tempoxyz/gateway answers an agent payer that is busy, unreachable, timed out or answering 5xx with 429 and `Retry-After` (5 s when it could not reach the payer), and during gakonst/swarms' deploy drill of 2026-11-08 a Codex subagent on GPT-6.1 Sol whose calls its payer refused as busy failed for good after five tries in 9.9 s with `durable operation recovery execution policy failed`: a subagent's turn that fails is `failed`, whatever its disposition, and the Codex client tried five times, the Claude one ten times or 180 s for a 429 and handed a 5xx to durable recovery at once. Now such a status is patient in both clients (`retry::patient_status`, `retry::PATIENCE` in `nanocode-http`): it is retried with the client's backoff (Codex 1 to 16 s, Claude 0.5 to 32 s), waiting at least the server's `Retry-After`, until the call has waited 600 s, longer than the 1.5 to 7 minutes after a deploy in which Cloudflare resets objects still on the old code (Swarms and gateway drills of 2026-11-08). 402 and other final answers fail on the first try as before. `output/durability/thr.mjs` (Node 22.23, node:sqlite, a scripted Responses or Messages model; `PROVIDER=codex|claude`, `STATUS`, `RETRY_AFTER`, `THROTTLE_MS`, `TARGET=sub|lead`): a lead cell with a 30-minute yield spawns a subagent and waits on it, and the mock answers the subagent's model calls with `STATUS` for `THROTTLE_MS`, then normally. On build-041a91f's module, with 90 s of refusals, the Codex subagent failed after 5 tries in 8.4 s on 429 (Retry-After 2) and 20.4 s on 503 (Retry-After 5), the Claude subagent after 20.4 s on 503, and with 240 s of 429 after 10 tries in 158.8 s; with `TARGET=lead` the Codex lead's turn gave up after 5 tries in 20.1 s and asked its host for runtime recovery. On this build all of them completed: the subagents with `child-done` after 97.2, 102.7, 110.7 (Claude 429, which already passed with 90 s) and 119.6 s, the 240 s Claude one after 267.9 s, and the lead in place after 122.2 s; 402 failed the subagent on its only try on both builds. On production (gateway 77b561d, version 0fa1a0d1) with a fresh test-wallet key and an agent payer that answered 503 for 120 s from the subagent's first ask: before the pin the Codex subagent failed after 5 tries in 20.6 s; after it, the subagent was retried 10 times (Codex) and 8 times (Claude) and both completed with their leads.

The WebAssembly build has no arc-swap (SDK e67644b0e, 2026-11-07, which nanocode-ts builds from with clabby's nine SDK commits before it). nanocode-sync's `Atom` wrapped `arc_swap::ArcSwap` and about thirty SDK files used `ArcSwap` and `ArcSwapOption` directly, whose hybrid debt strategy, there to make loads safe against concurrent swaps on other threads, has nothing to protect in this single-threaded module: twiggy on a build with symbol names (`CARGO_PROFILE_WASM_STRIP=none`) counted 402,248 bytes in 1,435 arc_swap functions. The SDK now routes every such use through nanocode-sync's `Swap` and `SwapOption`, which are arc-swap's types on native targets and, on `wasm`, a `crossbeam_utils` `AtomicCell<Option<Arc<T>>>` that loads by taking the `Arc` out, cloning it and putting it back, with the same methods (`load`, `load_full`, `store`, `swap`, `rcu`, `compare_and_swap`); `nanocode-types-core`, which may not depend on nanocode-sync, interns custom model ids in a `thread_local` set on `wasm`. Measured in scratch copies of this package's Rust inputs built the way `scripts/build.mjs` builds (`cargo build --profile wasm`, then `wasm-opt -O1`), SDK 0a98b23a1 against the same tree with only this change: the raw module went from 13,839,874 to 13,474,060 bytes, the optimized one from 12,365,639 to 12,029,846 (4,040,011 to 3,991,424 with gzip -9), and the named build kept no arc_swap function and no function of the replacement (all inlined). `dist/nanocode_wasm.wasm` built here against e67644b0e is 12,017,974 bytes (3,991,458 gzipped), against 12,350,030 (4,038,176) on 0a98b23a1. Both scratch modules answered alike in `output/durability/restart.mjs` (`SCENARIO=sleep`, `yield`, `compute`, `plain`: `lookup` run once and its real output after the kill), `mcp-resume.mjs` (`resume` and `lose` with the work run once, `plain` and `unknown` the unknown outcome) and `cf/run.mjs` (`progress` through six restarts completed in 23.6 and 23.5 s, `nap` with one restart answered `woke`), and so did this build; `scripts/measure-node.ts` over ten interleaved runs each (medians, Node 22.23) opened a Claude runtime in 36.3 and 34.9 ms and a Codex one in 11.4 and 11.9 ms with first turns of 96.2 and 93.7 ms and 43.3 and 43.8 ms, and `bench/hands.ts --hand=local --n=5` run four times alternating showed no difference beyond run-to-run noise (`callback-seq-500` 41.9 to 44.7 ms a turn on either). This build writes more per turn than 0a98b23a1 for reasons in clabby's commits, not this change (`hands.ts` `noop` 19 SQL transactions and 30.0 KB a turn instead of 12 and 17.2, `callback-seq-500` 620 instead of 516). nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare's `npm run drill` (`charge` 2.5 s with one charge, `waiting` 7.8 s with one charge, `deploys` 4.1 s) and nanocodexyz/durable-vercel's eight runs passed on this tree packed (`npm pack`) into scratch copies; in the Vercel copy `npm install` of the tarball left the nested `node_modules/nanocode-ts/node_modules/undici` empty, which was filled from this checkout's undici 7.30.1 before the drill.

Workspace files and attachments (`src/internal/Files.ts`, 2026-10-24). `nanocode-ts/cloudflare` answers `GET /sessions/:name/files/<absolute path>` from the session object's `SqlFileSystem`, and `POST /sessions/:name/files?name=<file name>` (the raw bytes, at most `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES`, 5 MiB) writes `/workspace/uploads/<safe name>` (a taken name gets `-2`, `-3`, at most 64 files; with `&replace=1` it overwrites the taken name instead, which does not count against the 64, as tempoxyz/gateway's scp and SFTP uploads do so a second `scp data.csv` replaces the first instead of leaving the prompt's `uploads/data.csv` stale) and answers the `Attachment` `{name, path, type, size}`, and `GET /sessions/:name/files/<directory>?list` (`Files.listing`) answers `{path, entries: [{name, kind, size, mtime}]}` sorted by name, 404 for a missing directory and 400 for a file, which SFTP's `ls` and `scp -r` need (2026-11-05); both reach the account's session object like every session route, so a read-only caller may GET but not POST. `nanocode-ts/vercel`'s `GET /api/chat?sessionId=&file=` runs the app's `authorize`, replays the run's committed SQL batches into a `LocalSql` and reads the same `SqlFileSystem`. A durable-harness submission carries only text, so a POST's `attachments` (`[{path, data}]` with base64 data, at most 8 files and `VERCEL_ATTACHMENT_BYTES`, 3 MiB, per message, paths under `/workspace/uploads/`) ride at the end of the text between `ATTACHMENTS_OPEN` and `ATTACHMENTS_CLOSE`, and the drive that places the prompt strips them, writes them through the journal and keeps their images. `fileResponse` reads only under the workspace root (no `..`, no NUL), sniffs the type from the bytes (PNG, JPEG, GIF, WebP, AVIF, BMP, PDF, SVG by its root element, UTF-8 as `text/plain`, anything else `application/octet-stream` as an attachment), answers 413 above 8 MiB and always sends `x-content-type-options: nosniff` and a `sandbox` content security policy, so an SVG or HTML file opened directly runs no script on the site's origin. The module's `submit` takes `images` (data URLs) and makes the prompt `UserInput::Text` plus `UserInput::Image` items, so the model sees attached images as input images: Cloudflare reads the turn body's `images` (workspace paths) and sends at most 4 PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP files, none to Workers AI models, and Vercel's `promptImages(session)` decides per model. On the client, `Transport.files` (`TransportFiles`: `upload`, `read`, `maxBytes`, `scope`) and `Config.send(prompt, attachments)` carry attachments: the transports add one `Attached: <path> (<type>, <size>)` line per file to the prompt (`attachedPrompt`), the user entry keeps them as `attachments`, and a message sent while a turn runs may not carry any. In `nanocode-ts/react`, `imageSource` and `Image` also take `workspace:/workspace/...`, `sandbox:/workspace/...`, `file:///workspace/...` and bare `/workspace/...` sources (`workspaceFile`, also accepted in tool-output images), which `Image` reads through the config's `files` into a blob URL, cached 15 s per session and path, and only a raster image opens in a new tab. `workspaceFile` decodes percent escapes, because markdown turns a source's spaces and non-ASCII letters into them, and `Markdown` wraps a `workspace:` or `sandbox:` link or image destination that contains whitespace in `<...>` before parsing: on 2026-10-25 Haiku 5.5 on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare and /vercel answered with `![chart](workspace:/workspace/viem chart.png)`, which CommonMark does not read as an image, so the page showed the raw text, and `workspace:/workspace/chart ü.png` written with `<...>` or `%20` reached `files.read` as `chart%20%C3%BC.png` and was not found. `useAttachments()` is the headless composer state (hidden file input, paste, drop, uploads, `ready`), `AttachmentList` renders the parts `attachments`, `attachment` (`data-status`, `data-image`), `attachmentThumb`, `attachmentName`, `attachmentSize` and `attachmentRemove`, and `Chat` adds an `attach` button, takes pasted and dropped files and lists a user entry's attachments. Measured 2026-10-24 on local workerd and next dev with gpt-6-luna: asked what an attached 200x150 PNG of a green triangle shows, the model answered "Green triangle" with the image and "NO IMAGE" without it on both hosts.

`nanocode-ts/vercel` runs a session as a durable-harness kernel `Harness` (`nanocodeHarness`, for `durableAgent({ harness })` from `durable-harness`, an optional peer dependency): every SQL write of the SDK's store and turn log is a record-only kernel step `nanocode.sql` keyed `sql:<n>` in the session's Workflow run (since 2026-10-27 its input is `{ packed }`: the batch's transactions as JSON with each blob replaced by `{ $b: n }`, followed by the blobs, compressed with brotli quality 3, because devalue writes blobs as base64 and a `git clone` journaled every file that way; a zyzz clone's batches went from 19.0 MB of devalue text to 3.9 MB and a vercel/ai clone's from 124.7 MB to 51.7 MB in a local run with a recording kernel, and `committedBatches` still reads the earlier `{ transactions }` form, while builds before this cannot read a session written by it; a batch holds up to 24 MiB of raw writes (`BATCH_BYTES`, 4 MiB before), because on the Vercel demo each batch waited 270 to 470 ms for its event (a zyzz clone's drive waited 3.8 s over 14 batches, a vercel/ai clone's 18.6 s over 40), and 24 MiB written as base64 stays under Workflow's 50 MB step input even when nothing compresses), each drive replays the committed batches into a `node:sqlite` `:memory:` database (`SqlJournal`, `LocalSql`; it binds each `ArrayBuffer` as a `Uint8Array`, because Node 22.23's `node:sqlite` binds an `ArrayBuffer` only as a statement's first parameter and refused `SqlFileSystem`'s file rows with "Provided value cannot be bound to SQLite parameter 5", so nanocodexyz/durable-vercel's drill failed its workspace turn on Node 22 until 2026-10-28, while Node 24.21 binds it anywhere), submissions become turns of the SDK's `TurnInbox`, host tools are durable-harness `HostTool`s run as kernel steps `tool:<call_id>`, logged events reach the live stream in durable-harness's vocabulary, and model requests go to the Vercel AI Gateway with `AI_GATEWAY_API_KEY` or the Vercel OIDC token (`@vercel/oidc`, an optional peer dependency), unless the session runs on a plan: `App.create`'s `account(request)` stores the account a new session runs as in its kernel config (`account`), and on every drive `Harness.create`'s `subscription({ account, provider, model })` may return the account's ChatGPT or Claude plan (`Harness.Subscription`, a `dispatch` and, for Codex, a `socket`), which opens the session with `subscription: true` on `Gateway.planEndpoint(provider)` (`https://chatgpt.com/backend-api/codex`, or Claude's `https://api.anthropic.com/v1/messages?beta=true`) with no gateway and sends its model requests and sockets there; nanocodexyz/egress implements it with the plans' sign-in and sealed credentials. A signed-in account's requests reach its plan endpoint through the plan's dispatch, and a signed-out account and a session without an account keep the gateway's. A session's provider, model and reasoning effort come from the `nanocode: { provider, model, thinking, max_tokens }` entry of its kernel config when the host starts it with one (`agent.sessions.start({ config })`), as nanocodexyz/durable-vercel does for the harness and model a visitor picks, and otherwise from the harness options; the `nanocode.session` effect records them, so every drive opens the same backend and model, and the model endpoint follows the session's provider. A prompt whose whole text is `/effort <level>` is a command, not a turn: the drive checks the level against the session's model with `checkThinking`, records it or the SDK's refusal as that submission's placement, and sends the live stream `{"type":"effort","thinking"}` or an `error`. A submission of kind `steer` that arrives while a turn runs steers it: the drive watches for submissions while `drive` runs (durable-harness's `onHint`, or every `WAIT_POLL_MS` without hints), steers each with its submission ID, records the placement and sends the live stream `{"type":"steered"}`, and `text` and `reasoning` deltas carry the `item` they belong to, the output item or `reasoning:<model call>`, so a reply that follows a steer starts a new message; one that no running turn takes is submitted as a turn when the drive returns. `streamToolInput: true` passes `stream_tool_input`, so the live stream also carries `tool_input` events while the model writes a tool call. `maxPrompts` caps the prompts a session takes, counting every submission but `chaos` and `withdraw` in log order: the drive records a submission past it as placed with `nanocode.refused` and sends the live stream an `error`, and does not steer with it, so a public host enforces its cap inside the drive instead of reading the session before each submission, which cost nanocodexyz/durable-vercel 82–108 ms per prompt on 2026-10-21. Every later prompt is submitted with the effort in force as `metadata.thinking`, derived from the placements before it in the run's log so each drive derives the same one, and the drive calls `setThinking` before it drives a turn whose effort differs from the last it applied. `durability: "on"` acknowledges writes at once under the kernel's run-ahead durability and `"strict"` only after their batch commits. Under `"on"` the journal starts a batch at most every `batchMs` (`DEFAULT_BATCH_MS`, 500 ms), and at once for `durable()` (before a placement, a host tool, a steer and the settled marker), while a read neither waits nor starts a batch (since 2026-11-04, and under `"strict"` a read still waits for every earlier write), because the kernel gives a drive's delivery up ("demoted: durability stalled": a continuation recovers the session and the drive ends with `Yield('preempted')`) once its oldest run-ahead write is 5 s old, while its backpressure only waits for the queue once that write is 2 s old. Each batch is two World writes acknowledged one at a time, and nanocode logs every 50 ms while a model streams: on 2026-10-23, with world-local delayed to 150–190 ms a write, one batch per event-loop turn demoted every drive 5.8–6.3 s into a streaming turn and the turn never finished, while 500 ms batches finished it in one drive at 150, 190 and 300 ms a write. Large batches still demoted: on 2026-10-23 a cell that cloned a repository or wrote 8 MB on nanocode-vercel-durable was released mid-cell, so the model saw a host crash (with world-local delayed by 150 ms plus 1 ms per KB, every drive demoted 5 s in and re-ran the cell). `createNanocode` therefore runs the kernel strict unless `runAhead: true`: the journal still acknowledges writes at once under `"on"` and commits its batches in the background, and with the kernel strict the same cell finished in one drive. A batch also holds at most 4 MiB of statement values (`BATCH_BYTES`; a larger transaction is a batch of its own), and while more than that waits to commit, a write is acknowledged only once its batch commits, so a cell that writes faster than the World acknowledges is held back instead of queueing the whole workspace in memory and committing it as one step input (Vercel allows 50 MB per step input). On 2026-10-24 three public sessions on nanocode.xyz/vercel cloning vercel/ai (87 MB) at once got their shared Function killed with "instance was killed because it ran out of available memory", together with a fourth session on that instance, and their turns resumed about 265 s later; in Node with world-local one session cloning it peaked at 1,259 MB RSS with a 41 MB batch that took 5.3 s to commit and released the cell, and with the cap at 797 MB with batches of at most 4.2 MB and the cell kept running. `openReader` answers the SDK's reader commands from a session's fold. It reads the module from `dist/nanocode_wasm.wasm` beside itself (`new URL("../nanocode_wasm.wasm", import.meta.url)`). nanocodexyz/durable-vercel records its design, limits and measurements. Each completed model call is also published as a live `usage` event (`ModelCall`: index, replayed, first_output_ms, duration_ms, input_tokens, cached_tokens, output_tokens), so a Vercel chat stream shows per-call usage and prompt caching as Cloudflare's does; clients ignore it.

A Vercel drive starts its first model request before the turn's placement is durable (2026-11-05). `place` submits the prompt to the SDK and writes its `nanocode.place` step behind `journal.durable()` in the background. The drive awaits every placement before it reads `ctx.pending()` again, and the steer loop skips a submission whose placement is in flight. Under the strict kernel each step is two World writes, acknowledged one after the other. Before this change, a follow-up turn waited for three steps (the batch of the store's acquisition, the batch of the submission, and the placement) before its model request: 330 to 670 ms on Vercel on 2026-11-04.

A new session's `nanocode.session` step takes its UUIDv7 from the ULID of the run ID, so the drive also writes that step in the background. A session recorded before keeps its recorded ID, and a stored value that differs from the derived one throws `Restart`. A drive that starts with pending submissions skips its first `ctx.refresh()`. `callTool` awaits `journal.durable()` only before a tool whose `replay` is `never`. `Harness.create({ codeMode: false })` sends `code_mode: false`, and the session offers its tools directly instead of through `exec` and `wait`.

`App.create` serves its own `chatRoute` from `durable-harness`. A prompt that starts a session reads the live stream from index 0 without `streams.getInfo`, and a prompt to an existing session starts its submission while `liveEnd` reads the tail. The drive starts only after both return, so the stream cannot skip an event. `drive: 'queue'` serves the turn on the flow route.

Measured on 2026-11-05 with `output/vercel-bench` in nanocodexyz/nanocode: one Next app on Vercel (iad1) with nanocode-ts 40bfe87 and this change side by side. The lanes used Claude Haiku 5.5 at low effort, durable-harness's `fxTools(virtual())` and a `charge` tool, in eight interleaved sessions of five turns from San Francisco. Results (p50):

| | before | after |
|---|---:|---:|
| five-turn session | 18,641 ms | 15,713 ms |
| new session, first turn | 2,333 ms | 1,715 ms |
| follow-up text turn | 1,731 ms | 1,215 ms |
| turn with two tool calls | 3,889 ms | 2,986 ms |

The Vercel drill (claude and codex, on and strict, with `codeMode: false`) passed: one charge each, and the resumed turn reported the unknown outcome.

`nanocode-ts/cloudflare` is the Durable Object side: `DurableObjectSql` is the `SqlHost` over a Durable Object's `storage.sql.exec` and `transactionSync`, with `value_limit` set by its third constructor argument, `SQLITE_VALUE_LIMIT_BYTES` (8 MiB, where workerd's SQLite answers SQLITE_TOOBIG) by default, and an optional observer of each transaction's statements, rows read and written and duration. Cloudflare documents 2 MB per string, BLOB or row for SQLite-backed Durable Objects while workerd stored values of about 8.38 MB in production, so the limit is the host's policy, and a statement that binds a string or BLOB above it throws before any statement of its transaction runs, instead of leaving the failure to SQLite partway through. Its storage type is structural, so the adapter needs no `@cloudflare/workers-types`. nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare builds its session Durable Object on it.

`SqlFileSystem(sql, scope)` from `nanocode-ts/bash` is a `FileSystem` for `Bash` on any `SqlHost`: one `nanocode_files` table (scope, path, parent, kind, data, mtime, mode; a symlink is kind `link` with its target in `data`, so the schema did not change) written as `store` transactions, so files live wherever the session's SQL does: in the Durable Object's SQLite on Cloudflare and in the journaled SQLite of the Workflow run on Vercel, where they replay with the session. A file's first MiB (or the host's `value_limit`, if smaller) stays in its `nanocode_files` row and the rest goes to `nanocode_file_parts` (scope, path, part, data) in parts of that size, so rows written before parts existed read unchanged, an append rewrites only the last part, and a file is at most `maxFileBytes` (64 MiB; `createNanocode` passes 32 MiB, `workspace.maxFileBytes` overrides it); `rename` moves a directory's subtree and its parts in one transaction. `list` reads each child's kind, size, mtime and mode in the same query and keeps them for a second, until the instance's next write (a generation counter drops a listing that a write overtook), so the `stat` or `lstat` that `find`, `ls -l`, `du` and `grep -r` make for each listed entry costs no query, and `lstat` reads a file's data only for a symlink. Before 2026-10-25 `lstat` read every file's whole body: in Node over a file-backed `node:sqlite` holding vercel/ai (9,547 files, 120 MB), `find -type f` took 10.2 s and 14,515 transactions and `grep -rn TODO --include='*.ts'` 17.5 s, against 0.36 s with 1,658 and 0.73 s now, and on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare/chatgpt GPT-6 Luna's stats script over that clone ran two minutes before failing. Since the same day `time` is a pipeline keyword in `src/bash/internal/Syntax.ts` (`time (a | b)`, `time { ...; }`, `time -p`), writing `real`/`user`/`sys` to stderr after the pipeline; as a builtin, `time (find . | wc -l)` was a syntax error. On 2026-10-24 in workerd with a 2 MB SQL value limit, `seq 3000000 > big.txt` (22.9 MB) wrote and read back with the same MD5, where it had failed at the first 2 MB write. Every 64 `SqlFileSystem` transactions or 4 MiB of values read or written, the next one first yields a macrotask (`setTimeout(0)`), and `pause` yields every 64 calls as well as every 25 ms, because a Worker's clock does not advance during synchronous work and SQLite calls in a Durable Object are synchronous, so a `grep -r` over a cloned repository never yielded and held the session object's event loop: on 2026-10-24 on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare an events read of the session took 10.7 s and 9.8 s while three subagents grepped reth, and locally the slowest events read polled every 0.5 s during `grep -r 'fn ' reth | wc -l; find reth -type f | wc -l; grep -c heavy big/f1.txt` went from 0.91 s to 0.12 s (0.03 s in two more runs), the command taking 2.97 s before and 3.1 s after. Both runtimes open a `Bash` on it for every session unless `workspace: false`, at `/workspace` (`workspace.cwd`, seeded with `workspace.files`), and give it to the session twice: as `config.workspace` and the runtime's `workspace` host, so the provider's own workspace tools (Claude's `Bash`/`Read`/`Edit`/`Write`, Codex's `apply_patch`/`exec_command`) run on it, and behind the cells' `bash` global, so Code Mode cells call `bash` on the same files; its `curl` uses the cells' `fetch`. The shell's commands follow just-bash's light ones: besides the file and text tools, `tar` (ustar, `-z`), `gzip`/`gunzip`/`zcat` (on `CompressionStream`, file to file, since a command's output is text), `tree`, `rg` (a `grep -rnE` front end), `md5sum`, `od`, `xxd`, `strings`, `split`, `join`, `expand`, `file`, `env`, `which`, `timeout` (it stops waiting, not the command), `yes` (endless into a pipe, bounded by the output limit elsewhere) and `ln` (`-s` makes a symlink, a hard link copies). Pipeline stages stream: `yes`, `seq`, `cat`, `head`, `tail`, `wc`, `grep`, `sed`, `awk`, `cut`, `tr`, `uniq`, `tee` and `xargs` are `streaming` handlers that read stdin in line-aligned batches (`batches` and `eachBatch` in `commands/util.ts`), `head` closes stdin after its lines, and producers write through `put` or `pause`, which await `stdout.drain()` when the pipe is backlogged and yield a macrotask every 25 ms so the time limit can abort an endless pipeline. On 2026-10-24 in Node, `yes | head -1` and `seq 1000000000 | head -3` returned in 10 ms, and over 200 MB in 2,000 files `cat big/* | grep x | wc -l` took 0.83 s and `find big -type f -exec cat {} + | wc -l` 0.58 s with RSS within 70 MB of the files themselves; before, each stage buffered its whole input and all three failed at the 16 MB buffer limit. `wc -c` (`--bytes`) alone counts a regular file operand from `stat` instead of reading it, as GNU `wc` does with `fstat`: on 2026-11-04 over opentempi's D1 archive mount, `find 'ERA ISSUES' -type f -exec wc -c {} \;` (6,819 files, among them the parts of 221 MB documents) took 2.6 s and 4 queries, where it had not finished in 300 s and 2,449 reads, and in a production Temini turn it held a cell for 107 s until the cell gave up; 788 sampled archive files had the same size from `stat` as from `cat | wc -c`, and `wc` printed the same for files, stdin, `/dev/null`, a directory, a missing file and a symlink. `rg` with no path searches its stdin when stdin is a pipe or a redirection, empty or not, as ripgrep does, and the working directory otherwise (`Input.attached`: the top-level command, `sh -c` given no input, background jobs and `<&-` have no stdin). Before 2026-11-04 it searched the working directory whenever stdin was empty, so in a production Temini turn `sed -n 1720,1800p <file> | rg -v '^¹|^\s*$'`, whose `sed` printed nothing, searched all of `/workspace` with its archive mount for 37.5 s and failed at the 16 MB pipe limit; of 15 stdin cases only the three with an empty pipe changed. `grep` and `rg` run an expression of the form `.{M,N}REST` (after unwrapping whole-pattern `(?:…)` groups, without top-level alternation), where `REST` begins with literal text or a group whose branches all do, through `leadMatcher` in `commands/text.ts`: a match containing an occurrence of those opening literals at `p` starts at most `N` characters before `p`, so the line is searched from the next occurrence back `N` code points with a sticky copy of the expression, and a failure there rules out every start up to that occurrence, which gives the same matches as `exec` from the cursor. Natively each start position tries all `N` lengths, so the cost per line is `N` times its length: on 2026-11-05 `rg -n -i -o '.{0,250}τηλεδιο[ιί]κ.{0,250}' /workspace/archive` reset opentempi's session object in production on every run, while `τηλεδιο[ιί]κ.{0,250}` finished in 15 s, and over 300 lines of 2 to 20 KB the expression took 4.4 s natively and 2 ms this way. 180,000 random lines over 17 pattern shapes (lazy and exact repetitions, alternation groups, named back-references, escapes, quantified tails, wrapped groups) and the flags `g`, `gi`, `gu`, `giu` gave the same `test`, `matchAll` and first match as the native expression, and five archive searches over opentempi gave the same output with less CPU (the `-o` search 5.1 to 2.5 s). A file system with `search` may also offer `scanLimit(dir)`, the most files a search of `dir` may read when its index cannot narrow it: when the pattern has no literal of three or more characters, `rg` and `grep -r` walk the directory, and if more files than that remain they exit 2 with `the pattern has no literal of three or more characters for the index of this path, so it would read all N files (at most L without one); add such a literal or search a smaller directory`, before reading any (`rg --files` and named files are unaffected). opentempi's archive mount gives 5,000: `rg -il 'Ε.Ο.Δ.Α.Σ.Α.Α.Μ' /workspace/archive` read every document and reset the session object in production on each run. `Limits.maxMemoryBytes` (1 GiB by default; `createNanocode` sets 48 MiB, `workspace.limits` overrides it) is the memory a command may hold: whole-input reads, `sort`, `shuf`, `tac` with a multi-character separator, `base64`, `xxd`/`od`/`strings`/`gzip` inputs, `tar -c`, awk arrays, strings that `sed` (`N`, `G`, `H`), awk concatenation or `+=` grow, bash array assignments and the files a `wasm` or `python3` run loads (at most half of it) check an estimate first and fail with `exceeds this host's memory budget` (status 126), instead of crashing a 128 MB isolate. Pipes and output buffers flatten each chunk they keep (`flatten` reads one character), because a string built with `+=` is a tree of small pieces: `seq 3000000 | sort -n` held 141 MB live while reading 23 MB of such chunks and crashed the session Durable Object. `sort` without `-k`, `-c`, `-R`, `-f`, `-b`, `-h`, `-M`, `-V` or `-d` keeps its input as one string and sorts line offsets (integer keys packed with their offset in a `BigInt64Array`, other keys in a `Float64Array`, text by a merge sort of offsets), about 8 bytes per line; `rev` and `nl` stream, `tac` and `paste -s` write slices of their input, and `shuf` shuffles a `Uint32Array`. Measured on 2026-10-24 in Node (peak RSS growth): `seq 3000000 | sort -n | tail -1` 732 to 188 MB and 1.7 to 0.7 s, `sort` 737 to 197 MB, `sort -u` 878 to 191 MB, `tac` 487 to 109 MB, `shuf` 515 to 203 MB, `rev` 551 to 171 MB, `nl` 647 to 183 MB, `paste -sd,` 686 to 406 MB; and in workerd under `--max-old-space-size=128` the 3M-line `sort -n`, `sort` and `tac` that had crashed the process returned the right answer even under 64, while 6M lines fail with the budget error. A line longer than 1 MiB in a streaming stage is joined once, not on every chunk: `seq 3000000 | paste -sd, | wc -c` took 5.4 s before and 0.56 s after. `MemoryFileSystem` and `SqlFileSystem` keep a mode, an mtime and symlinks (`chmod`, `utimes`, `symlink`, `readlink`, `lstat`), resolve every path through links with `ELOOP` after 40 hops, and `test -L`, `ls -l`, `stat`, `find -type l`/`-xtype`/`-lname`/`-L`, `cp -a`/`-P`, `mv`, `rm`, `du`, `tar` and `pwd -P`/`cd -P` use `lstat`. just-bash's interpreters (`python3`, `sqlite3`, `js-exec`) and its `yq`, `xan` and HTML tools are left out.

`createNanocode(options)` from `nanocode-ts/cloudflare` (`src/cloudflare/App.ts`, moved from nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare with `store.ts`, `remote.ts` and `contracts.ts`) returns the `AgentSession` Durable Object class, the Worker `fetch` handler for the sessions API (`sessions/<s>/turns`, `events`, `warm`, `crash`, hands, metrics) and `deleteSession`; its host tools receive the calling agent's `context` in `HostToolCall`, and its egress receives each model request's `provider`; nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare's AGENTS.md documents that API and its options in full. Code Mode is always on (cells run in QuickJS inside the object, with `codeMode.fetch` or no network), a `SqlFileSystem` bash on the object's SQLite is on unless `workspace: false`, tool input streams unless `streamToolInput: false`, and subagents follow the SDK's default, which offers them, unless `subagents: false`. `mcp: { servers, fetch? }` gives every session `config.mcp_servers` (`servers` is a list or a function of the session's authority and env) and sends their requests, the dispatches with no `provider`, through `mcp.fetch` (the Worker's `fetch` without it) with their own method instead of to the egress's `/model`, so a host that passes a paying fetch pays MCP servers that answer JSON-RPC -32042 (mppx's HTTP transport retries such a call with the credential in `_meta`); without `mcp` no session has MCP servers. `HostOptions.blobs` (`BlobHost`: `put(key, bytes)`, `get(key)` answering the bytes or null, and an optional `threshold`, the store's `value_limit` by default) gives the SDK a content-addressed blob store: the module passes it to `SessionLog::blobs` and `ImageHistory::with_blob_store`, so prompt, steer and tool-output images and log values above the threshold live there instead of in SQL, and a model request loads only the images it carries inline. `nanocode-ts/cloudflare` keeps them in the session Durable Object's `blob_parts` table in `valueLimit`-byte parts (the SDK owns `blobs`: on 2026-10-24 both creating `blobs` broke every new Cloudflare demo session with no such column: blobs.state_id, so an object whose `blobs` table has this store's `part` column renames it on open), which `deleteAll` removes with the session; a host that imports a branch into another session copies the referenced blobs first. A Claude content tool's `tool.result` event still carries its images' base64 in `structured_result`. `valueLimit` is the session store's `DurableObjectSql` value limit, `SQLITE_VALUE_LIMIT_BYTES` by default, and its sessions instantiate with it as `log_logical_limit` as well as the store's `value_limit`, so an event above it is still logged as an `omitted` marker, a result above it fails its turn and a prompt above it is `too_large`, while a row whose columns together exceed the physical budget spills its body into `log_parts`; it also bounds a request body: the Worker and the object refuse a larger one with 413. `aiGateway(ai, gateway, serviceTier, firstByteMs)` is the egress that sends model requests through a Cloudflare AI Gateway binding; it holds each response until its first body byte and, when none arrives within `firstByteMs` (45 s), aborts the request and answers 504 `timeout_error` with `x-should-retry: true`, which the SDK retries like any 5xx, because on 2026-10-24 one Haiku 5.5 "say hi" turn on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare waited 269 s for its only model response while 18 others in the same minutes answered in 2 to 6 s (locally, a shim stalling the first call 30 s made a turn take 31.3 s, and 8.3 s with a 5 s limit), and `PublicLimit` with `client(address)` is a per-visitor rate-limiting Durable Object. `cloudflare({ model, headers, session, url })` from `nanocode-ts/cloudflare/transport` is the browser `Transport` for `createConfig`: it starts and streams turns over the sessions API, reconnects from the last event id, steers a running turn and queues a steer the object refuses with 409 as the next turn, cancels the open turn with `POST sessions/<s>/turns/<id>/cancel` (`cancel`, which also drops queued steers), and exposes `warm`, `crash`, `health` and `forget`.

An `AgentSession` keeps each scope's runtime lifecycle in one record (`lifecycles`, 2026-11-07): its WebAssembly instance, its opened runtime with the descriptor it was opened with (`opening`) and, once it opened, the runtime itself (`resident`), and the session reserved by a prewarm, where five maps (`instances`, `runtimes`, `residents`, `openedWith`, `prewarmed`) kept them apart and every change had to keep them in step; a hand connection's executor, hand and runtime are one `connections` entry, where `handIdentities` repeated them. tempoxyz/gateway's `AgentSession` subclass reads the private `driving`, `hands`, `handSockets` and `metadata` and wraps `attachHand`, `registerHand` and `recordHandRequests` by name (its `Internals` type in `src/Agents.ts`), and fails closed when any is missing, so they keep their names and shapes. Checked after the change with `npm run check:types` and `npm run build`, `output/durability/cf/run.mjs` (`SCENARIO=progress` through six restarts, also with `VERSIONS=change`, completed in 23.5 s; `loop` and `nap` failed by the tripwire after four restarts without progress, as before; `nap` with one restart answered `woke`), and both demos' drills on this tree packed into scratch copies: nanocode-cloudflare-durable's `charge` (2.5 s, one charge), `waiting` (7.8 s, one charge) and `deploys` (3.9 s) and nanocode-vercel-durable's eight runs (`charge` and `waiting` for Claude and Codex, durability `on` and `strict`) passed.

Deleting a Cloudflare session (`app.deleteSession(env, { account, name })`, the object's internal `POST /internal/delete`) frees its name for that account (2026-11-01; objects are named by `[account, name]`): once the object's storage is gone and the delete has answered, it resets itself with `ctx.abort('session deleted')` in a zero-delay timer, so the next request to that name instantiates a fresh object and opens a new empty session. Before, the object kept a `deleted` flag in memory and answered every request with 410 `session deleted` until Cloudflare evicted it, and a fresh object after that, so whether a name could be used again depended on eviction: gakonst/personal named every brief and writing-style run after its request ID because reusing a name seconds after deleting it answered 410 (2026-10-31). A request that arrives between the answer and the timer still gets the 410. Checked in local workerd (wrangler 4.151.1) with a minimal Durable Object doing the same delete: without the abort the request after the delete answered 410, with it the delete answered `{"deleted":true}` and the next request reached a new instance. The App itself was not run after the change: nanocode-cloudflare-durable's `measure-faults.mjs destroy` needs its UI bundle, which failed to build against a locally packed nanocode-ts (`Could not resolve "../dist/nanocode_worker.wasm"`). On Cloudflare the reset can reach the caller before the delete's answer does (the answer waits for the deleted storage to be confirmed while the zero-delay timer already ran): the stub's `fetch` then throws `session deleted`, so `deleteSession` answered by throwing and `POST /sessions/<s>/delete` with a 500, though the session was gone. Both now treat that error as a completed delete (`resetAfterDelete`): `deleteSession` answers true and the route `{ deleted: true }`. Seen on 2026-11-08 in gakonst/personal's production, where every `DELETE /v1/threads/:id` since 2026-11-01 answered 500 `session deleted` (local `wrangler dev` answered 204); on build-63a0a93 the two threads whose deletes had failed that way deleted with 204.

Subagent control (2026-10-30). The Cloudflare session API serves a root's subagent tree with the session's own authentication, descriptor and grant handling: `GET /sessions/<s>/subagents` answers `{ agents }` (`AgentRuntime.subagents.agents`), `GET /sessions/<s>/subagents/<id>` one entry, `POST .../<id>/messages` with `{ message, priority? }` (`urgent` steers a running child, `deferred`, the default, queues behind its turn) answers the `MessageReceipt` with 202, `POST .../<id>/interrupt` and `.../<id>/close` answer `{ agents }`, `POST .../<id>/side_question` with `{ question }` answers `{ text, usage }`, and `GET .../<id>/events?after&limit[&subtree=1]` pages, or with `Accept: text/event-stream` streams, the session log's events of that subagent, and with `subtree=1` of its descendants too, including ones spawned later. Subagent IDs are the SDK's `AgentId`s, positive integers per root, which the subagent journal persists with each child's descriptor and parent, so they and the tree survive a Durable Object restart. `GET .../subagents`, `GET .../subagents/<id>` and the membership check of `.../events` read the directory from a resident runtime, else from the `subagent_directories` row of the scope, and open a runtime only when neither exists. A route that opens a runtime uses the session's bound provider, model and executor, or the `background:` descriptor of the work in flight, so that the pump that follows reuses it. A session that never ran a turn has no subagents. A message also marks the scope's background work and starts the pump when no turn holds it, so a child that a message wakes keeps the object alive until `settle` reports quiescence, with the grant of the turn already running, else of the request. SDK errors map to 404 for an unknown agent and 409 for any other refusal. `App.create({ maxResidentSubagents })` passes `config.max_resident_subagents`. `ModelDispatch` and `SocketDispatch` carry the request's `origin` and `subagent`, and the app calls `codeMode.fetch(authority, env, subagent)` and `mcp.fetch(authority, env, subagent)` again for each call a subagent makes, so an egress or a paying fetch can bill and log each call by subagent and tree. Every subagent event in the log is `{ source: 'subagent', root_session_id, detach, update }`, and since SDK 2ac77424e an `event` update carries `id` and `parent` (null for a child of the root) beside the event, so `nanocode-ts/client`'s `Subagents.belongs(update, { members, subtree })` filters a per-subagent or per-tree feed from any page or stream, `Subagents.entries(updates)` rebuilds the directory from `added`, `status` and `completion` updates, `Subagents.tree(entries, { id })` names an entry's top-level ancestor, and `LiveEvent.fromLogged` maps subagent events to `{ type: 'subagent', update }`. A client `Transport` has an optional `subagents` control (`agents`, `sendUserMessage`, `interrupt`, `close`, `sideQuestion`), which the Cloudflare and Vercel transports implement. The Vercel app takes `POST { kind: 'subagent', sessionId, action, agent_id, message?, priority?, question? }`, where `action` is `send_user_message`, `interrupt`, `close` or `side_question`: it sends the command as a steer submission marked with `Harness.subagentText`, which the harness runs once in a `nanocode.subagent` effect, between turns or while one drives, and publishes as a `subagent_control` live event. The route waits for that effect and answers its outcome. `GET ?sessionId&subagents` answers `{ agents }`, the tree that `Subagents.entries` rebuilds from the committed log, since a route cannot open the runtime outside a drive. A stored command with another action, such as one written before the names below, fails with `unknown subagent action` instead of running. Measured on 2026-10-30 through tempoxyz/gateway's local Worker (`vp dev`, payments skipped by a transform outside both repositories, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash at low effort, Codex roots):
- Three children of one root, each told to run six 20 s cells, were listed with IDs 1 to 3 within 25 s of the turn. An urgent message to child 1 answered `steered` in 60 ms, and the child's `input.accepted` and `run.steered` followed its running cell's result at once, its next model call started 4 ms later, and it stopped and submitted the word the message asked for. A deferred message to child 2 answered `queued` and started a turn on it once its task ended. Interrupting and then closing child 3 answered `interrupted` and `closed`, and a side question to child 1 answered in 747 ms.
- Restarting the whole Worker (every Durable Object reset) 90 s into the run left IDs 1 to 3, their parents and statuses in the listing. Child 2 was restored and resumed 14 s after the restart with its running cell intact, finished its six steps, and the deferred message, re-admitted from the journal, then started its next turn.
- With the resident limit at 2, four children ran at once (their first model calls started within 7 ms of each other and all four finished within 2 s). With the limit at 0 every idle child was evicted, and urgent messages to two of them started turns on their restored snapshots, which answered from their earlier task, as did a side question.
- A root whose child spawned a grandchild listed the grandchild with `parent_agent_id` 1. That child's feed held 50 events and its `subtree=1` feed 92, and `Subagents.entries` over the log matched `GET /subagents` for every session above (`output/subagents-entries.ts`).
- The three-child session's log held 481 events in 310,817 bytes of JSON, 143 of them children's delta events (66.7 KB), which the log persists as it does the root's reasoning deltas. `parent` adds about 14 bytes to each child event.
- Every model request of these runs reached the gateway's egress with its `subagent` and `tree` (null for the root's own, the grandchild's tree its parent's ID), and a child's `tools.mcp__gateway__kv_set` reached the gateway's MCP server with its own. Before the MCP request read its session from `_meta`, it arrived with none.

Idle session reads and waiting turns (2026-11-07, after agent 29's Cloudflare audit: `AgentSession` used about 90% of the gateway's Durable Object CPU, and one nanocode-cloudflare-durable object fired its 5 s recovery alarm about 720 times an hour for more than 30 hours).

- The Cloudflare adapter stores the subagent directory (`AgentRuntime.subagents.agents()`) in `subagent_directories` (scope, JSON body) when the pump settles a scope with no queued turn and no `background:` work, and when a `GET` opens a runtime for a quiet session, and writes the row only when its body changed (`ON CONFLICT ... DO UPDATE ... WHERE body IS NOT excluded.body`, which writes no row in workerd when the body is the same). A `GET` serves the stored row only while no `background:` marker stands: the pump sets the marker before every `drive` and deletes it only once the scope settles, so a drive in progress, or one a reset cut short, never answers from the row. Every `POST` to `.../subagents/<id>/...` and `/side_question` deletes the row before it acts, since those change the directory without a drive. A per-object epoch, bumped before every drive, keeps a listing that a drive overtook from being stored. The directory changes only while a runtime drives, so the stored row matches what a runtime answers. Until 2026-11-07 the pump deleted the row before every drive and stored it again after, two rows written a turn.
- A turn that waits for its host retries after `RECOVERY_DELAY_MS` × 2^(attempts − 1), at most `RECOVERY_BACKOFF_MS`, which a host sets as policy. Without `RECOVERY_BACKOFF_MS` every retry waits `RECOVERY_DELAY_MS`, as before. A turn that waits for a hand (`kind: detached`) stops the timer once one retry waited the whole bound, also while the scope holds a `background:` marker, since background work waits behind the queued turn. The turn stays queued with its `error`, `kind`, `attempts` and the `retry_at` of its last retry, metrics answer `alarm_at: null`, and the hand's registration starts the pump. Background work whose runtime cannot open counts its attempts in the `retries:<scope>` metadata key and backs off the same way up to the bound, where it keeps retrying because nothing else starts it; an opened runtime deletes the key. build-135a3be still armed the alarm for a parked turn when a `background:` marker existed: the deployed nanocode-cloudflare-durable object `d520863a…` kept its 5 s cadence after the deploy, and a local run of `measure-background.mjs`'s held child plus a turn on an absent hand reproduced it (one alarm a second on build-135a3be with `RECOVERY_DELAY_MS` 1000, none after the turn parked on this change).
- A turn that waits for a retry after its runtime failed to open (any `kind` but `detached`) also retries before its `retry_at` in two cases (2026-11-09). A `POST /sessions/<s>/warm` whose runtime opens starts the pump at once. An object that restarts, as after a deploy, moves the alarm to `RECOVERY_DELAY_MS` after its construction, once per failed attempt: `unavailable` writes the attempt's `retry_at` to the `retry_waiting` metadata key, and the constructor, the warm and an opened runtime delete it. Before, only a new turn started such a turn early, so after a cause was fixed a turn that had backed off waited for its stored `retry_at`, up to `RECOVERY_BACKOFF_MS` (1 h on gakonst/personal). On gakonst/personal's production on 2026-11-09 a wallet change (a68090e) failed every session's runtime open for 4 h 20 min until 9254e52 was deployed. Checked with `output/retry-check/run.mjs` (Miniflare through nanocode-cloudflare-durable's local shim, an `mcp.servers` that throws while the case is broken, `RECOVERY_DELAY_MS` 1000, `RECOVERY_BACKOFF_MS` 3600000, five failed attempts, so the next retry was 15.8 s away). Once the cause was fixed, a warm completed the turn in 15,839 ms on build-28eeea8 and in 100 ms on this tree, and a restart completed it in 16,202 ms and 1,559 ms.
- The session log orders `log_turns` tails by `(scope, tail_first)` (`log_turns_order`, under SDK 2daf84a93's `session_log_order` format), an entry a turn's insert writes once and no tail append or turn update changes. Tails are disjoint and ascend by seq, so the writer's holder is the turn with the greatest `tail_first`, and a page after a cursor starts at the tail holding it (one bound, `tail_first >= max(from + 1, the greatest tail_first <= cursor)`, since SQLite seeks on one lower bound of a column and scanned every turn with two). SDK 3b8119b7f had indexed `(scope, tail_last)` and `(scope, status)`, which every tail append and every turn update rewrote: in workerd an UPDATE that names an indexed column in its SET writes the index entry even when the value is the same, and the append sets `tail_last` and `status`. `session_log_order` drops both indexes, and `session_log_indexes` keeps its version 1 with no statements, so build-135a3be to build-f1f32af open a newer store without recreating them or refusing it. The pending list scans the scope's turns again, about 2N rows a turn for a scope of N turns, which costs less than the status index's three row writes a turn until N is about 1,500 (Durable Object SQLite bills a row written 1,000 times a row read).
- Measured with nanocode-cloudflare-durable's local workerd shim (each statement's `rowsRead` and `rowsWritten`) on a session of 300 one-message turns of 8 events, with the demo bundled against build-f1f32af and against this tree (a scratch `measure-writes.mjs` beside its `measure-background.mjs`): the fill wrote 8,550 rows (28.5 a turn) on build-f1f32af and 4,052 (13.5) on this tree, against 3,768 before the indexes (build-38dd331's module). On build-f1f32af the tail appends' 1,200 statements wrote 3,600 rows, the turn updates' 600 wrote 1,800, the 300 inserts 900 and the directory 599; on this tree 1,200, 600, 600 and 1. The fill read 11,503 rows on build-f1f32af and 54,853 here, 45,750 of them the pending scans. On both an idle `GET /events` after the last event read 4 rows (303 before the indexes), an idle `GET /subagents` read none, and a restarted object's first `GET /events` read 307 and 308 rows (the writer's `SEQ_HIGH` still reads every turn once) and its first `GET /subagents` 1 row with no runtime, where before the stored directory it opened a runtime in 105 to 122 ms and read 404. A store run on build-f1f32af (20 turns), then this tree (20), then build-f1f32af (5) and this tree (5) kept every event in order, paged alike at limits 128 and 7, and held only `log_turns_order` after the first move. `bench/hands.ts --hand=local --cells=noop,callback-seq-50 --n=5` ran the same transactions and statements a turn on either module (19 and 119), and `output/durability/restart.mjs` (`sleep`, `yield`, `compute`, `plain`), `mcp-resume.mjs` (`resume`, `plain`, `lose`, `unknown`) and `cf/run.mjs` (`progress` through six restarts in 23.8 s, `nap` with one restart) answered as recorded below. The demo's `measure-background.mjs`, with checks added that a quiet session stores `[]`, that a restarted object with the marker standing lists the held child while the stored row stays `[]`, and that a restarted quiet object lists the stored directory with no runtime, passed 43 checks; on build-f1f32af it fails the second, because that build deletes the row before the drive. Both demos' drills passed on this tree packed into scratch copies: nanocode-cloudflare-durable's `charge` (2.3 s, one charge), `waiting` (7.7 s, one charge) and `deploys` (4.1 s), and nanocode-vercel-durable's eight runs (one charge each). tempoxyz/gateway deployed build-d3cc7ae as f56633b on 2026-11-07: a Codex agent's 30 one-line turns wrote 1,092 rows on build-f1f32af and 593 after it (Cloudflare GraphQL `durableObjectsPeriodicGroups` for the object), its idle polls read 4 to 7 rows a request on both, and a Codex and a Claude turn with MCP tools and a subagent completed; nanocode-cloudflare-durable (version c93d9163) and nanocode-vercel-durable answered a public turn on it.
- A turn on an absent hand with `RECOVERY_DELAY_MS` 1000 and `RECOVERY_BACKOFF_MS` 8000 fired 4 alarms and then answered `alarm_at: null`, where without a bound it fired one alarm a second. A `nanocode hand` attached afterwards started it in 517 ms. nanocode-cloudflare-durable's `measure-background.mjs` passed, with added checks that a restarted object lists the stored directory unchanged, answers one entry and pages a child's events without opening a runtime.

The subagent control names and fields are upstream nanocode's (Georgios, 2026-10-31: no custom naming for agent tools and their control APIs). Each runtime and client method, Vercel action and WASM command takes the name of the `SubagentControl` method it calls (`crates/runtime/subagents/src/runtime/focus.rs` at SDK 2ac77424e), as does the `side_question` route, the types take the SDK's names (`AgentStatus`, `AgentSummary`, `AgentDirectoryEntry`, `MessagePriority`, `MessageReceipt`, `SideAnswer`), and the bodies take the fields of the SDK's tools: the listing is `{ agents }` as `list_agents` answers, and a message is `{ message, priority }` as `send_agent_message` takes it. `send_agent_message`'s `purpose` and `in_reply_to` are not taken, because `SubagentControl::send_user_message` sends every user message as `coordinate` with no thread. Like the SDK's tools, which deny unknown fields, the Cloudflare routes refuse any other field with 400. Before, the listing was `{ subagents }`, a message took `prompt`, the side question route was `question`, and the client control was `list`, `message`, `interrupt`, `close` and `ask`. Checked on 2026-10-31 through tempoxyz/gateway's local Worker (`vp dev`, this tree built with `scripts/build.mjs` and installed with `npm pack`, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash at low effort, paid from a key of a test wallet):
- `GET /subagents` answered `{ agents }` with `can_message` and `can_manage` on each entry.
- An urgent `{ message, priority }` to a child in a 40 s cell answered `steered` in 19 ms, and the child completed with the note the message asked for 12.1 s later. A deferred one answered `queued` for a running child and `started` for an idle one.
- `interrupt` and `close` answered `{ agents }` with `interrupted` and `closed`, and `side_question` answered `{ text, usage }` in 3.9 s for a Codex child.
- A Claude root's side question to its completed child answered 409 with the SDK's `Unknown Claude model: configure max_tokens explicitly` until SDK c779f2ba5 (2026-11-08). Rehydrating a child from its journal builds its checkpoint with no `max_tokens` (`session_checkpoint` in the Claude backend), and `ClaudeBuilder::resume` replaced the builder's configured `max_tokens` with that `None`, so every Claude child on a model outside the SDK's table failed to restore: its side questions, and its resume after a runtime restart, which Swarms swarm f3a42cf87339's Opus 5.5 subagent hit on production that night (`subagent 4 could not resume after a runtime restart: invalid task request: Unknown Claude model: configure max_tokens explicitly`). `resume` now keeps the builder's `max_tokens` and auto-compact window when the checkpoint records none. A native harness against the SDK built a Claude agent on `vercel/anthropic/claude-opus-5.5` with `max_tokens`, turned its native checkpoint's snapshot into a journal checkpoint with `session_checkpoint` and resumed it on a builder with the same `max_tokens`: `Unknown Claude model` before, restored after.

Root side questions (2026-11-05, asked for by Georgios for Swarms: "/btw support … as we do in the CLI"). The Cloudflare session API answers `POST /sessions/<s>/side_question` with `{ question }` (no other field) as `{ text, usage }`, from the root's own conversation, without tools and without interrupting or steering a running turn: the CLI's one-off side question, the SDK's `Agent::side_question`. `AgentRuntime.sideQuestion({ question })` sends the `side_question` WASM command without `agent_id`, which the command now takes as optional and, when absent, answers on `session.inbox.agent()` instead of `SubagentControl::side_question`. A session that has no conversation yet answers 409, a question that is empty or a body with another field 400, and a read-only descriptor 403. The CLI's forked side conversation (`/btw` on the main thread forks the session and gives the side thread tools) has no session API; a host that wants a thread sends the earlier questions and answers in the question, as the CLI's one-off side pane does (`EARLIER` in `bin/nanocode/src/side.rs`).
- Checked on 2026-11-05 with the gateway (tempoxyz/gateway `feat/agent-side-questions`) on a local pack of this change under `vp dev` with the AI binding, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash on Codex: 9 s into a turn that wrote a haiku about lighthouses and then waited 30 s in a cell, a side question answered "The subject is a lighthouse — …" in 1.7 s with 13,312 input tokens cached, the turn stayed running and completed with its haiku, and a side question after it answered the poem's path. `cargo` built the module against SDK 97b32dc in 49 s (`output/build-side-question.log`), and `npm run check:types` passed.

Subagent budgets (2026-11-05, asked for by Georgios for Swarms: "each subagent can also have a budget … so that we can fund sub-populations of a given swarm"), on SDK 6727a5f1e. `App.create({ subagentBudgets: true })` sends `subagent_budgets` in the agent configuration, which turns on the spawn policy's `budgets`, so `spawn_agent` takes `budget_usd` (the SDK refuses one above the spawning agent's lineage's). Every request context's `subagent` (model dispatch, MCP and cell fetch, host tool calls) now also carries `lineage`, the agent IDs from the agent up to its top-level ancestor, and `budgets`, `{ id, usd }` for each budgeted agent of that lineage, the agent's own first, for a host that meters what a subtree spends and refuses its requests past a budget. `POST /sessions/<s>/subagents/<n>/budget` with `{ budget_usd }` (a positive number, or `null` to clear it; no other field) sets one through `SubagentControl::set_budget` (the `set_budget` WASM command, `AgentRuntime.subagents.setBudget`) and answers `{ agent }`; an unknown subagent is 404. Directory entries and summaries carry `budget_usd`.

Subagent models (2026-11-07, asked for by Georgios so a Swarms creator picks the models its subagents may use), on SDK a706a3650. `App.create`'s `limits` callback now also gets the session's `authority` (`account_id`, `session_id`, `executor_id`, `session_name`), and may answer `subagent_models`, a list of `{ harness, model }`, which the agent configuration carries to `src/wasm.rs`: each entry becomes a `ModelSelection` the way the session's own model does (`Provider::select`: a Claude ID the SDK knows or any other name as a custom Claude model, a Codex ID through `Model`'s parse) and the list becomes the spawn policy's `models`. The SDK then lists exactly those names and harnesses in `spawn_agent`'s `model` and `harness` enums and refuses any other child model, inherited or family-default ones included, with `model <id> (<harness>) is not offered to subagents; offered: <id> (<harness>), …`; an empty list refuses every spawn. A host that wants one policy per agent reads it from its own storage by `authority` (tempoxyz/gateway reads its agent row). The callback runs when a runtime opens, so a changed policy reaches a resident runtime only when it next opens. `npm run check:types` and `cargo check --locked --lib --target wasm32-unknown-unknown` (against `../nanocodex` at a706a3650) passed.
- The subagent session API's runtime is no longer retired by the request that opened it: `subagentRuntime` called `releaseIdle(true)` after opening a runtime for an idle session, which retires every resident runtime over `RUNTIME_MEMORY_BUDGET_BYTES` (64 MiB at the gateway), the one it was about to return included, so a message that started an idle subagent's turn had that turn cancelled within milliseconds and the subagent recovered as after a restart, with the restart notice, which led DeepSeek V4.1 Flash to run its finished task again. `releaseIdle` now keeps the runtime it is given; it is released at the end of the next turn.
- Checked on 2026-11-05 with tempoxyz/gateway (`feat/subagent-budgets`) on a local pack of this change under `vp dev` with the AI binding, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash on Codex, paid from a throwaway wallet's OUSD key: a child spawned with `budget_usd` 0.01 ran paid kv writes until the gateway refused it at $0.0105, `budget` with 0.03 answered the entry with `budget_usd` 0.03, a message then started it and it completed at $0.0156 without the restart notice (before the `releaseIdle` change, the same message's turn was cancelled 3 ms in and the child ran its loop again up to $0.0301), `null` cleared it, 99 answered 404, and a child given 0.02 had its own spawn with 0.05 refused while its child given 0.005 stopped at $0.0054. `npm run build` built against SDK 6727a5f1e and `npm run check:types` passed.
- Hand requests carry their subagent (2026-11-05, for tempoxyz/gateway's priced hands, whose `hand.use` charges counted against no subagent budget). The SDK's `RemoteContext` names only the child session (`session_id`), and only the WebAssembly maps a session to its lineage and budgets. `remote_send` now sends each dispatching frame's `subagent` in its `origins` entry, looked up from the frame context's `session_id` as model dispatches do, and `null` for the lead. The Cloudflare app writes it into each such request frame for a hand connection as a top-level `"subagent"` field, appended before the frame's last `}`, so a hub can bill the subagent. The SDK's request decoder (`Request` in `crates/runtime/code-mode/src/remote/frame.rs` at 6727a5f1e) does not deny unknown fields, so hands ignore it. The field adds about 100 bytes to a request frame and is not counted against the hand's `max_frame_bytes`.
- Checked on 2026-11-05 with `output/hand-subagent.ts` (not committed), on `bench/handbench.ts`'s scripted Claude model in Node 22.23 and a session opened with `subagent_budgets`. The lead's cell called `spawn_agent` with `budget_usd` 0.25 on a hand, and the child's cell ran `Bash` with `hand: 'bench'`. The lead's two `Execute` frames had `subagent: null`. The child's had `{ id: 1, tree: 1, lineage: [1], parent: null, budgets: [{ id: 1, usd: 0.25 }] }`, which the script appended as App.ts does. A `bench/hand-ts.ts` hand and `nanocode hand` 0.6.7 each ran the frame and replied `child-ran`, and both turns completed. `npm run build`, `npm run check:types`, `cargo fmt --check` on the changed file and `cargo clippy --lib --target wasm32-unknown-unknown --profile wasm -- -D warnings` passed.

Code Mode cells in a Cloudflare session stop sooner (2026-11-05). `App.create({ cellInterruptChecks })` (default 2,000; the SDK's own default is 10,000) sends `cell_interrupt_checks`, which the WASM module passes to `set_interrupt_checks`, so one synchronous run of a cell stops after that many QuickJS interrupt checks with the SDK's `Script stopped: one synchronous run exceeded this host's CPU budget for a cell`. A recovered cell replays with that budget times its inputs plus one, so at 10,000 the gateway's Swarms agent agt_5e32d62b…, whose cell ran a 1,600-fit bootstrap in QuickJS, was replayed on every recovery with 30,000 checks: `wrangler tail` showed one recovery using 106 s of CPU and the session reset with `Durable Object exceeded its CPU time limit` and then, with the gateway's limit raised to five minutes, `Durable Object storage operation exceeded timeout`, so every request to that session answered 500 after 13 to 131 s and its swarm never saw the turn end. Measured natively with rquickjs 0.14 on an M-series Mac, one interrupt check took 0.11 ms in a bare loop, 0.38 ms in a loop calling Math functions and 0.72 ms in the swarm's grid fit, so 2,000 checks are about 0.2 to 1.5 s natively and a few seconds in workerd, and a replay of a cell with ten inputs stays well under the 30 s that Durable Objects allow by default.

A session stops recovering a turn that keeps resetting it (2026-11-05). The pump counts, in session metadata (`resets:<request id>`, made durable with `storage.sync()` before the runtime opens), each time it starts driving a turn, and clears the count when that pump ends, normally or with an exception; a pump the Durable Object reset never clears it. The count is kept with a progress marker (`resets:<request id>` holds `<count> <marker>`): the sum of `state_rows.revision` over the object, which only a commit raises (a takeover raises `fence`; four restarts of a turn stuck in a cell left the revision at 3), and the row count and `total(seq)` of `nanocode_cell_journal_batches`, which a cell's nested calls change. A pump that finds another marker than the stored one counts from zero, so only resets without progress between them count. On the fifth start without progress the turn finishes `failed` (`kind: recovery`, "The session was reset while driving this turn four times in a row without the turn making progress, so it stopped recovering it.") instead of being driven again. Until 2026-11-06 every reset counted, so the resets each gateway deploy causes failed healthy long turns: a 25-minute Swarms turn was failed after four deploys. `output/durability/cf/run.mjs` (Miniflare, the instance disposed and recreated every 2.5 s): a turn of 14 model calls of 1.2 s each, restarted 7 times, failed after the fourth restart on 4e35cf7 and completed (22 model calls) on this build; a turn whose cell sleeps 120 s (`SCENARIO=loop`) and a turn whose model call never answers (`SCENARIO=hang`) still finish `failed` after the fifth start. Progress from a long cell's nested calls alone was not exercised (the harness has no host tool). A host may also pass `App.create`'s `version(env)`, its deployment version (tempoxyz/gateway passes `CF_VERSION_METADATA.id`); the counter keeps it too (`<count> <marker> <version>`) and counts from zero when it changed, so a deploy's reset never counts, also during a turn that makes no journal progress for a long time, such as one cell running a 25-minute `compute_exec` while deploys land every few minutes (Swarms swarm f7df8db3fe54's round 6 failed after five gateway deploys in 25 minutes on 2026-11-07). Through `run.mjs` with `SCENARIO=stuck` (a cell sleeping 20 s) restarted every 2.5 s six times: with the same `DEPLOY_VERSION` the turn finished `failed` after the fifth start, and with a new one at every restart it completed with `woke`. Before, a turn whose recovery always exceeded the object's limits was retried on every alarm forever: the gateway's Swarms agent agt_5e32d62b… answered every request with 500 after 13 to 135 s for over two hours, resetting with `exceeded its CPU time limit` and then `storage operation exceeded timeout`, and the lower cell budget above did not end it.

The tripwire's progress marker no longer counts what recovering a turn commits (2026-11-08). The pump also records, at the first model dispatch or socket open after it started driving (`dispatched`), the progress the session had reached by then, in the counter (`<count> <marker> <version> dispatched`), and a pump that finds that marker unchanged, or a counter whose pump never dispatched, keeps counting; commits made between a pump's start and its first dispatch are the recovery's own, such as the parent of a finished subagent being handed its completion again, which raised `state_rows.revision` by 2 at every restart, so every pump saw progress and the turn was driven forever. Background work (a subagent completion or a message with no host turn pending) is counted the same way under `resets:background:<scope>`, and after four resets without progress its `background:` marker is dropped, so the session stops pumping it until the next turn. tempoxyz/gateway's Swarms lead `agt_538a876087cb9dab0c0badffce665505` reopened every 35 to 50 s for over three hours on 2026-11-08 (each reopen logged subagent 12's `added` and `completion` updates and the completion turn's `input.accepted` and `run.started`, then the object was reset with `Durable Object storage operation exceeded timeout`); the gateway's own hang is fixed there. Reproduced with `/tmp/night/a68/repro/run.mjs` (Miniflare with nanocode-cloudflare-durable's `local-runtime.mjs`: a root turn spawns a subagent and ends, the subagent submits its result, a second turn is queued, every model call answering the parent's completion hangs, and the instance is disposed and recreated every 2.5 s): on build-c9c940b the counter stayed at 1 while the revision rose 20, 22, 24 … 38 over ten restarts and the second turn was still `running` with 13 hung completion calls; on this tree it read 2, 3, 4 `dispatched` and the second turn finished `failed` after the fifth start (15.7 s), the background completion was pumped four more times and then dropped, the session made no model call in the next 6 s and answered an events page in 7 ms, and once the provider stopped hanging a third turn completed with the completion delivered first. `output/durability/cf/run.mjs` on this tree: `progress` completed after 12 rounds through six restarts (23.7 s), `hang` and `loop` failed after the fifth start, and `stuck` completed with `VERSIONS=change` and failed without it.

A subagent's events (`GET /sessions/<s>/subagents/<id>/events`) are read from the root log in pages of `EVENT_PAGE_SIZE` whatever the request's `limit`, return at most `limit` events (`next_cursor` the last one's seq when cut), and stop after 32 pages (`subagentScanPages`, 4,096 events at the gateway's 128, chosen without measurement) with `has_more` and the cursor reached. Before, a read paged the log with the request's own `limit`, without bound, until it met an event of that subagent: the gateway asked `?after=0&limit=1` for each of a session's subagents to map a completion to its subagent, which read a long log one event at a time and held the object for minutes. `AgentSession.completionOrigin(turn)` answers the host turn a `subagent-completion:` turn's child runs under (the SDK's `completion_origin`) from the resident runtime only, `undefined` when none is resident, so a host can attribute a completion without reading the log and without waiting on a runtime that is opening. In the repro's Miniflare a subclass asking it while the parent's completion turn ran answered `t1`, the turn that spawned the subagent, and `null` for a turn that is not a completion. On production on tempoxyz/gateway 59b1153 (build-019c5fa), `GET /v1/agents/agt_538a…/subagents/12/events?after=0&limit=1` on that 12-subagent session answered in 426 ms with no events, `has_more` and a cursor 32 pages on.

`Forward.create({ fetch })` (`src/cloudflare/Forward.ts`, 2026-10-28) is the egress for a host that sends model traffic through a proxy of its own: it answers the private binding's `/model` with a `Request` to the dispatch's `endpoint` carrying its headers and body and `/socket` with a WebSocket upgrade to the socket dispatch's `endpoint`, hands each to `fetch(request, dispatch)` and returns its response (for a socket, a 101 with its `webSocket`); `/warm` answers 204, `/metrics` ok and `/remote` 501. The host decides where each request goes and adds what it needs, so a credential-injecting proxy keeps every secret. `SocketDispatch` carries the Codex Responses `endpoint` the socket opens and `subscription`, as `ModelDispatch` does; before, an egress had to hard-code `chatgpt.com` for plan sockets. On 2026-10-28 a Worker on `Forward` with `subscription: () => true` sent every request through an `mtls_certificates` binding to paradigmxyz/iron-proxy's reverse listener (paradigmxyz/iron-proxy#294) on a GCP VM, which injected a ChatGPT plan's access token from a file that iron-token-broker kept refreshed, the plan's account ID and a Claude plan's `claude setup-token` token. With no credential in the Worker, Claude Haiku 5.5 over HTTPS and GPT-6 Luna over the Codex WebSocket each ran a turn with a Code Mode cell (4 s and 6 s). gakonst/nanocode-iron holds that Worker and the VM's configuration.

Visitors can run their sessions on their own ChatGPT or Claude plan (2026-10-28), through nanocodexyz/egress (`nanocode-egress`, a dependency installed from its `build-<sha>` release and bundled into this package's tarball, `bundleDependencies`), so a deployment writes no plan code of its own. Bundling is so hosts install the release with nothing else (2026-11-01): pnpm 12 refuses a tarball URL below a direct dependency (`ERR_PNPM_EXOTIC_SUBDEP`), so tempoxyz/gateway removed this package's `nanocode-egress` with an override and listed the same tarball itself, moving both in step. The packed tarball holds `node_modules/nanocode-egress` (43 files, 228 KB), which `nanocode-ts`'s own files resolve to, and, since 2026-11-07, `undici` (210 files) and `@cloudflare/containers` (17 files), nanocode-egress's optional peers that this package also depends on: npm takes a peer of a bundled package that it places under this package as part of the bundle (`inBundle` in the host's lockfile) although the tarball did not hold it, so whenever a host's own copy differed from this package's pin, `npm install` and `npm ci` left `node_modules/nanocode-ts/node_modules/undici` empty or missing and `src/vercel/internal/Egress.ts` ran on the host's undici. Checked on 2026-11-07 with copies of nanocodexyz/durable-vercel's `package.json` and `package-lock.json` (its root undici is 7.30.0): with build-b325614, `npm ci --ignore-scripts` installed only `nanocode-egress` there and `undici` resolved from `dist/vercel/internal` to the root's 7.30.0; with a tarball packed from this change (43,049,566 bytes against the release's 43,125,651), `npm install` of it and then `npm ci` from the lockfile it wrote each installed all three, `undici` resolved to the bundled 7.30.1 and `dist/vercel/internal/Egress.js` loaded; a copy of the gateway's `package.json` and `pnpm-workspace.yaml` without the override or the direct dependency installed under pnpm 12.15.0 and resolved `nanocode-egress/cloudflare` from `dist/cloudflare/Plans.js` to the bundled copy. A host's relay container image is now `./node_modules/nanocode-ts/node_modules/nanocode-egress/relay.Dockerfile` under npm and pnpm alike (npm no longer hoists it to `./node_modules/nanocode-egress`). On Cloudflare, `App.create({ plans: { vault, relay, account } })` (`src/cloudflare/Plans.ts`) wraps `egress` in the egress's plan binding: a signed-in account's model requests and Codex sockets carry its plan's credential, injected in the Worker, and ChatGPT ones leave through the `RelayContainer`, because chatgpt.com answers requests from Workers with 403. It also sets `subscription` to whether the account's plan is signed in, unless the host passes its own, and serves `/plans` for `plans.account`: `GET` for both plans' status, `POST /plans/{claude|codex}/sign-in`, `POST /plans/claude/complete` with `{ code }` and `DELETE /plans/{claude|codex}`. `plans.account` defaults to `authenticate`'s account unless that principal is read-only; it must return `null` for an account several people share, such as a demo's `public` account, so that one person's plan never serves another.
