The Worker exports `PlanVault` and `RelayContainer` from `nanocode-ts/cloudflare`, sets only `PLAN_SECRET` (at least 32 characters; the relay's secret is derived from it), and its `wrangler.jsonc` lists `{ "class_name": "RelayContainer", "image": "./node_modules/nanocode-egress/relay.Dockerfile" }` under `containers`, `PLANS` (`PlanVault`) and `RELAY` (`RelayContainer`) under `durable_objects` and both classes in a `new_sqlite_classes` migration; `wrangler deploy` then builds the relay image with Docker. Without `relay`, ChatGPT-plan requests fail with a message naming this setup, and Claude plans need no container. On Vercel, `App.create({ account, plans: {} })` (`src/vercel/Plans.ts`) sets `subscription` the same way over Upstash Redis (`KV_REST_API_URL` and `KV_REST_API_TOKEN`, as the Marketplace database sets them) sealed with `PLAN_SECRET`, both read on the first request that needs them, and returns `plans`, the `GET`, `POST` and `DELETE` handlers of `app/api/plans/[[...path]]/route.ts` (prefix `/api/plans`); chatgpt.com serves Vercel Functions directly. In the browser, `Plans.create({ url, headers })` from `nanocode-ts/client` is the routes' client, `usePlans(client)` from `nanocode-ts/react` loads both plans and polls a pending ChatGPT sign-in, and `PlanConnect` renders one row per plan, ChatGPT's code and link while it waits for approval, and Claude's link and a field for the code Claude's page shows, since Claude's OAuth client redirects only to its own page; its parts (`plan`, `planTitle`, `planText`, `planCode`, `planActions`, `planButton`, `planInput`, `planStatus`) take the hand panel's styles in the default skin. On 2026-10-28 nanocode-egress's examples ran real nanocode agents on both plans on both platforms; whether a public deployment may sign visitors in with their consumer plans is up to OpenAI's and Anthropic's terms.

`createNanocode(options)` from `nanocode-ts/vercel` (`src/vercel/App.ts`) builds the durable-harness agent around `nanocodeHarness` with Code Mode, subagents and streamed tool input on by default, and returns `{ agent, GET, POST, chaos }`, so a Next route is `export const { GET, POST } = nanocode`: `POST` starts a session for `{ harness, model, effort, text }` (validated against `options.harnesses` by `selectionOf`), warms one for `{ warm: true }`, and forwards `sessionId` messages and steers to the chat route; `authorize` is a function that throws a `Response` to refuse, or `"public"`. `directDriveMs` and `deadlineMarginMs` pass through to `durableAgent`: durable-harness drives a turn in the chat route that received it for at most `directDriveMs` (60 s by default) and yields `deadlineMarginMs` (15 s) before that, and a Code Mode cell lives in its drive, so at the yield a running cell was cut and came back to the model as interrupted by a host crash; on nanocode.xyz/vercel on 2026-10-24 every cell that ran 45 s or more (a 70 s `python3` loop on both harnesses) failed that way, and the next drive answered `wait` on it with `exec cell 1 not found`. A host sets it just under its chat route's `maxDuration`; the route's queue message, delayed past the deadline, still recovers a drive that died, and a reconnecting reader takes the session over sooner. The live stream carries a cell's first `STREAMED_WASM_CALLS` (20) `wasm` calls (`op: "call"`, one per call of an export through the cells' `WebAssembly` shim) and then one `tool_output` line saying how many more it left out, since a cell that timed 100,000 calls of an export on 2026-10-24 sent each as a `tool_start` and `tool_end` and the session's stream fell 55 to 90 s behind. The SDK logs at most 100 nested calls of each tool per cell since fa19016c4bc1411e03b747c380e23c1a1f763abb and then one `tool.notification` with the count it left out: through `nanocodeHarness` on world-local on 2026-10-24, a cell calling an export 100,000 times had logged 2 events per call and never committed its first SQL batch (the drive was handed over and the turn restarted 23 times in 15 minutes), and with the cap it logged 216 events in 81 SQL transactions (200 KB) and finished in 4.9 s. `vercel({ selection, headers, session, url, chaosUrl })` from `nanocode-ts/vercel/transport` is its browser `Transport`. Its `cancel` (the chats' Stop button) posts `{ kind: "cancel", sessionId }`; `POST` sends that as a `steer` submission whose text is `CANCEL` (durable-harness's chat route takes only `prompt`, `steer` and `followUp`, and withdraws only submissions not placed yet), and the drive places it with a `nanocode.cancel` step after `AgentRuntime.cancel` on the turn it is driving (or the first pending one), so the turn ends `cancelled` and its cells stop. On nanocode.xyz/vercel on 2026-10-24 the chats had no Stop button and an endless cell could only be steered; with this, a local `next dev` turn running a 90 s cell ended `cancelled` 9 ms after the cancel POST and the next turn ran normally. `send` saves the warmed (or newly begun) session ID before posting the prompt and calls the client's `TransportParameters.changed`, which persists the transport snapshot, and a reconnect without a cursor starts from `FIRST_CURSOR` (`0.0`, the session's first live event), while `GET` answers that cursor with an immediate `end` for a session with no submissions: before, the session ID and cursor reached storage only with the prompt POST's first event, so reloading while a session's first turn still waited for its response headers (several seconds on a cold Function) left the chat idle with the prompt and no answer, as on nanocode.xyz/vercel/chatgpt on 2026-10-24.

A native `nanocode hand` dials a Vercel Function directly when the host passes `hands: { upgrade: upgradeWebSocket }` (from `@vercel/functions` 3.10 or later, which needs the `ws` package): `createNanocode`'s `GET` hands a request with `Upgrade: websocket` to `handRoute`'s `SOCKET`, which admits `?sessionId=` with the session's hand channel as the `Authorization: Bearer` token (`nanocode hand wss://<host>/api/chat?sessionId=<id> --token <channel>`, `NANOCODE_RUNNER_TOKEN` for the token), answers 403 otherwise, which stops the hand for good, and upgrades with `maxPayload` set to `hands.maxFrameBytes` (1 MiB by default; `upgradeWebSocket`'s own default is 256 KiB). `POST { warm: true, hand: true }` starts a session with a new random channel and answers `{ sessionId, hand }`. The Function that holds the socket registers it per session in its process (`globalThis[Symbol.for("nanocode-ts.vercel.hands")]`, so two copies of the module share it), appends the catalog and the connection's end to the attach stream and relays the World's down stream to the socket and the socket to the up stream, so `dialHand` still finds the hand from another instance; a drive in the same process (the chat route's direct drive usually is) takes the socket itself, gets the catalog first and sends and receives frames with no World write, and a second brain or a reconnect of the hand closes the earlier brain's socket. Writes to the hand streams coalesce: while one line is being written the next frames wait and go out as one line. On a preview in `tempoxyz` on 2026-10-27 (iad1, a hand in San Francisco 79 ms away, mock model), every drive found the socket in its own process, and the exec call, one World commit for the brain's durability before the Execute plus the round trip, took a median 196-309 ms with SDK 3061155a6's cached tool definitions against 379-412 ms before; forcing the World relay instead took 325-364 ms, and before the hand stopped sending a `DetachedComplete` ahead of every reply and the writes coalesced, one forced run waited out the 30 s operation deadline on three calls in a row. A WebSocket closes at the route's `maxDuration`, and the hand redials. Brains on these sockets resume across that, because `dialHand(world, runId, signal, { resumes: true })` keeps its socket open while the hand is away and the harness's `RemoteExecutor` policy says `resumes`, so the SDK client marks its requests resumable and the hand keeps their work and replies: a local brain parks (its frames wait in memory), takes the hand's next socket on the same instance with its new catalog and writes the waiting frames, after which the SDK sends `Resume` and the hand sends the replies it holds; a brain on the World streams moves to the new connection's streams when the attach stream names it. A hand that comes back as another runtime fails the client with the SDK's `HAND_RESTARTED`, and a parked local brain whose hand attached on another instance closes at once, as before. Locally (`createNanocode` on world-local with a `ws` upgrade and a 3 s cell, `nanocode-vercel-durable`'s bench script), cutting the hand's socket 1 s into the cell left the cell's output arriving at 3.1-3.2 s on both paths, where the turn used to fail at once, and a restarted hand failed it within 30 ms; on a preview on 2026-10-27, cutting the hand's connection 2.5 s into a turn running a 4 s cell, through a local proxy so the hand redialed the Function, left the cell completed (`slept 4000`). The SDK's own changes since (SDK 25614f5f5): a turn whose session has nothing running on the hand no longer waits for the hand's answer to its `CancelTurn` at its end, which took the exec call's result to the turn's completion from a median 84-118 ms to 43-46 ms on that preview, and the hand keeps brain tool lists across reconnects.

Hand listings say which attached hands run no scripts: the Cloudflare session's `GET /sessions/<s>/hands`, the Vercel chat route's `?hands` (`Hands.listing`) and `HandRelay`'s listing answer `tools_only` beside `hands`, read from each catalog's `tools_only` (`Remote.toolsOnly`). `Relay.Source.listing()` (the Cloudflare and Vercel transports, `Cloud.listing` for machines, `Session.listing` for the site's relay) returns both as `Relay.Listing`, `useHand` keeps them as `HandState.toolsOnly` and `HandState.where` (`Relay.where`: `on <hands that run scripts> · tools from <tools-only hands>`), and `HandConnect` and the machines chat show `where`; before, a session with only a tools-only tab attached said it ran `on tab` while its scripts ran on the server.

`HandConnect`'s command panel also links to https://nanocode.xyz/hand with the session's hand URL and token in the hash (`Relay.commands` returns it as `tab`; `tab: false` or another page through `HandConnect`'s and `commands`'s `tab` option), a page that makes the visitor's browser tab the session's hand: it runs the cells on just-bash in the tab or, tools only, offers the tab's location, notifications and clipboard. On 2026-10-28 through that page, in headless Chrome 156, the deployed Vercel, Cloudflare and home page demos ran `uname -a` and `pwd` in the tab and, tools only, read its location, and the machines demo (a session started over its API with a Tempo CLI key, the hand URL through nanocode.xyz/machines) ran `uname -a && pwd` there in 5.8 s.

Anyone can connect a machine to a demo session with one command, as a way to bring people to the `nc` CLI. The installer is https://nanocode.xyz/install (nanocodexyz/nanocode's `install`, uploaded to its rolling `nanocode-cli` release with the builds and served by nanocodexyz/nanocode-site): it picks the build for the machine, checks it against the release's `SHA256SUMS`, installs `nanocode` and `nc` to `~/.nanocode/bin`, puts that on the PATH and runs `nanocode` with its arguments, so `curl -fsSL https://nanocode.xyz/install | sh -s -- hand '<url>' --token <token>` installs and connects in one paste. `vercel({ hands: true })` gives every session it starts a random 128-bit hand channel, sent as `hand` with the session's first POST and kept in the transport's snapshot (`session().hand`), and `hands()` reads `GET ?sessionId=<id>&hands`, which `createNanocode` answers with the attached hand's name from its catalog (`attachedHands(world, runId)`: the local socket, else the attach stream). `Hand` from `nanocode-ts/react` (parts `hand`, `handTitle`, `handText`, `handCommand`, `handActions`, `handButton`, `handStatus`) shows a command, a copy button and whether a machine is attached. The Cloudflare runtime mints a per-session hand token: `POST /sessions/<name>/hands` answers `{ token }`, `nch_` plus the account's UTF-8 in hex, `_` and a 256-bit secret the session object keeps in its metadata (the same token on every call); a request to `/sessions/<name>/hands` or `/hands/<hand>` bearing such a token skips `authenticate`, routes by the account it names, and the session object refuses it unless it matches its secret and is a `GET` on the hands route, so the token can attach hands to that one session and nothing else; `cloudflare()`'s `handToken()` and `hands()` mint it and list the attached hands. With Miniflare and the public demo's routes on 2026-10-27, a minted token attached `nanocode hand` and ran a turn's cell on it, a wrong token was refused with 403, on which the hand stops, and the same token failed on a turn route.

`HandRelay` (`nanocode-ts/cloudflare`, `src/cloudflare/Relay.ts`) gives a hand to a brain that cannot accept sockets, such as an agent running in a browser tab: hands and brains both dial `relay(request, namespace)`, which routes by a token of 32 to 128 hex characters (`Authorization: Bearer` or `?token=`, read by `relayToken(request)`) to one Durable Object per token, using the hibernation API. A socket is a hand unless its query says `role=brain`. The hand's first frame is its catalog, which the object stores and offers to each brain that connects while the hand is attached; the hand's later frames go to the brains that took the catalog, and a brain's frames to the hand. A hand socket that comes with the same `runtime_id` replaces the earlier one (closed with 1000), and one with another runtime closes it with 1008 and `another hand attached under this name`, so a second `nanocode hand` on that token stops. When the hand reconnects or leaves, brains that took the earlier catalog are closed with 4001 (`HAND_LEFT`), a newer brain closes older ones with 4002 (`BRAIN_REPLACED`), and a brain is closed with 4001 at once when no hand is attached unless its query has `wait`. A `GET` without an upgrade answers `{ hands: [name] }`. `relayHands(url, token, policy, onHand, signal)` (`nanocode-ts`, `src/Bridge.ts`) is the brain side: it dials `?role=brain&wait&token=`, makes a `RemoteExecutor` from the catalog and calls `onHand({ executor, name, connection })`; on a close it parks the executor and redials, at once after a socket that carried a catalog and otherwise backing off from 1 s to 30 s, a catalog with the executor's `runtime_id` rebinds it (the SDK then sends `Resume`), another runtime gets a new executor, `onHand(undefined)` follows when the executor fails, such as when the hand stays away for `timeout_ms`, and 4002 stops it. Node's WebSocket fires `error` and no `close` when a dial is refused, so a socket ends on either. `RemoteExecutor.host()` is the `remote` option of `AgentRuntime.open` over that executor: it claims the executor for the runtime it is passed to, whose earlier runtime's polls then end, and does not dispose it with the runtime, since the relay loop owns it; a browser agent opens `{ hands: true }` with it, calls `attach(name, connection)` and submits turns with `hand: name`, and reopens without them when `onHand` reports no hand. `openSession({ scope, open(hand), relay })` (`src/Session.ts`) is that agent's turn loop for a host that drives its own runtime, as nanocodexyz/nanocode-site's home agent does: `open(hand)` returns an opened runtime, with the hand's config and `remote: hand.executor.host()` when it gets one, and the session attaches the hand (`handName(hand)`, the catalog's name or `machine`), names it as each turn's `hand`, drives queued turns (a waiting turn again after `waitMs`, 500 ms) and answers `ask`, `steer`, `resume`, `runtime()` and `interrupted`. Work on the runtime holds a lease, and the session reopens on the newest hand, or without one, only when no lease is held, so a machine that connects or leaves mid-turn takes effect after the turn; a reopen that fails on a hand reopens without it, a runtime left closed by a failed reopen is opened again by the next lease, and nothing reopens after `dispose`. A turn submitted on a hand records it in its `metadata` (`{ hand }`), so after a reload `resume()` waits up to the relay policy's `timeout_ms` for the relay to report a hand before it drives the interrupted turn, instead of resuming it without one. `hand()` creates the relay token on first use, reports it through `relay.keep` and starts `relayHands`, which starts at once when `relay.token` is given. In Node against `wrangler dev` on 2026-10-27, a session opened without a hand, reopened on `nanocode hand` once it attached through the relay, and ran two turns' cells on it in 47 and 22 ms; a process killed 1.5 s into a 3 s cell on the hand left the turn pending, and the next process on the same database opened without a hand, waited in `resume()` until the relay reported it (69 ms), finished the turn with the SDK's unknown-outcome result for the interrupted call and ran the next turn's cell on the hand. `createRelayToken()`, `relaySource(url, token)` (a `HandSource` for a relay), `handCommands(access, install)`, `socketUrl(path)` and `INSTALL_URL` (`nanocode-ts/client`) build what a page shows, and `useHand(source)` with `HandConnect` (`nanocode-ts/react`) show the command, poll `hands()` (every 3 s while open, 20 s while a machine is attached) and say which machine runs the session; `vercel()` and `cloudflare()` transports are `HandSource`s too (`hand()`, `hands()`). Measured on 2026-10-27 with `wrangler dev` (nanocodexyz/nanocode `output/relay-test`, a Node brain with the SDK module and a scripted Claude model, `nanocode hand` 0.0.0-25614f5f5): a `tools.Bash` cell ran on the hand through the relay in a 69-90 ms turn; after the hand was restarted as a new runtime the brain got a new executor and the next turn ran on it; and restarting the relay 2.5 s into a 4 s cell left the cell completed with its output 5.4 s after the turn started, through the brain's redial, the rebind and the SDK's `Resume`.

`AgentRuntime.instantiate(module, sql, { published, wake })` instantiates the module for one session scope; `submit`, `append`, `turn`, `pending`, `page`, `cancel`, `interrupted`, `retry`, `finish`, `import`, `importEvents`, `catalog` and `admit` run on that instance through the SDK's `SessionLog` without opening the SDK session, and `open(config, options)` opens it on the same instance; it cannot be opened twice. `append(scope, events)` logs host-authored events through `SessionLog::append` in one transaction in order with the SDK's own events and publishes them like any logged event: each is `{ request_id?, event }` where `event` is a JSON object with a string `source` naming the host (for example `my-host`), `request_id` attributes it to a turn that is in the log, so `page` with that turn returns it, and the SDK's `source` values `agent` and `subagent` are reserved, so one of them, a missing `source` or an unknown turn refuses the whole batch with nothing logged; an event above `HostOptions.log_logical_limit` is logged as an `omitted` marker like any other and a body above `value_limit` is stored in `log_parts`, and `liveEvent` maps none of them, whatever their `type`, because they are the host's to render, nor does one end a turn's event stream in the Cloudflare runtime or its transport. A host records an annotation such as a runner change in the session's log this way instead of a table of its own, which it would have to merge with the log's order. `config.model` names a model of `config.provider`, such as `gpt-6-luna` or `claude-haiku-5-5`, and `config.thinking` its reasoning effort; without them a session uses Sol or Opus 5.5 at the model's default effort. A Codex session's model must be a Codex model; a Claude session takes any model ID since SDK f96a9a0d7, a Claude model ID or alias or another model a Messages gateway serves, such as `zai/glm-5.3` or `moonshotai/kimi-k3` on the Vercel AI Gateway, and a model the SDK does not know needs `config.max_tokens`, its output limit, which the Rust side applies through `ClaudeHarness::configure`. `config.gateway: "vercel"` routes a Codex API-key session through the SDK's `ResponsesGateway::vercel()`, which names OpenAI models with the gateway's `openai/` prefix and also serves the open-weight GLM-5.3, Kimi K3 and MiMo V2.6 Pro (`config.model` `glm-5.3`, `kimi-k3` or `mimo-v2.6-pro`) as `zai/glm-5.3`, `moonshotai/kimi-k3` and `xiaomi/mimo-v2.6-pro` since SDK 3d983dea2, at `config.endpoint` (`https://ai-gateway.vercel.sh/v1`); a ChatGPT subscription with it is a configuration error, and Claude sessions need only the gateway's Messages URL as `config.endpoint`, since the Vercel AI Gateway accepts Claude's own model IDs. `config.gateway: "workers_ai"` with `config.cloudflare_account_id` runs a Codex API-key session on Workers AI through the SDK's `ChatGateway::workers_ai`, which serves GLM-5.3 (`glm-5.3`) as `@cf/zai-org/glm-5.3` and translates its Responses requests to Chat Completions inside the module: `dispatch` receives them with an `endpoint` ending in `/chat/completions` at that account's `/ai/v1`, and `config.endpoint` is not used. `config.service_tier` (`standard`, `priority`, `fast` or `ultrafast`, the `ServiceTier` type) is a new session's initial service tier, which the SDK sends to OpenAI models as `service_tier: "priority"` and refuses as a configuration error for models without a faster tier, such as Claude models other than Opus 5.5. `config.stream_tool_input: true` logs and publishes the SDK's `tool.input.delta` events, each tool call's input as the model writes it with its `call_id` and `tool`, which `liveEvent` maps to `tool_input` events with `name`, `delta` and `call_id`, so a page can show code, a file or a frame while the model writes the call; it turns on the SDK's `tool_input_deltas` on both backends, and without it the agent emits none, because the log would store every tool input twice. `config.mcp_servers` (`{ name, url, headers?, bearer_token? }`) connects Streamable HTTP MCP servers during `open` through the SDK's `nanocode_mcp_tools` (`src/wasm/mcp.rs`) and registers their tools as the SDK's MCP tools, named, described and converted as natively and offered like host tools; their requests go through `dispatch`, so a paying fetch pays MCP servers too. A server that fails to start fails `open` with a retryable error and an invalid server is a `configuration` refusal; every `open` discovers the servers' tools again. `config.subagents: false` leaves out the delegation tools, `config.providers` opens the other provider's backend as well, so a subagent can select a model of either (the SDK registers every configured backend in one `Harness`, and a child that names a provider the session did not configure fails with "<provider> harness is not configured for this host"): `providers.claude` or `providers.codex` takes that provider's `endpoint`, `subscription`, `gateway`, `cloudflare_account_id` and `max_tokens` as the top-level fields do for the session's own provider, which they keep configuring, so an entry for the session's own provider is a configuration error instead of a second way to set the same thing, and an optional `instructions` that replaces `config.instructions` for that backend's agents, with the hand placement and Code Mode instructions appended to either, so a Claude child gets the host's Claude prompt and a Codex child its own; `stream_tool_input`, `config.tools`, `config.mcp_servers` and the Code Mode host apply to every backend, each backend's agents get their own provider's workspace tools when `config.workspace` is set, and the default model of a backend that is not the session's is Sol. On 2026-10-22 a Claude session that spawned a Codex child dispatched its own requests as `claude` and the child's as `codex`, and the child's Codex requests carried the Codex instructions and the Claude requests only the session's, where without `providers` the same spawn failed with that refusal; with `config.workspace` and an MCP server as well, the root's requests offered `Bash`, `Read`, `Edit` and `Write` and the child's `exec_command`, `write_stdin`, `update_plan` and `apply_patch`, each with the host tool and the MCP tool and neither with the other provider's workspace tools, directly and inside Code Mode, and the MCP requests reached `dispatch` with `provider` null. The `hands` tool is offered only to sessions with a remote executor or hands. On 2026-10-21 through the Vercel AI Gateway, nanocode-vercel-durable's first Luna request with its six workspace tools held 796 input tokens instead of 2,469 (6 KB instead of 17.6 KB, and 2.6 KB instead of 15.5 KB for Claude), and the priority tier moved Luna's median time to first text from 989 to 792 ms over 8 requests each, the slowest from 1,750 to 995 ms. The host passes the configuration through unchanged except for the fields it adds (`runtime_id`, `started_ms` and the defaults of `provider`, `remote`, `hands`, `event_coalesce_ms` and `tools`), and the Rust `Configuration` denies unknown fields, so a misspelled or unsupported field is a `configuration` refusal at `open` instead of a silently ignored one; `scripts/build.mjs` fails when `AgentConfiguration` and the Rust `Configuration` name different fields. Before, `open` copied a fixed list of fields and dropped `max_tokens` until it was added there. `config.instructions` adds host instructions to the agent's developer context, before the hand placement instructions when Code Mode has an executor or hands. `identity` reads the name and runtime ID a hand's catalog advertises, and `catalog` only the name. `retry` records why a turn waits on its host, with an error, kind and next retry time, and `finish` ends a turn the host gives up on as failed or cancelled. `open` throws `HostFailure` with kind `configuration` and `permanent` set when the SDK refuses the runtime configuration or the session it opens, such as a reasoning effort its model does not take, so a host can fail the turn instead of retrying it (before, the session's refusal was a plain error and durable-harness redelivered the drive without end); hosts construct `HostFailure` for their own classifications too. `drive` runs the SDK's `TurnInbox` for one root turn, `settle` reports quiescence, `compact` runs the SDK's `AgentHandle::compact` for a session between turns and answers `{"compacted":true}` once its events are logged and published, or `{"busy":true}` without compacting when a turn is running, a submission is pending in the log, or a subagent or an earlier `compact` is active, because the Claude backend's compaction cancels every running turn, so the check must precede it; a `drive` that arrives while a compaction runs answers `{"waiting":true,"busy":true}` like a second concurrent `drive`, and a `submit` only logs the turn, which the next `drive` runs on the compacted history. A Codex compaction logs and publishes `model.compaction.started`, `model.attempt.started` and `model.compaction.completed` with no `request_id`, a Claude compaction `model.compaction.started` and `model.compaction.completed` (or `model.compaction.failed`) with no `request_id` as well, and a Claude session with no history answers a command error ("cannot compact empty history"). `attach` and `detach` register hands, `preconnect` opens a Codex socket early, `steer(requestId, prompt, into?)` steers the running turn, or only the turn `into` names, through the SDK's `TurnInbox::steer` with an identified steer and answers false when no turn takes it, so a host that steers a turn it saw running cannot reach one that started after its check, `replaceQueued(scope, requestId, editId, text)` edits a queued prompt's text in place through the SDK's `SessionLog::replace_queued`, keeping its images, publishes the `replaced` receipt and answers `applied` once per edit ID, `already_applied` for any retry, `conflict` for an edit ID reused with other text, `not_queued` or `too_large` with `bytes` and `limit`, `moveQueued(requestId, into?)` moves a queued prompt into the running turn through `TurnInbox::move_queued`, whose decision is the running turn's durable steer receipt, so a crash between the steer and the dequeue neither loses nor repeats it, and answers `moved` (also for a repeat), `not_queued`, `not_running`, `conflict` or `unknown` (the session cannot prove the steer absent, and nothing moved); on 2026-10-22 a Node script with a scripted Claude mock steered a named turn, saw a steer naming a queued turn refused, edited, retried and moved a queued prompt while its turn ran, and the running turn's next request carried the edited prompt once while the queued turn ended cancelled with `steered: true`, `subagents.agents()`, `subagents.sendUserMessage(agent_id, { message, priority })`, `subagents.interrupt(agent_id)`, `subagents.close(agent_id)` and `subagents.sideQuestion(agent_id, { question })` run the SDK's `SubagentControl::agents`, `send_user_message`, `interrupt`, `close` and `side_question` for the session's root (WASM commands `agents`, `send_user_message`, `interrupt`, `close` and `side_question`): `agents` answers the whole tree (`AgentDirectoryEntry`: `agent_id`, `parent_agent_id`, `role` and `task` cut to the SDK's 160 bytes, `status`, model, thinking, usage, `can_message`, `can_manage`), `sendUserMessage` delivers the message as `MessageSender::User` and answers its `MessageReceipt`, whose `disposition` is `steered` for an urgent message to a running child, `queued` for a deferred one, and `started` for either to an idle child, `interrupt` and `close` answer the summaries (`AgentSummary`) of the agents they stopped (the child and its descendants, with each `status`), and `sideQuestion` answers `{ text, usage }` (`SideAnswer`) from the child's last committed state without changing its transcript; each refuses an unknown agent, a session opened with `config.subagents: false` (it has no subagent runtime) and a disposed runtime, and a side question refuses a child without a completed turn. `config.max_resident_subagents` sets `SubagentControl::set_max_resident`, the idle children kept open in memory; without it the journal-backed sessions this package opens keep the SDK's `DEFAULT_MAX_RESIDENT_SUBAGENTS` (16), because `DURABLE_MAX_RESIDENT_SUBAGENTS` (2) applies only to `Registry::set_store`, which nothing here calls. Eviction takes only idle children, so the limit never stops a running one; on 2026-10-22 with two Claude children whose model requests were held open, interrupting the first answered its summary as `interrupted` and closing the second as `closed`, and the host's `dispatch` signals for both requests aborted. `completionOrigin(requestId)` returns the host context a subagent completion turn's child was spawned with, the spawning root turn's request ID for a root's child, or null for any other request ID, so a host can authorize a completion turn's requests by the turn that spawned the child, `checkThinking` checks a reasoning effort against the session's model and `setThinking` also applies it to the model calls that follow, which both backends allow at any time (`Mutability::Anytime`), and `settings({ thinking?, service_tier? })` (`RuntimeSettings`) applies either or both to the open session without reopening it: it checks every field against the model's `ModelCapabilities` first and applies none when one is refused, because the Codex backend stores a service tier it was given while idle without checking it, so a model without a faster tier would otherwise fail its next request instead of the call; on the Codex backend the request's `reasoning.effort` keeps the session's baseline and the change reaches the model as a `configuration_update` item with `reasoning.effort` in the next request's input, while a service tier replaces the request's `service_tier` (a mock-provider run on 2026-10-22), `connected` reports whether it holds an open model socket, `releaseConnections` closes model sockets and `dispose` shuts the SDK session down. A model response body or Codex socket that the SDK cancels after the host registered it, before the reply reached WASM, is cancelled or closed at once instead of staying open until `dispose`. `metrics` reports counters, WASM memory, timing spans and the last `RECENT_MODEL_LIMIT` (64) model dispatches, the retention policy for diagnostics. `sessionUuid()` makes the UUIDv7 session IDs the SDK requires, and the host uses it for its runtime ID too, because `crypto.randomUUID` is missing from some iOS browsers, such as Brave, where `crypto.getRandomValues` works.

`config.hand_tools` selects advertised tools of remote hands by the session's backend family: `{ codex?: { include?: string[], exclude?: string[] }, claude?: { include?: string[], exclude?: string[] } }`, with canonical names, an omitted include allowing the whole advertised catalog and excludes winning. Omitted families allow every advertised tool; unsupported family keys are configuration errors. `HandToolSelection` exports the include/exclude type. The WASM host applies the SDK's `RemoteCodeModeHost::with_tool_selection` to the default executor and every attached hand, and the SDK binds each root and child's own family, so mixed-provider hosts can keep provider-native workspace schemas on one hand while sharing other tools. The SDK filters the model contract and hand ALL_TOOLS and sends the fixed selection with Execute and cross-hand Call; a hidden tool is refused before dispatch. A policy stays fixed for the session; changing it on a live hand session fails. The hand must advertise the SDK's `tool_selection` capability: older hands and hands with dynamic providers or a runner pool are refused when a policy is configured, while omitting `hand_tools` keeps their behavior. Local Code Mode (`LocalCells`) publishes no native hand tools and does not advertise this capability, so local-only Code Mode leaves `hand_tools` unset. The selection covers only tools a remote hand advertises: the workspace tools `config.workspace` registers, MCP tools and host `tools` are the session's own and `hand_tools` never filters them. `createNanocode` from `nanocode-ts/cloudflare` attaches and reattaches its hands through `attach`, so a policy reaches them, but it opens sessions without `hand_tools`, so its sessions see every advertised tool. On 2026-10-22 an external Node script with SQLite, an advertised hand catalog and scripted Claude and Codex providers completed both turns and checked the actual exec declarations: Claude had Read/Edit/Write/Bash/TaskOutput/TaskStop, Codex apply_patch/exec_command/write_stdin, and both view_image/update_plan; a native SDK socket exercise also refused a hidden Read without invoking its handler.

`config.environment` (`{ cwd?, shell?, timezone? }`) describes the machine the session's tools run on instead of the one running the module, for every backend the session configures: a Codex backend gives it to the SDK's `ExecutionEnvironment` (working directory, shell and timezone in the model's environment context, with the date read through `calendar_date` on every turn so a long session follows the host's clock), and a Claude backend to `ClaudeEnvironment` (working directory and shell only, since Claude's environment section has no timezone and keeps the module's UTC date). `environment.date` sets the Claude environment's `Today's date` (`ClaudeEnvironment::date`, for every Claude agent of the session, children included), which the SDK otherwise reads from the clock each time a runtime opens; the Cloudflare app sends the UTC date the session first opened, kept in its metadata as `session_date` (or `session_date:<scope>`), so a runtime reopened after a UTC midnight, as every deploy reopens them, sends the system prompt it sent before instead of rewriting it and missing the provider's prompt cache for the whole conversation (2026-11-08, build-0b6a628). Checked in tempoxyz/gateway's harness (its AGENTS.md has the run), which moved the session isolate's `Date.now` a day ahead and then reset the session between a tool result and the next call: on build-6dd81a7 the recovered call read `Today's date: 2026-11-09` where earlier calls read `2026-11-08`, and on build-0b6a628 every call read `2026-11-08`. A Codex backend's date follows the clock, since the SDK appends a new environment context when it changes, which keeps the earlier prefix. `open` refuses a timezone `Intl.DateTimeFormat` does not know, and a Codex session without a timezone uses Etc/UTC, as the SDK does on WASM. The working directory the model is told is where `config.workspace` tools run: `environment.cwd` defaults to `config.workspace` when that is set, `open` refuses an `environment.cwd` that differs from it as a `configuration` error, and without either a Codex session reports the SDK workspace `/` while a Claude session with `config.environment` names no working directory. `modelCatalog()` and `nanocode-ts/models` give each model its `context_window_tokens` and `auto_compact_token_limit` (null when the backend does not compact on its own) from the SDK's `default_context_window_tokens`, `CONTEXT_WINDOW_TOKENS` and `auto_compact_token_limit`, so hosts keep no copies. `config.auto_compact_window_tokens` sets the Claude backend's auto-compaction window (the SDK's `ClaudeBuilder::auto_compact_window_tokens`, for every Claude agent of the session, children included; a positive number), and without it the module uses `CLAUDE_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW_TOKENS`, 200,000, so a Claude session compacts once its context reaches 167,000 tokens (the window less the SDK's 33,000-token reserve), which the catalog reports as the Claude models' `auto_compact_token_limit`. Before 2026-10-24 Claude sessions used the model's whole window, 1,000,000 tokens for Haiku, Sonnet and Opus 5.5, so the public demos sent requests of up to 967,000 tokens before compacting; on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare locally (wrangler dev, Claude Haiku 5.5 relayed to gpt-5.4-mini) a turn of 195,000 input tokens was followed by a 195,013-token request before and by a compaction and a 9,380-token request after, and a 161,000-token turn did not compact. A restored Claude checkpoint carries the window it was saved with, so sessions started before may keep the old one. On 2026-10-22 a Codex session opened with /srv/hand/work, fish and Pacific/Auckland sent those three values and Auckland's date, 2026-10-23 while UTC was still 2026-10-22. On Cloudflare, `App.create({ limits })` sets both per session: `limits({ provider, model }, env)` runs before each runtime opens, with the session's provider and model (`CLAUDE_MODEL` for a Claude session without one), and the `max_tokens` and `auto_compact_window_tokens` it returns join the open configuration, so a host can open a Claude session on a model outside the SDK's catalog, which the Claude backend refuses without `max_tokens` (`Unknown Claude model: configure max_tokens explicitly`). tempoxyz/gateway's agents use it for Workers AI models (2026-10-29); before, they wrapped each runtime's `open` through the app's internal `instance`. `App.create({ harnesses: 'both' })` also opens a session's other harness, as `providers` with that provider's API endpoint (`https://api.openai.com/v1` or `https://api.anthropic.com/v1/messages`, so it runs in API-key mode), the `max_tokens` that `limits` returns for it without a model, and the host's `instructions`, and gives both harnesses `environment.cwd`, so `spawn_agent` may switch harness (the module refuses a switch to a harness the session did not open); the default `session` opens only the session's own. Every `SessionAuthority` the app hands the host (egress dispatches, host tool calls, MCP and cell fetches) now carries `session_name`, the name the host addressed the session by, so a host can tell which of an account's sessions a request serves. tempoxyz/gateway uses both for its agents (2026-10-29). Since SDK ee0f8ba1e a subagent's thinking is no longer bounded by its parent's, so a session at low may spawn a child on the other harness with `thinking: null`, which takes that harness's default effort (medium); before, `spawn_agent` refused it with "medium thinking exceeds the spawning agent's own low thinking". `App.create({ hands: 'always' })` opens every session in hands mode (the SDK's placement instructions, its `hands` tool and `handInstructions`) whether or not a hand is attached, so a hand that attaches while a turn runs is usable in that same turn, steers included: a runtime opened without hands mode only attaches hands it can place work on once it is reopened, at the next turn. Cells that name no hand still run in the session; on 2026-10-29 in Node a Codex session opened with `hands: true` and no hand ran a cell locally and listed `hands` among its nested tools. The default, `attached`, keeps the earlier behavior. A hand socket that closes with `Remote.handLeft` (4001) detaches from the session at once instead of being parked for `REMOTE_TIMEOUT_MS` while the session waits for it to reconnect, so a relay or hub that knows its hand is gone for good (removed, revoked, replaced or past its own grace) takes it out of the session's `hands` listing and placement immediately; any other close still parks. tempoxyz/gateway's account hands close with it (2026-10-30): before, a hand removed from the account stayed listed on its agents for 30 s.

`config.auto_compact_window_tokens` bounds a session's context for both backends (2026-11-07). A Claude backend takes it as its auto-compact window, as before, and defaults to 200,000 without it. A Codex backend takes it as its context window (`OpenAiBuilder::context_window_tokens`, which also trims old tool outputs to 95% of it) and compacts at 90% of it (`OpenAiBuilder::auto_compact_token_limit`, nanocode d7fca4a87), the fraction the SDK's own `auto_compact_token_limit` applies to OpenAI's models; without it a Codex session keeps the SDK's defaults, under which a model outside OpenAI's list never compacts. Before, the field reached only the Claude backend, so the gateway's Codex agents on 1M-token models never compacted: on 2026-11-07 its production ledger held 1,173 agent model calls above 244,800 prompt tokens in two days (up to 866,705), $9.60 of the $23.76 its agents' model calls cost, all of them Codex agents on DeepSeek V4.1 Flash. A compacting Codex session sends its history with a `compaction_trigger` item and expects one `compaction` output item, so its model endpoint must answer that protocol (the gateway's `/v1/responses` does since this change; the SDK's chat transport does for chat gateways). Checked with `cargo check` of the SDK's codex crate for native and `wasm32-unknown-unknown`; the module was built by the release workflow. The gateway pinned build-7ec9ce9 and answered that protocol (tempoxyz/gateway f6eb836, deployed as version fdcce456 on 2026-11-07): locally, a Codex agent on a 131,072-token test model compacted at 138,006 tokens and its next request fell from 169,143 tokens to 109,470, where build-38dd331 grew to 200,283 without compacting; on production, Swarms swarm f7df8db3fe54's lead compacted from 708,467 tokens and its next 24 calls sent 20,037 to 46,387.

`exportBranch(session_id, at)`, `exportPage(session_id, exported, cursor, max_bytes)`, `importBranch(session_id, exported)`, `importPage(session_id, exported, page)` and `publishBranch(session_id, exported)` carry the SDK's branch transfer (`SessionStore::export_branch`, `export_page`, `import_branch` and `BranchImport`) on an instance that need not be opened, so a host that keeps one store per conversation, such as a Durable Object per thread, can fork a thread into another object or run a throwaway fork: the source instance answers the export and its pages, the target imports each page, which the SDK verifies against its content address and commits with the import's cursor, and publishes the copy, which the host then opens with `config.session_id` set to the imported ID. `at` is `"latest"`, `{ through: turn }` or `{ before: turn }`, naming turns by request ID. `importBranch` and `importPage` answer the next cursor to request, `null` once every page is in, so a host that loses an instance mid-import calls `importBranch` on a new one and continues from the cursor it answers, and a page the import already holds is ignored; `src/wasm/branch.rs` keeps the open import per session ID on the instance, so later pages do not acquire the target again and a new instance acquires it once. `importEvents(scope, after, events)` copies the conversation's session log through the SDK's `SessionLog::import_events`: it writes events that the source's `page(scope, after, limit)` returned under their own seq, request ID, creation time and body, answers the last imported seq as the cursor for the next page, writes nothing for events the target already holds and refuses, before writing, a different event at an imported seq, a target log that ends before `after`, a seq another scope owns and an event above `HostOptions.log_logical_limit`; a body above `value_limit` is stored in `log_parts` like an appended one, and a replay decodes it before comparing. A full fork is therefore: `exportBranch`, `importBranch` and `importPage` until the cursor is `null`, and `publishBranch`; then `import` of each copied turn with the source's `first_seq` and `last_seq` from `turn` (the host supplies the prompt, which finished turns no longer keep); then `page` on the source and `importEvents` on the target up to the boundary turn's `last_seq`; then `open` with `config.session_id` set to the imported ID. Events cross as parsed JSON, so a replay compares their re-encoded bodies with the bodies the import stored, and the target pages the same JSON values as the source. On 2026-10-22 `bridge-transfer.mjs` (node:sqlite with mock Claude and Codex endpoints, kept outside the repository) exported each backend's three-turn session, whose second prompt was 35 KB, Through its second turn in 4 KiB pages, moved it into another database across an instance restart after two pages, refused a page with one changed byte and an early publish, resumed the copy, which answered from both inherited turns, and forked the copy through its first inherited turn into a third database, which answered from that turn alone. On 2026-10-22 `bridge-fork.mjs` (node:sqlite with the same mocks, kept outside the repository) forked each backend's four-turn session through its third turn: the target paged the same events as the source through that turn (Claude 30, 45,780 bytes of JSON; Codex 33, 49,711 bytes), every copied turn's `turn_page` and report matched, a new instance replaying the first page and a replay of the whole range answered the same cursor, a changed event and a gap were refused, and the opened fork answered from the three copied turns with its next turn's events starting at the following seq.

Hosts implement these interfaces (`src/Contracts.ts`):

- `SqlHost.transaction(kind, operation, statements)` runs one operation's statements in one atomic SQLite transaction and returns each statement's rows as arrays. `SqlHost.value_limit`, when set, is the largest string, BLOB or row the host's SQLite stores: `instantiate` passes it to the module with the `configure` command before any other, and the module gives it to every scope's `SessionLog` as `value_limit`, so owned event bodies and prompts above the physical row budget are stored in immutable content-keyed `log_parts` within that transaction, and `import` batches within the physical binding budget. `HostOptions.log_logical_limit`, when set at `instantiate`, separately bounds logical admission and event sizes: oversized events become `omitted`, results fail the turn and prompts return `{"submitted":"too_large","bytes","limit"}`. Without that option, logical values have no admission cap. `AgentRuntime.page(scope, after, limit, { request_id?, max_bytes? })` bounds decoded event JSON UTF-8 bytes before reading parts; it excludes the crossing event except that it always returns the first event, even with a zero-byte budget. The page envelope is outside the byte budget; bounded pages require a positive event count. Passing a request ID string as the fourth argument keeps the earlier count-only API. On 2026-10-22 an external Node SQLite exercise of the compiled module with a 2,000,000-byte binding guard admitted a 3,000,022-byte value, kept the largest binding at 1,999,909 bytes, read a 3,000,095-byte result event back exactly in its own page, exercised import/reopen and explicit logical admission, and kept a small submit-and-finish at four row writes. `kind` is `store` for the SDK's `SqlStore` and `log` for its `SessionLog`; the SDK owns the `states`, `owners`, `blobs`, `records`, `turns`, `events` and `log_parts` tables and every statement (since SDK 197a41a8c records go to `blobs` and `records` holds only what earlier builds wrote), binds only text parameters and reads text or null. Durable Objects' `transactionSync`, `node:sqlite` and sqlite-wasm satisfy it synchronously; an asynchronous host may return a promise.
- `published(events, bytes)` receives each batch the SDK's `SessionEvents` pump logs, already committed, in sequence order, with each event's encoded JSON size in bytes; `wake()` runs after the SDK's remote Code Mode client receives a batch or finishes a callback, so a host can check quiescence again.
- `dispatch(request, signal, timing)` sends a model or MCP HTTP request with its `method` and returns a fetch `Response`; model requests are `POST`, and an MCP server's event stream is a `GET` whose empty body the host must not forward. `request.provider` is `claude` or `codex` for a model request, the backend that sent it, which in a session with `config.providers` is a child's backend rather than the session's, so a host can route, credential or meter by provider without parsing the endpoint, and null for an MCP request, which no model backend sends. A response without a body, such as an MCP server's `202` or `204` for a notification, reads as an empty body. The body crosses from WASM as raw bytes and must be forwarded unchanged. The host owns credentials: Claude clients send no authentication headers and Codex uses `OpenAiAuth::host_managed_chatgpt()` for subscriptions and `OpenAiAuth::gateway_managed()` for API keys, so the host or its egress injects them. Redirects are refused. `request.origin` is the SDK's origin of the request (`nanocode::http::origin`): the opaque host context or turn operation whose work it continues, which for a root session is the turn's request ID, also for the requests after a steer and those of a background child the turn spawned, a subagent completion turn's `subagent-completion:` ID, or null, so a host can authorize each model request by its turn. MCP requests carry a null origin: the SDK scopes the origin around a Codex turn's whole task but only around a Claude model response, so an MCP call's requests would otherwise carry the turn on one backend and nothing on the other, and `src/wasm/mcp.rs` sends them outside any origin. `request.subagent` is `{ id, parent, tree }` when a subagent made the request (`tree` is its top-level ancestor, the child of the root its branch starts from) and null for the root: `src/wasm/transport.rs` reads the calling agent's session from the SDK's `origin::session()` and resolves it with `SubagentControl::lineage`. An MCP request is sent from the MCP client's connection task, outside the calling agent's poll, so `src/wasm/mcp.rs` resolves its subagent from the session ID the SDK puts in a `tools/call` request's `_meta["x-codex-turn-metadata"]` instead.
- `socket.open(request, signal, timing)` opens a Codex Responses WebSocket (`request.provider` names the backend, always `codex`) with the SDK's handshake headers and returns a `SocketHandshake`: the status, response headers, an open `MessageSocket` (`send`, `close`, `addEventListener` for `message`, `close` and `error`) on 101, an optional `idle_ms` read deadline and `text()` for a rejected handshake's body. `socket.admit()` runs before every frame is sent, so a host can wait for durability or refuse a cancelled turn's frames. `socket.open` receives the opening request's `origin` (null when a session opens its socket before any request) and `subagent`, and `socket.admit(origin, subagent)` each frame's.
- `tools.call(name, input, signal, context)` runs a host tool; `context` is the SDK's `ToolContext` of the calling agent: `call_id`, the model's tool call ID or `<call>/code-<n>` for a Code Mode nested call, which stays the same when a turn replays, so a host can key its own record of the call or an idempotency key on it, `session_id`, the session of the agent that made the call, which is a subagent's own session and not the root's, so a host can let a child read but not write a store the root writes, `turn_id`, the turn that call belongs to (null outside one), `model`, that agent's model ID, and `instruction_revision`, its instruction revision or null. On 2026-10-22 in `node` a root Claude call and the Codex child's call it spawned reported two session IDs and models, `claude-opus-5-5` and `gpt-6.1-sol`, and a different `turn_id` each. `context.origin` is the call's `ToolContext` host context, the root turn's request ID or the host context a child was spawned with, or null, and `context.subagent` the calling subagent as `dispatch` receives it. `fetch(url, init, subagent)` receives the same for a cell's `fetch`. Each `config.tools` definition's `name`, `description` and JSON Schema `parameters` become the SDK's `FunctionTool` with parallel calls, offered directly and inside Code Mode on both backends; a string result becomes the tool's text output and any other result its JSON output, a thrown error a tool error, and a definition with `content: true` may also answer `{ content, structured_result? }`, whose `input_text` and `input_image` items (a data URL and a `detail`) become a text-and-image output through the SDK's `FunctionTool::content_results`, so a tool can show the model a screenshot or preview without a Rust tool, and the SDK keeps the call's receipt like any tool's.
- `remote.execute(request, id)`, `cancel(id)` and `dispose()` carry the SDK's remote Code Mode frames for a configured executor (`connection` null) and for hands. `RemoteExecutor` implements them over any `MessageSocket`: `connect(open, policy)` dials and listens within `timeout_ms`, `accept(socket, policy)` and `restore(socket, policy, catalog)` take a socket whose messages the host forwards to `receive` and `disconnected`, as a hibernating Durable Object does. It keeps frames opaque, enforces `max_frame_bytes`, keeps the first frame as the catalog and lets one runtime own a hand connection at a time (`claim`, `owns`, `release`). `refuse(reason)` closes a connection with 1008 and the reason in the close frame, so the hand can read it. A host that sends none of a `remote_send` batch throws `RemoteRefusal`, which crosses the bridge marked unsent: `RemoteExecutor` throws it when its connection already failed or its first frame cannot be sent, and hosts throw it for their own refusals, such as a revoked hand or a cancelled turn, before any frame reaches the socket. The SDK then reports the batch's operations as not dispatched instead of as unknown outcomes; any other error leaves them unknown. `remote_send` lists every frame that starts work in `origins`, each with its index and the origin of its call; a host that refuses only some of them sets `refused` to their indexes and a `reason` and passes the request on, `RemoteExecutor` writes the other frames and answer `{ refused, reason }`, and the SDK fails only those operations, as not dispatched, while the connection and its other operations keep running (`CodeModeHostError::refused`). With a cell running on a `LocalHand` on 2026-10-22, refusing a second Execute this way failed only that `exec`, as not dispatched, and a later `wait` returned the running cell's output, while throwing `RemoteRefusal` for the same batch failed the `wait` too and failed the hand. With `resumes` in its policy, `remote_catalog` answers `resumes: true` and the WASM link resumes the hand's advertised runtime (`RemoteCatalog::resumes`), so the SDK marks requests resumable, acknowledges replies and sends `Resume` when a later catalog frame arrives; `park(reason)` then keeps the executor open for `timeout_ms` instead of failing it, queuing what `remote_send` writes, and `rebind(socket, catalog)` takes the hand's next socket, hands the new catalog to the SDK as a frame and writes the queued frames. The Cloudflare runtime sets `resumes` and parks a hand's executor when its socket closes or errors; when the same hand name attaches with the runtime ID its executor already serves, `registerHand` rebinds that executor to the new socket under the same connection ID (updating its stored authority) instead of opening a new connection. Before, every dropped hand socket, such as a laptop changing networks, failed the session's running cells with unknown outcomes; with Miniflare and a TCP proxy cut 0.7 s into a 2 s `exec_command` cell, the cell's output now arrives at 2.1 s, a `wait` on a yielded cell cut the same way returns its output, and `measure-hand-faults` passes as before (21 of 22; its first check counts the hands' delayed `hand: connected` lines). Requests are resumable on every Cloudflare hand now, so the hand no longer cancels a session's cells when its socket drops: after a Durable Object restart (a deploy) the old brain's cells keep running on the hand until the new brain's next `CancelTurn` or `Cancel` for the session ends them. On a deployed session with a hand 25 ms away, a resumable turn ran in a median 60-63 ms against 59 ms without resumes (31-33 ms of it the storage sync before the Execute).

Every session is Code Mode only, as codex-rs's `code_mode_only`: the model's tools are `exec` and `wait`, and host, MCP, workspace, web search and subagent tools are reached from cells (Georgios, 2026-10-28). `AgentRuntime.open` sets `code_mode` itself, true unless the session runs its cells on a `remote` executor, which is code-only too, and neither adapter has a switch for it. Before, `code_mode` defaulted to false and a session opened without it offered every tool directly: through `Harness.create` without `codeMode` on 2026-10-28, a gpt-6-luna turn on a ChatGPT plan called `web__run` directly instead of running a cell. The scripted models in `scripts/measure-node.ts` call the host tool from an `exec` cell.

Code Mode runs the SDK's own engine. A session that names no `remote` executor installs `nanocode_code_mode::code_mode::LocalCells` (`src/wasm/cells.rs`) as the executor `local` (`config.executor` renames it), the default one, or with `hands` the fallback (`CodeModeExecutors::set_fallback`): a hands session's cells run on the hand that sent the prompt or the only attached hand that runs cells, and otherwise here, where they reach tools-only hands' tools with `hand`, so the Cloudflare and Vercel runtimes keep the session's `SqlFileSystem` bash in hands sessions too (they dropped it while a hand was attached or named). From #168 until later on 2026-10-28 a hands session had no `LocalCells`, so with only a tools-only hand attached every cell failed with `no hand is selected`; on 2026-10-28 with a tools-only TS phone hand, one cell ran here (`here: local`, `Bash` printing `Linux` and `/workspace`) and called `where_am_i` on the phone, and with the same hand running cells, the cell ran on the phone (`here: phone`). `LocalCells` is the SDK's `CodeModeRuntime` and its QuickJS cell worker (`crates/runtime/code-mode-engine`), compiled into this module, so cells behave exactly as on native builds (the `// @exec:` pragma, yields, `wait`, `terminate`, truncation, session-scoped cell IDs and `store`, nested calls as `<exec call>/code-<n>`, every Code Mode global) and need no `eval` in the host, which Workers and strict Content Security Policies refuse. The session's host tools and the SDK's delegation tools are the cell's `tools.*`, and `exec` and `wait` are the only tools the model calls directly. `LocalCells::prelude` adds host globals to every cell, over `LocalCells::host_tools` that only the prelude reaches (they are not in `tools`, `ALL_TOOLS` or the model's tool list, because on 2026-10-23 gpt-6-luna wrote `tools.bash.writeFile` when the shell was both a nested tool and a global): `src/wasm/prelude.js` defines `fetch` (resolving to a `Response`) over a host `fetch` tool that the host answers with `options.fetch` (`globalThis.fetch` when unset; the `cell_fetch` command sends text bodies as text and others as base64), so a host passes a paying fetch, such as mppx's `Mppx.create(...).fetch`, and every request a script makes is paid without a payment tool; and, when the session has a `config.workspace`, `bash` (`bash(script)`, a tagged template that quotes each interpolated value and spreads arrays as separate words, `bash.readFile`, `bash.writeFile`, `bash.appendFile`) over a host `bash` tool that runs `shell_run` with an optional `stdin`; `bash.writeFile` writes an `ArrayBuffer` or typed array as raw bytes (sent as base64 through `base64 -d`), since `String(content)` had written a 256-byte `Uint8Array` as 913 bytes of comma-separated numbers on nanocode.xyz/vercel on 2026-10-24. The prelude also defines UTF-8 `TextEncoder` and `TextDecoder` (`fatal`, `ignoreBOM`, `stream`, U+FFFD for invalid bytes), which QuickJS lacks: Codex Luna and Claude Haiku both reached for `TextEncoder` to count UTF-8 bytes and lost a cell to `ReferenceError`. `Response.json()` on a body that is not JSON throws a `SyntaxError` naming the URL, the status and the body's start instead of QuickJS's bare `unexpected token: 'Request'`. The rest of what models write in cells and QuickJS lacks is also in the prelude, found on 2026-10-24 by `output/code-mode.mjs`-style plans of about 50 cells (every probe threw `ReferenceError` or `TypeError` before): `Headers`, `Request`, `Response` with `body` (a `ReadableStream` of one chunk), `blob()`, `formData()` and `clone()`, `fetch` bodies from `FormData` (multipart), `URLSearchParams`, `Blob` and streams, `AbortController` signals and `AbortSignal.timeout`, `URL` and `URLSearchParams`, `Blob`, `File`, `FormData`, `ReadableStream`, `WritableStream`, `TransformStream`, `TextDecoderStream`, `Buffer`, `structuredClone`, `EventTarget`, `setInterval`, `setImmediate`, `crypto` (`getRandomValues` and `randomUUID` from `Math.random`, `subtle.digest` for SHA-1, SHA-256, SHA-384 and SHA-512, and HMAC `importKey`, `sign` and `verify`), an `Intl` that formats en-US in UTC and throws `RangeError` for other time zones, with `toLocaleString` on numbers, BigInts and dates and `localeCompare` built on it, `process` (an empty `env`), a `require` that throws an error naming what to use instead, a `bash.readFile(path, { encoding: null })` that returns bytes, and every one also as a property of `globalThis`, so `new Function` and `eval` code reaches them. `BigInt.asUintN` and `asIntN` are replaced, since QuickJS returned `-1n` for `BigInt.asUintN(64, -1n)` and for every width of 32 bits or more. `bash.readFile` reads through `base64` in 600,000-byte pieces when the shell's 1 MB output limit truncates `cat`, and bash results carry `truncated`. QuickJS compiles a whole function body before it runs, and the prelude is compiled again for every cell: as one 105 KB file it added 11 ms to each cell. So `src/wasm/prelude.js` holds only what most cells use, and `cells.rs` puts `src/wasm/prelude/{crypto,extra,intl,web}.js` in block comments of functions ahead of it (`*/` written as `*\u0001/`), which the prelude reads with `Function.prototype.toString` and compiles the first time a cell touches one of their globals: QuickJS lexed 76 KB in a comment in 0.48 ms and as a string literal in 1.8 ms. 100 one-line cells took 1,110 to 1,165 ms instead of 820 to 950. The SDK defines the cell's `console`, `setTimeout`, `clearTimeout`, `text` and `exit` as globals before a cell runs, since it evaluates cells as global scripts, so the prelude leaves them alone and reads them from `globalThis` the first time a timer, `process.stdout` or `process.exit` needs them. The prelude used to install them from a parameter default, `__nanocodeHost = __nanocode(setTimeout, clearTimeout, text, exit)`, which global scripts never evaluate: on 2026-10-24 `setInterval`, `setImmediate`, `AbortSignal.timeout` and `process.exit` threw `TypeError: not a function`, and the prelude's own `console` never reached a cell. The SDK's `console` has Node's methods, an error shows its `cause`, a thrown non-`Error` fails the cell as `Uncaught <JSON>`, a rejection nobody handles fails the cell, and `JSON.stringify` of an object nested 5,000 levels deep throws `RangeError` instead of overflowing V8's native stack inside the module (4,400 levels pass); the `v` regex flag is still a `SyntaxError`. With a workspace the prelude also defines `wasm` and a `WebAssembly` shim, because QuickJS has no WebAssembly, over a host `wasm` tool that the bridge op `cell_wasm` sends to `WorkspaceHost.wasm` (the shell's `wasmHost`, so the same engines and interpreter run it): `wasm.run(bytesOrPath, args, { stdin, env })` writes bytes to `/tmp/.wasm/` and runs the `wasm` command, `wasm.invoke(bytesOrPath, name, ...args)` calls one export of a fresh instance, and `WebAssembly.instantiate`, `compile`, `instantiateStreaming` and `new WebAssembly.Instance` keep an instance in the host (16 per shell, not durable), whose exports are async functions (i64 values as BigInt) and whose memory export has async `read`, `write` and `grow`; imports cannot call back into a cell, so an imported function traps. Bytes cross as base64. `wasm.rs` adds `LOCAL_INSTRUCTIONS`, since the upstream `exec` description says scripts have no network access, and `code_mode` with `remote` is a configuration error. A cell that never awaits blocks the host's event loop and Workers freeze the clock during CPU work, so the engine stops one synchronous run after `DEFAULT_INTERRUPT_CHECKS` (10,000) QuickJS interrupt checks and fails the cell with a message telling the model to yield; on 2026-10-23 that took 2.2 s in Node and 2.3 s in local workerd, and the next cell ran. A runaway recursion throws `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded` and the instance keeps running: the engine's stack check calls the module's `host.stack_room` import, which `src/AgentRuntime.ts` answers with a lower bound of the native stack left (nested `Reflect.apply` calls of 2,048 arguments, 16 KiB each, up to the bytes the engine asks for), and `enter` adds one to the word the import names, so answers from an earlier entry, which started at another native depth, are dropped. The limit follows each host's real stack instead of a fixed budget. Node's main thread has only V8's default 984 KiB, which a Next.js request stack and TurboFan frames of about 390 bytes per QuickJS call leave too small: on 2026-10-25 nanocode.xyz/vercel (Node 24.21, x64) stopped plain recursion near 2,400 calls and `next start` on an M1 at 1,590 to 1,920, while Cloudflare reached 2,700. Vercel ignores `--stack-size` (Node refuses it in `NODE_OPTIONS`, and `v8.setFlagsFromString` does not move a running isolate's limit), so the Vercel harness passes `engine: threadEngine()` (`src/internal/Thread.ts`): the WASM instance runs in a `worker_threads` Worker with `resourceLimits.stackSizeMb` 8, built from the source of `localEngine` (`AgentRuntime.ts`, which therefore references only its parameters and globals), and `AgentRuntime` calls it synchronously: each entry posts its bytes, waits on an `Atomics` flag and reads the reply with `receiveMessageOnPort`, and the module's `dispatch`, `cancel`, `reply` and `panicked` calls reach the host in order after the entry returns, which changes nothing because the host already defers re-entry. `stack_room`, `clock`, `random`, `calendar_date` and the WASI imports run in the worker. Cells then reach the engine's own shadow-stack limit, 2,782 plain calls, on every entry; a runaway recursion still throws `RangeError` and the next cell runs. 200 timer yields and 20 `bash` calls in one cell took 247 to 252 ms against 244 to 264 ms on the main thread, and opening a runtime about 20 ms more for the worker. Hosts without `worker_threads` (Workers, browsers) keep `localEngine` on their own thread. On 2026-10-24 that let about 2,750 plain calls and 4,400 nested `JSON.stringify` levels run in Node 24, Node 26 and workerd (858 and 2,940 before), and none of 28 recursion shapes trapped the instance, also under `node --stack-size=500` and with 0 to 720 KiB of host frames below each entry, where the fixed budget had trapped it. `output/code-mode.mjs` (a scripted Claude mock in Node) ran host tools, a paid POST, `bash` and its file helpers, `notify`, `store` and `load` across cells, a missing tool, a 100 ms yield resumed by `wait`, a thrown error, an endless loop, and a child agent spawned and awaited from a cell; the Cloudflare default deployment ran the same cells in a Durable Object under Miniflare and the Vercel adapter in `npm run drill`. Before 2026-10-23 a TypeScript `LocalHand` reimplemented the cell runtime over the remote protocol and ran cells in quickjs-emscripten; hosts that passed `remote: new LocalHand({ bash, fetch })` now pass `fetch` and `workspace: shell.workspace()` with `config.workspace: shell.cwd`. When the session has a workspace, `open` appends the workspace host's `instructions` to the session's instructions, unless they already contain them: `Bash.workspace()` gives `BASH_CODE_MODE_INSTRUCTIONS`, which tells the model that the persistent filesystem and simulated bash already exist (and that a question about a filesystem, VFS or shell means this one), what the shell can do (git clone, WASI programs, Python on hosts that compile WebAssembly), and without the Python sentence on hosts that cannot compile WebAssembly; on 2026-10-23 GLM-5.3 in the Cloudflare-hosted ChatGPT-style demo, which had only `LOCAL_INSTRUCTIONS`, answered "code mode vfs for me" by writing a 115-line in-memory VFS class instead of using the workspace. A session whose cells run on a hand (the browser tab) has no workspace of its own, so `createNanocode`'s `handInstructions` in `nanocode-ts/cloudflare` and the Vercel harness's go into its instructions only then, appended to `instructions`: the demos used to append the tab's `BASH_CODE_MODE_INSTRUCTIONS` to `instructions` itself, which sent a server-side session that paragraph twice, 573 more prompt tokens (12,736 instead of 12,124 for a new Luna session's first request in the Cloudflare demo, measured on 2026-10-24 with OpenAI's input token count). A caught overflow does not shrink later limits through stale stack state: on 2026-10-24 the stack_room answers ended correct after every overflow. The limit drops (to about 2,480 on Node 24, 1,740 on Node 26, 1,740 to 2,490 on workerd) once V8 tiers QuickJS's interpreter loop up from Liftoff to TurboFan, whose inlining makes each JS call use about 200 more bytes of native stack; this follows any hot recursion, not only an overflow, and Node 26 with --liftoff-only kept 2,775 after ten caught overflows. The limit stays adaptive rather than capped at the tiered depth, and the instructions tell the model the limit varies.

Bytes cross between cells and the host without base64 or JSON. With a prelude, the SDK engine gives it `__nanocodeBytes` (`crates/runtime/code-mode-engine/src/bytes.rs`), which the prelude takes off `globalThis`: `put` copies a string's UTF-8 or a typed array's bytes into a byte store keyed by a numeric ID, `take` returns a stored buffer as an `ArrayBuffer` without copying it, and `decode`, `encode`, `toBase64` and `fromBase64` are native, so the prelude's UTF-8 and base64 helpers (`TextDecoder`, `TextEncoder`, `Buffer`, `Body.text()`) no longer loop in JavaScript. `digest(name, buffer, offset, length)` and `random(length)` are native too: `crypto.subtle.digest` and HMAC hash with the SDK's SHA-1 and SHA-2, and `crypto.getRandomValues`, `randomUUID` and form boundaries take the platform's randomness instead of `Math.random`. Before (2026-10-28), the prelude hashed in JavaScript, which took 17 s for a 26 MB file in nanocode-swift's shell; with SDK e79097458 a cell hashes 26 MB in 1.4 s in Node. The lazy prelude modules (`crypto`, `web`, `extra`, `intl`) reach the prelude as JSON string literals in `lazySources`, which `cells.rs` writes: they used to be comments inside functions that `toString` read back, and once the SDK stripped source text from prelude bytecode (52c7498be, 2026-10-26) every lazy global in a cell (`URL`, `Blob`, `crypto`, `AbortController`, `Intl`) threw `SyntaxError: expecting ';'`, as the build-477ffb2 module the demos run did on 2026-10-29. Cells pass IDs in tool inputs: `fetch` sends a body as `body_ref` (a body under 64 KiB that is valid UTF-8 still goes as `body`), `cells.rs` sends its bytes with `dispatch_body`, and the host answers `cell_fetch` with one frame (the body, the JSON head, the head's length as a little-endian `u32`), which `bridge::call_framed` splits by truncating, and returns the body as `body_ref`. The `Response` keeps that ID until its body is read: `text()` and `bash.readFile` with UTF-8 decode the stored bytes straight into a string (`__nanocodeBytes.text`), `arrayBuffer()`, `bytes()`, `clone()` and `body` take them as an `ArrayBuffer`, and a `FinalizationRegistry` discards the bytes of a response collected unread, so a fetched text never sits in a buffer and a string at once. `bash.readFile`, `bash.writeFile` and `bash.appendFile` go through a host-only `file` tool (`op` `read`, `write` or `append`) and the bridge ops `cell_read` and `cell_write`, which call `WorkspaceHost.readBytes` and `writeBytes` (the simulated shell's filesystem, with parent directories created) or, for workspaces without them such as the sandboxes, `base64` in the workspace's shell on the host side. Before, a binary body or file went as base64 inside JSON through QuickJS, Rust and the host, a write through the shell's `stdin` (cut at 1,000,000 bytes) and a read in 600,000-byte `base64` pieces. With `output/bytes.mjs` on 2026-10-24 (Node, a mock provider, one cell that fetches N MiB, writes it with `bash.writeFile`, reads it back and checks it), the module's memory grew to 85, 651 and 419 MiB for 1, 8 and 32 MiB of binary data before (every write kept only 1,000,000 bytes, and 32 MiB did not finish in 300 s) and to 6.6, 20.6 and 68.6 MiB after, in 19, 64 and 149 ms; text took 27, 184 and 724 MiB before (reads were cut at 1,000,000 characters) and 8.8, 36.8 and 132.8 MiB after. In the Cloudflare demo under Miniflare (nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare with a cell harness like its `output/cells.mjs`), 8 MiB of binary data ran in 142 ms with 20.8 MiB of module memory and a 243 MiB workerd peak, where before the cell was still running after 60 s with 109 MiB of module memory, and 8 MiB of text ran in 168 ms with 36.9 MiB and a 263 MiB peak instead of 3.8 s and a 630 MiB peak. `TextDecoder` decoded 2 MB in 5 ms instead of 681. With the lazy body and native string reads (2026-10-24, same harness), text took 7.8, 28.8 and 100.8 MiB instead of 11.4, 47.5 and 167.5 MiB, in 21, 95 and 499 ms instead of 83, 544 and 2,027 ms, and binary 9.7, 23.7 and 71.7 MiB instead of 11.3, 25.3 and 73.3 MiB. Fetching 32 MiB of text and writing it took 69.8 MiB instead of 103.3 MiB. In Miniflare 8 MiB of text took 28.8 MiB of module memory instead of 47.6 MiB, and 32 MiB of text a 413 MiB workerd peak instead of 479 MiB. `wasm.run`, `wasm.invoke` and `WebAssembly.instantiate`/`compile` send a module's bytes the same way, as `module_ref` with `dispatch_body`, and `host.ts` hands them to `WorkspaceHost.wasm` as `module_bytes`; a string longer than 4,096 characters or containing NUL is rejected in the cell as not a path, with a hint to pass the path or read the file with `{ encoding: null }`. Before (2026-10-25), a module went as base64 in the `wasm` tool's input, which the journal and the events kept, and `bash.readFile` text passed as a module became a missing-path error that echoed the whole file: on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare both ended in a host crash for the 16 MB wasm-tools WASI release, while its path ran. In `output/stress.mjs` with the interpreter (Node, mock provider), running it from bytes wrote 21.4 MB of events and a 21.5 MB journal, and from `readFile` text 85 MB of events, a 92 MB journal and a 1.27 GB peak RSS, where both now write 0.02 MB of events and a 0.11 MB journal, as running from the path does.

Sessions get their provider's own web search tool, in the shape the SDK keeps from upstream, directly and in Code Mode cells, and both run the provider's real web search through the session's own `dispatch`, so the key, gateway or payment that runs the session's turns runs its searches: Claude agents get Claude Code's `WebSearch` (`query`, `allowed_domains`, `blocked_domains`), `nanocode::claude::web_search_tool`, a nested Messages request with Anthropic's `web_search_20250305` server tool on the calling agent's model; Codex agents get Codex's `web__run` (`web.run`'s `search_query`, `open`, `find` and other commands), `nanocode::codex_tools::web_search_tool`, a nested non-streaming Responses request to `<api base>/responses` with OpenAI's `web_search` tool on the calling agent's model (the commands' JSON as the text of one user message, `search_query` domains as `filters.allowed_domains`, the `openai/` model prefix behind the Vercel AI Gateway). A ChatGPT-plan Codex session (`subscription`) gets `nanocode::codex_tools::chatgpt_web_search_tool` instead, the same request streamed and without `max_tool_calls`, because chatgpt.com/backend-api/codex/responses refuses the API form: on 2026-11-08 every `web__run` call of gakonst/personal's gpt-6-astra plan sessions failed with 400 `Input must be a list` (the commands' JSON was the `input` string then), and the plan also answers 400 `Stream must be set to true` without `stream` and `Unsupported parameter: max_tool_calls`, while it accepts `max_output_tokens`; its `response.completed` carries an empty `output`, so the SDK builds the answer from the stream's `response.output_item.done` items (SDK 4179b40d7). Every session gets them. A Codex agent on another vendor's model (GLM, Kimi, MiMo) searches with gpt-6-luna, `web_search_tool`'s fallback (SDK bb3b395). A Workers AI session's chat gateway ignores its `endpoint`, so its `web__run` sends the nested request to `<endpoint>/responses` with provider `codex`, the route an OpenAI Codex session on that host takes: the Cloudflare adapter passes `https://api.openai.com/v1` for both, which `aiGateway` sends to OpenAI's Responses through the AI Gateway, so on 2026-10-25 a Codex GLM-5.3 session gained the `web__run` that Luna and Sol had; a Workers AI session with an empty `endpoint` gets none. Hosts do not intercept or answer them: the Cloudflare adapter's `aiGateway` sends the Messages request through `env.AI.run` and the Responses request through `gateway().run` like any turn, and the Vercel adapter through the AI Gateway. `config.web_search: false` (`webSearch: false` in `nanocode-ts/vercel`) turns them off, `{ claude: false }` or `{ codex: false }` one of them, and a host tool named `WebSearch` or `web__run` replaces the matching one (`web_search` both). Anthropic bills about $10 per 1,000 searches plus the nested request's tokens; OpenAI bills its `web_search` per call plus tokens.
