Neither Cloudflare nor Vercel offers a free general web search API, and keyless DuckDuckGo, Brave and Startpage scraping answers datacenter addresses with captchas or 429s.

The Cloudflare runtime waits for `storage.sync()` (`durable`) only before effects the session must have recorded first: a host tool call and a durable remote send (`request.durable`, which the SDK sets only for `Execute`, `Cancel` and brain callback results). Model requests and model WebSocket frames go out without it, because a model call has no side effect to protect and the SDK's durable writes before it are still made and confirmed in the background; a client still never sees unconfirmed state, since the object's output gate holds its responses. On the deployed bench Worker (2026-10-26, mock model, a `nanocode hand` on a Mac reaching SJC), a turn with one Code Mode cell went from p50 278-317 ms to 102-112 ms inside the object and from about 420 to 200-250 ms for the client. The WASM side interns SQL statements across the bridge: each `SqlStatement::sql` (a `&'static str`) is sent once as `[id, text]` and then as its id, and the host maps ids back before running the transaction (`statements` in `src/AgentRuntime.ts`).

A Cloudflare session can prime its model path when a turn arrives (2026-11-09). A new Durable Object runs in a new isolate, and an isolate's first request through the AI binding is slow whatever it asks: in a probe Worker in the demo's account (`output/cold-probe/` in nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare, one new object per line, a one-token Haiku 5.5 request through the demo's gateway) the first `AI.run` took 1.1 to 3.0 s and the next ones 0.42 to 0.75 s, a first OpenAI request through `gateway.run` 1.7 to 2.1 s against 0.65 to 0.9 s, a direct `fetch` of api.anthropic.com before it changed nothing (0.47 to 1.9 s after it), and the path stayed fast across 5 and 15 s of idleness and was slow again in one of two objects after 30 s. A request the provider refuses primes it as well as one it serves, for either provider: after an Anthropic request with `max_tokens: 0` (400 in 0.8 to 1.4 s, no tokens) a streamed Anthropic request took 0.53 to 0.74 s and an OpenAI one 0.64 to 0.85 s, and a real request sent 700 ms after that refused one had started took 0.44 to 0.88 s. So `MODEL_PRIME_IDLE_MS` (optional; unset keeps the old behavior) makes the session send `POST /prime` to its egress at the start of a turn's admission, before the module is instantiated, when no model request or prime left the object within that many milliseconds, for a session the deployment pays for on a model that is not on Workers AI; `Gateway.create`'s egress answers it with that refused request (`max_output_tokens: 0` for Codex), and other egresses' answers are ignored. It costs one subrequest and one refused entry in the gateway's log per primed turn, which is why a host opts in. On the deployed demo with 15000 (the longest idle time that stayed fast in both probed objects), un-warmed new Haiku 5.5 sessions over the public API (`output/first-turn/trace.mjs` there, batches of 8 alternating deploys without and with it) had a first model call of 2,435 ms at the median without it (16 sessions, 1,081 to 4,214) and 1,770 ms with it (24 sessions, 998 to 2,998), against 0.8 to 1.0 s for a second turn's; the turn's total moved less (medians of 4,836 and 3,827 ms without, 4,543, 3,471 and 3,953 ms with), because the time before the session answers varies more than the gain. The prime gets only the 0.1 to 0.8 s head start that opening the runtime takes, so the first call still waits for most of the isolate's setup; a session warmed before its turn (`/warm`) does not. Asking the session object to start while the Worker asks the plan vault (a `wake` request beside `subscribes`) was tried and dropped: 8 sessions showed no gain in the time to the turn's answer (1,815 ms at the median against 1,001 to 1,538). nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare's `npm run drill` passed with the variable unset and, in a scratch copy whose mock refuses `max_tokens: 0`, with it set (3 primes, one per turn).

Turn admission answers before the pump works (2026-10-30). The object's output gate holds an answer until its writes are durable, and an isolate does no I/O while JavaScript runs. Before, the pump that an admission starts ran at once, so a 202 waited for the pump's CPU work up to its first I/O. A pump that opened a runtime made the 202 wait for the whole open: on tempoxyz/gateway in production a new agent's first turn spent 333 ms in the session (p50) while its pump reopened the runtime for 256-303 ms. Now the direct-start pump waits one macrotask (`nextTask`) before it reads the queue, so the admission's commit starts first. A runtime that must open first waits for `storage.sync()` and one more macrotask, so the admission's answer leaves the object before the open. The admission still arms the watchdog alarm before it answers: in three runs that killed tempoxyz/gateway's local Worker right after a 202, each turn completed after the restart with `recovered: true`. That first turn reopened its runtime because the runtime key includes `web_search`: the gateway sends `web_search: false` with every turn, and the warm had no way to pass it. `Principal.recorded` (`recorded` in the descriptor) states that the host checked the session's record in this request, so a session's first request records the session without `recordSession`.

Measured on 2026-10-30 through tempoxyz/gateway's local Worker (`vp dev`, Apple M-series), with production costs added by a transform outside the repository: 250 ms of CPU at the start of each runtime open (the production reopen), 15 ms at the start of each `drive`, 10 ms each way between the Worker and the session, and 70 ms for each D1 query. The table gives the time from the start of the admission until the object can answer (its writes durable and its thread free), and the start of the first model request, p50 and p95 over 10 sessions each.

| Case | Answer before | Answer after | Model request before | Model request after |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| First turn, 3 s after the warm | 285, 353 ms | 26, 73 ms | 280, 337 ms | 21, 58 ms |
| Later turn | 22, 27 ms | 24, 27 ms | 17, 20 ms | 19, 21 ms |
| New session without a warm | 363, 406 ms | 6, 29 ms | 354, 397 ms | 286, 308 ms |
| First turn sent with the warm | 287, 310 ms | 28, 156 ms | 278, 304 ms | 22, 23 ms |

The later turn still waits for the 15 ms `drive` locally, because local storage confirms at once. In production the pump's CPU work runs while the commit replicates, so the answer waits for the longer of the two instead of their sum. Without the added costs, the gateway's `session` Server-Timing for a first turn went from 7 to 5 ms (p50) and for a later turn from 6 to 4 ms.

`connectHand(module, { url, workspace, cwd, fetch, name, WebSocket, transport, onStatus })` (`src/Hand.ts`) makes the calling JavaScript host, such as a visitor's browser tab, a hand: it instantiates the module without opening an agent (`AgentRuntime.serveHand`, the commands `hand_open`, `hand_frame` and `hand_closed`), dials the session's `/hands/<name>` WebSocket, publishes the SDK's `Catalog` frame and answers `Request` frames with the SDK's own hand core (`nanocode_code_mode::remote::HandCore`, the same one `nanocode hand` runs), which `src/wasm/hand.rs` serves over `LocalCells` (`HandHost` and `HandSession`; commands `hand_open` with a `runtime_id`, `hand_connect` per socket, `hand_frame`, `hand_closed`, and the host ops `hand_send` and `hand_failed` per connection): `Execute` and `Wait` run cells in the host with the same prelude (`bash` on the given workspace, `fetch` through `options.fetch`, `wasm` and `WebAssembly` where the shell allows them), progress goes back as `Update` frames, the session's own tools (subagents, host tools) are `Callback` frames answered by `CallbackResult`, and `Cancel`, `CancelTurn`, `DetachTurn`, `BeginTurn`, `Preempt` and `SettlePreempt` map onto the cells' controls. It is resumable like a native hand: its catalog carries a `runtime_id`, a dropped socket redials after `reconnectMs` (500 ms, doubling to 64 times that) while cells keep running and the core keeps their replies, callbacks wait for the next connection, and a reconnect answers `Resume` with the retained replies; a close with 1008 or `another hand attached under this name` ends it. It reports `attached` on the first frame it hears, not when its socket opens: brains send an empty `Ack` or `Resume` as soon as they take the hand (the SDK's `RemoteClient`), and the Cloudflare hand route, the Vercel hand socket and `HandRelay` send `HAND_ACCEPTED` (`{"type":"ack","ids":[]}`) when they accept it, so a refused hand goes from `connecting` to `closed` and `connectHand` rejects with the reason. `token` authenticates the dial: Node's `WebSocket` sends it as `Authorization: Bearer`, other hosts (browsers) as the `bearer` subprotocol (`bearerProtocols(token)`), which `bearerToken(request)` and `bearerAccepted(request)` (`src/Remote.ts`) read and answer on the Cloudflare hand routes, the Vercel hand socket and the relay, as Rust brains do. A browser cannot read the status of a refused upgrade, so these servers refuse a subprotocol credential after the upgrade instead: `refuseSocket(request, reason, status)` (`nanocode-ts/cloudflare`) answers a WebSocket request that offered `bearer` with a socket closed with 1008 and the reason, and JSON with the status otherwise, and the Vercel hand socket closes its upgraded socket the same way, so the hand stops instead of redialing. Before 2026-10-27 the module carried its own port of the native frame loop, which cancelled every session when its socket dropped and cached no brain tools; on 2026-10-27 in Node (nanocodexyz/nanocode `output/relay-test`) the WASM hand kept a 4 s cell running across a relay restart and returned its output 5.5 s after the turn started, and ran a cell for the Rust `brain` example (real Luna) with the token as the bearer subprotocol. So a durable server session keeps its turn log and receipts while its cells run in the tab, with the tab's native WebAssembly, Pyodide and paying fetch. On 2026-10-23 a Node process standing in for a tab (nanocode-cloudflare-durable `output/browser-hand.mjs`, Miniflare and a scripted model) attached as `browser` and a turn submitted with `hand: "browser"` ran bash on the tab's files, native `WebAssembly.instantiate` (42), the tab's fetch, a `tools.list_agents` callback and `store`/`load` across cells, and completed in 419 ms. The hand publishes the providers' workspace tools in its catalog as `nanocode hand` does (Codex's `apply_patch`, `view_image`, `exec_command` and `write_stdin`, Claude's `Read`, `Edit`, `Write`, `Bash`, `TaskOutput` and `TaskStop`), over the given workspace's files and shell, and the brain selects its backend's ones, so a model on a browser hand calls `tools.exec_command` or `tools.Bash` as on a machine; before 2026-10-27 the catalog had none and Luna's `exec_command` calls failed with `TOOL_NOT_AVAILABLE`. It also publishes tools of its own, which cells call as `tools.<name>` and which run in the host: `options.tools` (host tools answered by `tools.call`) and `options.mcpServers`, whose tools the SDK's MCP client lists and calls through `options.fetch`, so a tab's Mercator and gateway calls pay from its wallet (`mcp__gateway__kv_set`, ...). `handKeeper({ url, listed, module, workspace, ... })` keeps one hand per session for a page: `attach(session)` connects it, waits until `listed(session)` sees it, and answers the hand's name for the turn, and `nanocode-ts/cloudflare/transport`'s `hand(session)` option submits each turn with it. Vercel Functions cannot accept WebSockets, so `nanocode-ts/vercel` hands speak the same frames over the session's Workflow World streams (`src/vercel/Hands.ts`), with `connectHand`'s `transport: "stream"` and `url` the chat route with `?sessionId=<s>&hand=<channel>`. `createNanocode` stores the channel a session starts with (the start body's `hand`) in its config, and its `GET` and `POST` serve a request whose query names `hand` only when the session's config holds that channel (checked with `sessions.inspect` once per isolate), else 404. The tab POSTs `{connection, catalog}`, which appends an attach record to the stream `<s>_hand`, reads `<s>_hand_down_<connection>` as Server-Sent Events from the `cursor` it last saw (each response ends after `hands.holdMs`, 240 s by default, and the tab reconnects), and POSTs `{connection, brain, frames}`, appended as one line to `<s>_hand_up_<connection>`; `{connection, close: true}` (sent on `close()` and `pagehide`) appends a closed record. Each line is one chunk of JSON ending in a newline. Each drive's `dialHand` reads the last attach records, takes the newest open connection's catalog as the first frame, names itself with a random `brain` ID, writes its frames to the down stream tagged with it and reads the up stream from its tail, keeping only lines tagged with its ID, since the tab tags its frames with the newest brain it has heard; a later attach record closes it as "the hand reconnected" and a closed record for its connection as "the hand disconnected". With no open connection the drive runs its cells locally at once, without waiting. Otherwise the drive passes the socket to `RemoteExecutor.connect` (5 s), opens the runtime with `hands: true`, attaches the hand as `browser`, places prompts with it and waits for the journal before every dispatching frame. `createNanocode` turns hands on by default (`hands: false` turns them off); `hands: { relay(channel) }` dials the brain URL `relay` returns for a session's channel on a `HandRelay` (below), such as `wss://<relay>?role=brain&token=<channel>`, without `wait`, so a drive with no hand attached runs its cells locally at once; `connectHand`'s default WebSocket transport dials the same relay as the hand (`?token=<channel>`). `nanocode-ts/vercel/transport`'s `hand(session?)` option answers the channel: without a session for a new session's start body, and when a session has no ID yet the transport starts it with a warm start before the first prompt and calls `hand(id)`, so the tab attaches before the session's first drive dials. On 2026-10-24 `nanocode-vercel-durable` `scripts/vhand.mjs` (world-local behind a `node:http` server, a scripted model, a Node hand on `transport: "stream"`) ran bash, WebAssembly (42), the hand's fetch and tool, and store/load in the hand in two settled drives of one session (410 and 175 ms), a hand POST naming another channel answered 404, and with no hand attached the cells ran locally.

A cell running on such a hand routes its nested calls' `hand` as `nanocode hand` does: each `HandHost::open` gives the hand's `LocalCells` the brain's `HandCalls` (`route_hands`, named after the hand's `name`), so a call naming this hand drops `hand` and runs here, a call naming another hand goes to the brain as a cross-hand `Callback`, and a tool whose schema declares `hand` keeps it (SDK a4a5c27f8). Before 2026-11-01 the WASM hand routed nothing, so `hand` stayed in every input: gakonst/swarms' lead (on a gateway account hand served by `Hand.connect` over the in-memory shell, 2026-10-31) named its own hand on `exec_command`, which `tools.hands()` reported as `here`, and both cells failed until it left `hand` out, and a TS hand's cell could not reach another hand's tools. Checked on 2026-11-01 with `output/hand-routing.ts` (bench/handbench.ts's TS hand `bench` in Node, WASM brain, scripted Claude model): with the release build-f964a9d hand, `exec_command({ cmd, hand: 'bench' })` failed with `unknown field \`hand\``, `Bash` with `unsupported Bash option: hand` and a call naming `nowhere` with the same parse error; with this build they printed `routed-here` and `bash-here` and answered ``hand `nowhere` is not attached (attached hands: bench, local); nothing was dispatched``.

`prepareHand(module, options)` instantiates the module and runs `hand_open` (the workspace, the catalog and the MCP servers' `tools/list`) without a connection and returns `{ cwd, connect(url, onStatus), dispose() }`, so a page pays that cost before it knows the session; `connectHand` is `prepareHand` followed by `connect`. `handKeeper` takes `credential(session)` for hands whose token is per session, such as the Cloudflare runtime's minted `nch_` tokens, and passes it to `connect(url, onStatus, token)`; on 2026-10-27 a Node keeper with minted tokens and the bearer subprotocol attached to a production Cloudflare demo session in 3.5 s and a Haiku turn ran `Bash` on it (`Linux`, `/workspace`). It keeps one prepared spare: `prewarm()` compiles the module and prepares the spare with a workspace that forwards to the session's (`workspace(session)` is called on attach, and a `cwd` other than `options.cwd`, `/workspace` by default, prepares a new hand), and `prewarm(session)` attaches at once. `nanocode-ts/cloudflare/transport`'s `warm()` calls `hand(id)` with the session it warms, so the hand attaches while the visitor types. `compileModule(url, { cache, key })` compiles with `WebAssembly.compileStreaming` (or `compile` when the response is not `application/wasm`) from the Cache API entry `url` (plus `nanocode-key=<key>` when given; name the module's hash when its URL has none) in the cache `nanocode-wasm` (`cache` renames it, `false` skips it), stores a fetched response there and deletes every other entry of that cache, so one cache holds one module. The stream transport sends frames at most `batchMs` (20 ms) after the first one and never two POSTs at once, so a cell's update and reply go in one POST. Measured 2026-10-24 in headless Chrome 156 on the hosted Hand against the deployed backends, with the demos' pages built locally (nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare and nanocodexyz/durable-vercel branch `hand-fast`): before, the first message compiled the 12,694,775-byte module (served as 3,524,575 bytes of zstd on Cloudflare and 3,632,730 of brotli on Vercel, both `max-age=0, must-revalidate`; compile 0.17 to 0.47 s), listed the MCP servers (0.75 s) and opened the hand (Cloudflare WebSocket 0.44 s, Vercel catalog POST 0.25 s), so the turn started 1.3 to 2.6 s after Enter on Cloudflare and 2.1 to 4.2 s on Vercel when Enter followed the first keystroke at once; with `prewarm()` on page load it started 0.55 to 0.73 and 0.69 to 0.85 s after Enter, and at once when the prompt was typed at 40 ms a key. Enter to the cell's output on the page with typing took 2.6 and 2.7 s instead of 5.3 and 5.3 s on Cloudflare (Codex model latency dominates the rest). A hand-only module (only `hand_open`, `hand_frame` and `hand_closed` in the command table, same profile, built in a scratch copy) measured 2,348,306 bytes (786,509 gzip, 581,511 brotli) against 12,690,346 (3,849,789 and 2,477,262), and Node 24 compiled it in 10.6 ms lazily against 51.7 ms; QuickJS and libc are 32% of its code and no agent, provider or durability code remains. Revisits in one profile compiled in 69 to 97 ms from the Cache API and 90 to 132 ms over a revalidated local HTTP response.

`runtimeId` fixes the runtime ID a hand's catalog advertises (a fresh UUID by default) for a host that restarts its hand and resumes the operations the hand had in flight itself, such as tempoxyz/gateway's machine hands (2026-10-30): with the same ID, brains and relays take their resume path instead of failing those operations as a restarted hand's, so a host that sets it must answer the resume for what it had received, because a fresh hand answers every ID it never saw as not received. `Hand.connect` and `Hand.prepare` take `environment` (`Contracts.HandEnvironment`: `platform`, `os_version` and `shell`; `{ shell: 'bash' }` by default, the just-bash shell), `capabilities` (`Contracts.HandCapability`: `name`, `description`, the `tools` it gates, `permission()` and an optional `request(reason)`, which defaults to `permission`) and `toolsOnly`, which the hand's catalog publishes (`hand_open`'s `environment`, `capabilities` and `tools_only`; the host ops `hand_capabilities` and `hand_request_capability` answer the brain's `Capabilities` and `RequestCapability` operations). A brain shows such a hand under `about` in `tools.hands()` and asks its user for a capability with `tools.hands({ request: { hand, capability, reason } })`. A tools-only hand runs no cells: a cell elsewhere calls its tools with `hand`, including tools the cell's own catalog lacks, so a Worker brain keeps its just-bash tools and also reaches a tab's or a phone's. `Capabilities.browser(names?)` (`nanocode-ts/client`) is a tab's pack, each capability only where the browser has its API: `location` (`navigator.geolocation`, its permission read from `navigator.permissions`), `notifications` (`notify`) and `clipboard` (`clipboard_read` behind the permission, `clipboard_write` without one), as `{ tools, capabilities }` for the hand's options; `Capabilities.merge(...packs)` joins packs and a hand's own tools. On 2026-10-28 a headless Chrome tab (Playwright) attached as a tools-only hand with it to `nanocode exec --runner-mode replace`: with geolocation granted, Opus 5.5 called the tab's `location` and answered Lisbon; with it not granted, `permissions.query` reported `prompt`, the model asked with its reason, Chrome denied the prompt and the model reported that without retrying or guessing; headless Chrome reports notifications and clipboard reads as denied. On 2026-10-28 one cell of a WASM brain with `LocalCells` and a tools-only Node hand named phone (`location` gating `where_am_i`, permission prompt) listed the hands, requested the permission, which the hand logged, called `where_am_i({ hand: 'phone' })` on the phone and `Bash` on its own shell; the remote brain keeps the hand's description through `RemoteCodeModeHost::described`. A new hand against the deployed site, Vercel and machines brains, which ignore the new catalog fields, ran its turns as before.

`pricing` makes a hand paid (2026-10-31): `Hand.connect(module, { ..., pricing: { payee, describe, currency, price(operation) } })` prices each `Call`, `Execute` and `Wait` request before the module receives it, with no change to the module. `Hand.Operation` is the request as it arrives: `kind` (the tool name, `execute` or `wait`), `access` (`read`, `write`, `run`, `cell` or `tool`), `input`, `command` (`cmd` or `command`), `paths` (the input's `path`, `file_path`, `dir_path`, `notebook_path` and `paths`, the files of an `apply_patch` patch and the words of the command after its first, relative to `cwd` when under it), `source` for a cell, `session`, and the `agent` and `max` that the brain stamps (`agent`, `max_usd`). `price` returns USD or `undefined` (free), and a throw refuses the request with its message. The protocol additions, which brains and hands that do not know them ignore:

- The catalog carries `pricing: { payee, describe, currency }`, and the hand adds a `pricing` capability (permission `granted`) whose description starts with `describe`, so `tools.hands()` shows the prices. `currency` (`ousd`, the default when absent, or `usdce`, added 2026-10-31) names the token the payee is paid in: tempoxyz/gateway's hub has agents pay the payee directly over MPP session channels in that token, and MACH cannot be offered because it reaches only whitelisted merchants. Owners still need only an address.
- A priced request runs only when it carries `max_usd` and the price is at most that. Otherwise the hand answers an Err reply (`this operation costs $0.01, more than the $0.005 this request allows; nothing ran`, or `… the brain that sent it does not bill hand charges; nothing ran`) and the module never receives it.
- The hand appends `"charge": { "usd", "item" }` to the request's `update` frames and to an Ok reply, never to an Err reply. A resumable request keeps its quote until an `ack` names it, so a reply the module sends again on resume carries the charge again. tempoxyz/gateway's account hub holds such frames until it has charged the agent.

Path prices are exact only for a tools-only hand: a hand that runs cells receives an `Execute`, whose nested calls run inside the module and are not priced one by one. A command's paths are its words, so `cd data; cat secret.txt` names no file under `data/`, and a hook that protects files prices or refuses commands it cannot attribute. Checked on 2026-10-31 through tempoxyz/gateway's local Worker against Tempo mainnet (its AGENTS.md has the runs): a tools-only Node hand on `nanocode-ts/bash` charged $0.01 once for `cat data/secret.txt` and nothing for `cat notes.txt`, refused reads over a $0.005 `max_usd` before running them, and returned nothing of a $0.20 read whose payment failed.

MCP servers are deferred by default (`src/Mcp.ts`'s `deferMcp`, which `AgentRuntime.open` and `serveHand` wrap around `dispatch` for the configured `mcp_servers`, so every host gets it). It answers `initialize` and `tools/list` from a per-isolate cache keyed by URL and the request's credential headers for `RuntimeOptions.mcp.cacheMs` (10 minutes), fetches the list after `initialize` answers on a miss, answers `notifications/initialized` locally once cached, and passes a server that returns `mcp-session-id` straight through. The per-isolate cache holds only settled values, the `initialize` result and the tool list, and a `tools/list` still in flight is shared only within the runtime that started it (`pending` in `defer`), because a Worker isolate runs many Durable Objects and requests, and a request that awaits a fetch another request started can hang once that request ends (2026-11-07). With `mcp.deferred` (true unless a host passes false, as `createNanocode({ mcp: { deferred } })` does on Cloudflare and Vercel), `tools/list` gives each tool only its first sentences and its argument names and types, plus a tool `describe_tools({ names })` that the layer answers locally with the full descriptions and input schemas; `tools/call` of any other tool goes to the server unchanged, so payments and metering do not change. Measured on 2026-10-24: Mercator's and the gateway's lists went from 90 KB to 15 KB, a Cloudflare demo session's first model request from 73 KB to 46 KB (its tools from 67 KB to 39 KB, 37% less spend per Haiku turn), and a new session's runtime open in a warm local isolate from 180-560 ms to 75-130 ms; a cold isolate pays one `initialize` and one `tools/list` per server in parallel (0.3-1 s, Mercator's `initialize` alone took 0.4-1.6 s from the hosted Hand). `RuntimeOptions.mcp.store` (`Contracts.McpStore`: `get(key)` and `put(key, value, ttlMs)`) shares those listings beyond one isolate: a miss in memory reads the store under `nanocode-mcp:` and the SHA-256 of the cache key, so credential headers never appear in a key, before it asks the server, and a listing fetched from a server is put there with its fetch time, which a reader checks against `cacheMs` and `staleMs`. On Cloudflare `App.create({ mcp: { store: env => Listings.store(env.MCP_LISTINGS) } })` backs it with one `McpListings` Durable Object per deployment (`Listings.ts`, exported flat for its binding), which needs no external resource, unlike a KV namespace, so the same configuration deploys to any account. New sessions there land in other isolates, so before this a new session's first turn spent 3.1-3.2 s opening its runtime without a warm (2026-10-28, nanocode-cloudflare-durable.tempo-dev.workers.dev, two sessions: four MCP requests to mercator.sh and gateway.tempo-dev.workers.dev during the open, against none and 0 ms on each session's second turn); in Node with the real servers and a file standing in for the store, a fresh process opened in 1,575 ms with four MCP requests without a stored listing and in 58-60 ms with none once one was stored. `POST sessions/<s>/warm` opens the session's runtime with those listings and preconnects it, also for a session with no turn yet: that runtime opens under a session ID held in memory, which the first turn takes when its provider, model and executor match the warm's, and a resident runtime is reused only for the session ID, provider, model, executor and `web_search` it opened with, so the warm body takes `web_search` as a turn body does. Before 2026-10-25 the route only instantiated the module, so on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare/chatgpt a new session's first turn spent 0.9-2.8 s (0.3-0.4 s 3 s after a warm) opening the runtime between `queued` and `running`; in Miniflare with a fresh isolate per round, MCP servers answering in 150 ms and a warm 1.5 s before the turn, its result came 382 ms after the POST instead of 791 ms, and 379 instead of 796 ms for a restarted session with history. With `mcp.core` (`{ [server name]: tool names }`, which `createNanocode({ mcp: { core } })` on Cloudflare and Vercel, `AgentRuntime.open`'s `mcp` option and `handKeeper({ mcp })` pass on), `tools/list` gives only the named tools of that server in full, plus `describe_tools` and `call_tool({ name, arguments })`, whose description names every other tool with a few words of its description; the layer rewrites a `call_tool` call into that tool's own `tools/call` before `dispatch`, so wallets, payments and metering see the real tool name. `nanocode-ts/machines`' `Mcp.core` lists no tool of either server in full, so each offers only `describe_tools` and `call_tool`, and the demos pass it: since nanocodexyz/nanocode#1082 a cell's direct `tools.mcp__<server>__<tool>` call for a deferred tool runs through that server's `call_tool`. On 2026-10-25 the Cloudflare demo's first model request lost 1,372 o200k tokens with each provider (Claude-shaped 9,380 to 8,008, Codex 10,108 to 8,736; about 15.8k to 13.5k Claude tokens per call), and without a connected Tempo Wallet every MCP call answers `tempo_wallet_required`, so most visitors' sessions never use these tools. compute_terminate is listed so a model that creates a sandbox can end it and get its unused lease refunded: on 2026-10-24, before it was, Luna in a `createMachines` cloud agent created a sandbox and then called `tools.mcp__gateway__compute_terminate` directly, and the cell failed with TOOL_NOT_AVAILABLE, leaving the sandbox to run out its paid lease. Measured on 2026-10-24 with tiktoken o200k on the local Cloudflare demo's first model request ("Say hi in 3 words."): Codex went from 13,551 to 11,637 model-visible tokens and Claude from 11,723 to 9,800; Luna and gpt-5.4-mini both found `call_tool` unprompted for "List my running gateway compute sandboxes" (describe_tools, then call_tool compute_list).

An MCP listing older than `cacheMs` is served while it revalidates (2026-11-09). `defer` answers `initialize` and `tools/list` from a listing in memory or in `mcp.store` that is at most `cacheMs` plus `RuntimeOptions.mcp.staleMs` old (one day by default). Past `cacheMs` it starts one background `initialize` and `tools/list` per listing and isolate, which replaces the listing in memory and in the store. An older listing is fetched before the answer, as before, and the store keeps each listing for the whole bound. Before, a listing expired after 10 minutes and the next runtime open waited for both requests of every server. On gakonst/personal's production from 2026-11-06 to 2026-11-09, outside the incident below, 138 of 283 turns that opened a runtime waited for them: `runtime_open_ms` p50 3,578 ms and p90 5,412 ms, against 616 and 817 ms with a cached listing, and the first model request p50 4,503 ms against 1,232 ms. A replay of that window's 316 runtime opens gave 141 opens that wait with a 10-minute listing and 1 with a day of revalidation. The longest gap between two opens was 350 minutes (p99 119 minutes), which is the source of the one-day default. `output/mcp-swr.ts` ran `defer` against the real Mercator and gateway servers from this Mac, with a map as the store and a shifted clock:

| Listing | Open | Requests before the answer |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| none | 470 ms | `initialize` and `tools/list` of both servers |
| fresh, in memory | 4 ms | none |
| 11 minutes old, in memory | 3 ms | none, revalidated after the answer |
| 12 minutes old, in the store of a new isolate | 13 ms | none, revalidated after the answer |
| 3 days old | 277 ms | `initialize` and `tools/list` of both servers |

An MCP server that cannot start no longer fails the runtime's open (2026-11-09). `src/wasm/mcp.rs` keeps the tools of the servers that answered `initialize` and `tools/list`, logs each failure as `MCP server <name>: <error>` and returns the failures, which `init` answers as `mcp_unavailable` and `AgentRuntime.mcpUnavailable` lists. A hand's open leaves such a server out the same way. On Cloudflare the session logs them and retires that runtime once it is idle, so the next turn opens a new runtime and asks the server again. A configuration error still fails the open. Before, one failed server failed the open, and the turn backed off: on 2026-11-09 a wallet change (a68090e) failed the paid servers' free `initialize`, so every runtime of gakonst/personal failed to open for 4 h 20 min. `output/mcp-unavailable.ts` opened a runtime with two servers of a local mock, `good` and `bad` (503). build-28eeea8's module failed the open in 34 ms with `MCP server bad: MCP initialize failed: ... HTTP 503`, and this tree opened in 58 ms with `good`'s tools and that failure in `mcpUnavailable`.

An MCP server's tool timeout is the host's (2026-11-08): `Contracts.McpServer` takes `tool_timeout_ms`, which `src/wasm/mcp.rs` passes to the SDK's `McpServer::tool_timeout`, so `App.create({ mcp: { servers } })` on Cloudflare, the Vercel harness, `AgentRuntime.open`'s `mcp_servers` and `connectHand`'s `mcpServers` all take it; without it a call still fails after the SDK's 300 s (`DEFAULT_TOOL_TIMEOUT` in nanocode's `crates/integrations/mcp-tools/src/config.rs`), and 0 fails the runtime's open with the SDK's `ZeroTimeout`. tempoxyz/gateway's own tools run longer than five minutes (`compute_stream` up to an hour, and a paid call that waits up to ten minutes for a busy agent payer), and on 2026-11-07 a gateway agent's cell failed with `MCP tool gateway/kv_set exceeded 300.0 seconds` while the gateway went on and wrote the value at 433 s. When the SDK gives up on a call it cancels the host's request (next paragraph). Checked in Node with `output/mcp-timeout.mjs` (a scripted Codex or Claude model whose cell calls a local streamable-HTTP MCP server that answers `tools/call` after `SLEEP_MS` with an event stream kept open by comments, `AgentRuntime.open` with `mcp_servers: [{ name: 'slow', url, tool_timeout_ms }]`): without `tool_timeout_ms` a 310 s call failed at 300.1 s with `exceeded 300.0 seconds` (Codex and Claude) and the server saw the caller leave at 301.1 s; with `tool_timeout_ms: 2000` a 5 s call failed at 2.0 s with `exceeded 2.0 seconds`; with `tool_timeout_ms: 400000` the 310 s call answered `slept 310000` at 310.1 s with both harnesses. A host running the runtime in Node needs a fetch that waits longer than undici's default 300 s `headersTimeout` too: the first 400 s run, whose server sent its headers only with the result, failed at 301 s with `Transport send error: … fetch failed`; Workers' fetch has no such bound.

An MCP tool call whose caller stops waiting is cancelled at the host (2026-11-08, nanocode 231b4198c): a turn cancel, a subagent interrupt or close, and the server's tool timeout abort the `signal` the host's `dispatch` got for that `tools/call`, the server sees its connection close, and it also gets `notifications/cancelled` for the request. The SDK already sent the notification when the call's future was dropped, but its streamable-HTTP driver kept the request's POST running to the end, so the host fetch's signal never fired until the runtime was disposed, and a stateless server, which cannot tie a notification on another connection to the request, ran the call to the end (tempoxyz/gateway paid and wrote a cancelled `kv_set` 80 s after its turn was cancelled). The driver now aborts a request's POST when it sends that request's `notifications/cancelled`; and since a server that streams its answer sends its headers first, `http_start` here keeps an MCP request's `AbortController` (provider `null`, never a model call's, whose early body close the gateway meters) with its body, and `http_close` before the end of the body, a cancelled `http_read` and `dispose` abort it. Checked in Node with `output/mcp-cancel.mjs` (a mock MCP server whose `tools/call` sleeps 60 s and logs a closed connection and `notifications/cancelled`; a scripted Codex or Claude model whose cell, or whose subagent's cell, calls it; `MODE` `timeout` with `tool_timeout_ms` 4000, `cancel` at 4 s, `interrupt` or `close` of subagent 1 at 5 s; `SSE=1` for an event-stream answer): on build-a8c7606 (SDK 9a695d182) every mode got the notification but kept the request open and never aborted the host signal; on this build every mode, with both harnesses and with JSON and event-stream answers, aborted the host signal and closed the server's connection within 0.1 s of the cancel. With the SDK change and without the `http_close` abort, an event-stream answer closed the connection but left the signal unfired. `RESTART=1` (dispose right after the cancel or interrupt and reopen the session from the same SQLite; `MESSAGE=1` also messages the interrupted subagent) sent no `io.nanocode/resume` call on either build: a cancelled turn's or interrupted subagent's cells are retired and their journals forgotten, so their running calls are never resumed. On production (tempoxyz/gateway 01719b2, version d8dc3935, pinning build-54713a9), a subagent interrupted and a turn cancelled while their gateway `kv_set` waited for a payer that was down were never paid or run; the gateway's AGENTS.md has the drill.

The module builds against nanocode 305ff23d4 (2026-11-09): after f4522379b, the Claude subscription wire claims Claude Code 2.1.304 instead of 2.1.283, since a Claude plan serves Fable 5.2 only to 2.1.290 and later, and the ChatGPT extension relays a host's authorization beside a step's input (which the bridge does not use). `Cargo.lock` stayed the same; `node scripts/build.mjs` (incremental, 37 s), `npm run build:ts`, `npm run check:types` and the WASI SDK `cargo clippy` passed, and `node output/fable-plan.ts` (a `subscription` Claude session on `fable` over a canned-stream `dispatch`) sent `claude-fable-5-2` with `user-agent: claude-cli/2.1.304 (external, cli)` and `cc_version=2.1.304.<fingerprint>` in its billing block. Before that it built against nanocode f4522379b (2026-11-09): after 4179b40d7, Claude workload identity federation (the caller's OIDC identity tokens exchanged for Claude access tokens, which the bridge does not use) and Claude Fable 5.2 and Claude Mythos 5.2 (`claude-fable-5-2` and `claude-mythos-5-2`, which the `fable` and `mythos` aliases now name; Opus 5.5 stays the Claude default here as in the SDK). `Cargo.lock` stayed the same; `node scripts/build.mjs` (incremental, 59 s), `npm run build:ts`, `npm run check:types` and `cargo clippy --locked --lib --target wasm32-unknown-unknown` (with `scripts/build.mjs`'s WASI SDK environment) passed. `node output/fable-5-2.ts` (a Claude session per model in Node over a `dispatch` that answers with a canned stream) sent `fable` and `claude-fable-5-2` as `claude-fable-5-2` and `mythos` as `claude-mythos-5-2`, each with `max_tokens` 128000 and adaptive thinking with `block_binding`, and `mythos` on the fast tier with `speed: "fast"` and the fast mode beta. Before that it built against nanocode 4179b40d7 (2026-11-08): after 58f8bf4ad, nanocode-sync's `Backoff::endless` and one `Duplex`, a bounded child process in nanocode-process and a sandbox budget in nanocode-sandbox (new APIs the bridge does not call), and Codex web search on the ChatGPT plan (above), which `open` picks for a Codex endpoint with `subscription`. `Cargo.lock` stayed the same; `node scripts/build.mjs` (incremental, 40 s), `npm run build:ts`, `npm run check:types` and `cargo clippy --locked --lib --target wasm32-unknown-unknown` passed. The SDK's own harness (`output/web-search-plan` in nanocodexyz/nanocode: the tool over a native `HostTransport` with the local Codex CLI login's access token) answered `web__run` searches through chatgpt.com/backend-api/codex with gpt-6-astra and gpt-6-luna, and the old string `input` got the same 400 there. `node output/websearch-plan.ts` (a Codex session with `subscription` in Node, a scripted socket model whose cell calls `tools.web__run`, and a `dispatch` that sends the search to chatgpt.com with that access token) on the published `build-28eeea8` module got a 200 streamed answer with its sources, and on `build-9254e52` the cell failed with `web search failed: WebSocket handshake was rejected with HTTP 400: {"detail":"Input must be a list"}`, the production error. Before that it built against nanocode 58f8bf4ad (2026-11-08), the SDK's master after 231b4198c. Its commits since then: a native-only Code Mode cell guard (`CellGuard`, `Tools::builder().cell_guard`, compiled out of `wasm32`); Claude effort changes mid-conversation as effort-only system messages, so the prompt cache survives them (Opus, Sonnet and Haiku 5.5, Fable and Mythos 5.1); with live deltas the root agent's reasoning deltas are live-only and its reasoning is logged once per block, as subagents' already were; branch exports with only a selected checkpoint and the blobs they name; a session list that skips a session's `<session>:cells` cell journal state (58f8bf4ad, which a native Code Mode cell made `SessionCatalog::list` fail on); a Postgres store change. No bridge code changed and `Cargo.lock` stayed the same. `node scripts/build.mjs` built it with the SDK checkout at 58f8bf4ad, `npm run build:ts` and `npm run check:types` passed, and gakonst/personal's Worker on the packed tarball (`wrangler dev`, AI Gateway) ran a Claude Haiku 5.5 and a GPT-6 Luna turn whose cells called four host tools each, and a GPT-6 Luna turn whose `spawn_agent` started a Claude Haiku 5.5 child that answered through `wait_agent`.

The module builds against nanocode 85d570598 (2026-11-09), whose `serde_json` parses numbers to the exact double (`float_roundtrip`). On build-07238e5 a cell journal read back from SQL gave a replayed script another `Math.random()` for about one double in ten (105,566 of 1,000,000 random doubles change in a best-effort JSON round trip), so a cell that put the number into a string argument diverged at that call after a restart and ended as `Script failed`, and a tool received `0.09596104461529852` for a cell's `0.09596104461529853`. `output/blog/replay.mjs` runs a cell that reads `Math.random()` and `Date.now()`, calls a host tool, sleeps 4 s and calls it again, kills its process 1.5 s into the sleep and finishes the turn in a second process on the same `node:sqlite` file: the second process opened 38 ms after the kill and finished 4,044 ms after the first call, with the same number in both ledger lines. `output/blog/replay-id.mjs` passes `"job-" + Math.random()` to the tool instead, and `output/blog/trials.sh` runs it 5 at a time: 6 of 80 cells failed on build-07238e5's module and 0 of 60 on this one, where `output/blog/cell-once.mjs` also delivered `0.09596104461529853` unchanged. `scripts/measure-node.ts` over five alternating runs showed no difference beyond its spread (a Claude open in 33 to 50 ms against 32 to 37 ms, a warm turn in 16 to 19 ms against 17 to 20 ms), and the module is 12,143,123 bytes against 12,110,113. The revision also carries the CLI's commits since 305ff23d4, which this package does not build.

Code Mode sessions on Cloudflare pass `brief_tools` (`createNanocode({ briefTools })`, default `send_agent_message`, `list_agents`, `close_agent` and `interrupt_agent`) to the SDK's `brief_code_tools`, which lists them as one line each in `exec` instead of in full: 432 o200k tokens off every request (Claude-shaped 8,008 to 7,576, Codex 8,736 to 8,304). `spawn_agent` and `wait_agent` stay in full because models need `spawn_agent`'s `output_contract` type: a local GLM-5.3 sweep (12 prompts, 5 with subagents, model calls relayed to Workers AI from gak-9) on 2026-10-25 passed 12 of 12 on 2ba5f5b, 11 of 12 with all six briefed but with 8 to 15 calls and many failed `spawn_agent` calls per subagent turn, and 11 and 10 of 12 with only these four briefed, whose misses were GLM reversing "nanocode" wrong (as on the live demo) and Workers AI 429s on subagent calls. Since nanocodexyz/nanocode's compact embedded `exec` preamble (2026-10-25), every WASM host's first request is 773 o200k tokens smaller again (Claude-shaped 6,803, Codex 7,531).

Cost and quality on the Cloudflare demo, measured live with the same 36-prompt sweep (12 prompts each on Haiku 5.5, gpt-6.1-sol and GLM-5.3, 5 with subagents; ~/w/sweep on the workstream's hosted Hand) on 2026-10-25, from nanocode-ts 7355c4a to 73d85e6: input tokens per model call fell from 16,422 to 11,902 for Haiku (-28%), 10,331 to 7,886 for Sol (-24%) and 11,259 to 8,303 for GLM-5.3 (-26%); GLM-5.3 uncached input per turn fell from 14,309 to 2,600 (cached share 65% to 91.5%, from `x-session-affinity`); session-log rows per turn of 3 cells and a reply fell 45% (live deltas). Pass rate went from 35 of 36 (GLM-5.3 failed any turn whose cell called `notify()`) to 35 of 36 with Haiku and Sol at 12 of 12 and the only miss GLM-5.3 reversing "nanocode" wrong. The prefix cuts were MCP tools behind `call_tool` (#131), briefed subagent tools (#132) and a compact `exec` preamble (#133); further prefix cuts would be small, since what remains is upstream Claude tool text, `spawn_agent`/`wait_agent` and the demo's own instructions.

`nanocode-ts/bash` (`src/bash/`) is a simulated bash for agents, written here from scratch in the spirit of vercel-labs/just-bash and not depending on it: a parser and interpreter over a virtual filesystem with no processes, so it runs wherever the host does, Workers included, since it generates no code from strings. `await Bash.create({ files, fs, env, cwd, fetch, limits, commands })` makes a shell whose `exec(script, { stdin, env, cwd, signal })` answers `{ stdout, stderr, exitCode, truncated }`; every `exec` starts from fresh variables, functions and working directory (`/home/user` by default) while the filesystem persists, and `readFile`, `writeFile` and `fs` reach it from host code. `fs` is a `FileSystem` (async `stat`, `read`, `write` with append, `list`, `mkdir`, `remove`, `rename` over absolute paths), `MemoryFileSystem` by default, so a host can back it with the Tempo gateway's bucket or kv. `src/bash/internal/Syntax.ts` parses words, quoting, `$'...'`, parameters with `${...}` operators, `$(...)` and backticks, `$((...))`, pipes, `&& || ; &`, redirections (`> >> < 2> 2>&1 &> <<<` and `<<` or `<<-` heredocs), `if`, `for` in both forms, `while`, `until`, `case`, groups, subshells, `(( ))`, `[[ ]]` and functions; `src/bash/internal/Shell.ts` runs them with brace, tilde, parameter, command, arithmetic, field-splitting and glob expansion, indexed arrays, `local`, `set -euxo pipefail` and the builtins (`cd`, `export`, `read`, `mapfile`, `eval`, `source`, `test`, `printf -v`, `bash -c`, ...), and `src/bash/internal/commands/` holds the commands: file tools, text tools, a `sed` with addresses, ranges, hold space and branches, an `awk` with its own lexer and parser (patterns, ranges, arrays, user functions, `printf`, `getline`, output redirection), a `jq` with generators, path expressions and assignment, and `curl`, which calls the host's `fetch` (no network without one), so a host that passes mppx's paying fetch lets scripts pay MPP services. `BASH_COMMANDS` lists them and `commands` adds or replaces one with a `CommandHandler`. `limits` (`DEFAULT_LIMITS` when the host passes none) bounds commands per call, iterations per loop, call and substitution depth, captured output, pipe and file buffers, filesystem operations (`maxFileOperations`, 1,000,000 stats, lists, reads and writes per call, which bounds `grep -r`, `find` and globs over a large or remote tree as nanocodexyz/nanocode's search admission did before its 2026-10-14 refactor; it was 100,000 until 2026-10-25, when GPT-6 Luna on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare/chatgpt, creating 10,000 files and then running `find`, `grep -r` and `du` over them in one call, ran out of it at `du`) and wall time; a limit ends the call with a message and exit 126, a timeout or the caller's `signal` with 124. `observe` receives a `BashEvent` when each `exec` (or a `tool()` call with invalid input) starts and finishes, after nanocodexyz/nanocode's `docs/just-bash-observability.md` at 1e0bd25f8: the call's sequence number, `command` (the script's first word when it names a command or builtin, else `other`), and on `finished` `status`, `exitCode`, `durationMs`, `outputTruncated` and a `category` from the bounded `BashCategory` (`none`, `search_admission`, `resource_limit`, `timeout`, `cancelled`, `syntax`, `command_not_found`, `command_exit`, `input_validation`, `exception`); no script text, arguments, paths, environment or output, so a host can log every call, and an observer that throws or rejects cannot change a result. There is no `queued` phase because calls are not serialized. On 2026-10-22 a scratch Node script produced every category but `exception` from its trigger, and `grep -rc` over 20,000 files took 56 ms with the budget and 59 ms without it. `shell.tool()` gives a host tool (`bashTool()`: `bash` with `{ command }`) and its `call`, whose text is stdout, `[stderr]` and `[exit code N]` cut to 30,000 characters; `config.workspace: shell.cwd` with `options.workspace: shell.workspace()` makes `bash` a Code Mode global: a cell calls `await bash("...")` or the tagged template, which joins its raw strings with only `\\`, `\`` and `\$` unescaped (so `\(` and `\\(` both reach bash as `\(`, and `\n` reaches awk or printf as `\n`) and quotes interpolated values, and `bash.readFile(path)` and `bash.writeFile(path, text)`, each through the nested `bash` tool and `shell_run` with an optional `stdin`; `BASH_CODE_MODE_INSTRUCTIONS` tells the model, for the session's instructions. `bash` also takes the shapes models write for other shells: `bash("...", { cwd, stdin, env, timeout })`, `bash({ command, cwd, stdin, env, timeout })` (or `cmd`, `script`, `workdir`, `timeout_ms`) and an argv array, which it quotes; its result keeps `{ stdout, stderr, exitCode }` and adds a non-enumerable `output` and `toString()` of stdout and stderr; and when Codex's workspace tools are present the prelude binds `exec_command` (a string becomes `{ cmd }`), `write_stdin` and `apply_patch` as globals beside `tools.*`. Before 2026-10-24 `bash({ command })` threw `TypeError: not a function` and the cooked template made `find . \( -name x \)` a bash syntax error, which cost Claude Haiku a cell on nanocode.xyz/vercel; one probe cell running every shape now passes in Codex and Claude cells on both demos' builds. `git apply [-R] [--check] [-pN] [PATCH...]` (stdin without a file) runs the shell's `patch` with `-p1 -f -s -d <cwd>` (`-p0` when the diff's names carry no `a/` or `b/` prefix and no directory), so a `git diff` or a model's unified diff applies to the working tree; before 2026-10-25 it was unsupported. `timeout DURATION COMMAND` runs the command in a forked runner with its own abort signal and answers 124 when the duration passes (before 2026-10-25 the builtin ignored the duration, so `timeout 1 sleep 5` slept 5 s and answered 0). `src/bash/internal/commands/system.ts` adds `dd` (`if`, `of`, `bs`, `count`, `skip`, `seek`, `conv=notrunc`, `status=none`; reading a device needs `count`), `nohup`, `printenv`, `less` and `more` (as `cat`), `sponge`, `uuidgen`, `factor`, `base32` and placeholder `df`, `free` and `ps`, because models reach for `dd if=/dev/urandom of=FILE bs=1M count=N` first and GPT-6 Luna spent cells on `dd: command not found`. `/dev/urandom` and `/dev/zero` read through `head -c` stream in 1 MiB slices built with `String.fromCharCode` over 8 KiB chunks: before 2026-10-25 `device()` built the whole size one character at a time and `head -c` rendered it in one piece, so `head -c 20000000 /dev/urandom > big.bin` peaked at 270 MB of Node heap and crashed the nanocode.xyz/cloudflare Durable Object twice (Haiku 5.5 and GPT-6 Luna: `Tool execution interrupted by a host crash`); it now peaks at about 49 MB. The workspace's `read` (the providers' Read, Edit and apply_patch tools, which read a whole file into the module) refuses a file larger than a sixth of `maxMemoryBytes` (8 MiB on Cloudflare's 48 MiB) with a message pointing at `head -c`, `sed -n`, `grep` and `split`, since reading a 20 MB binary that way peaked at 174 MB in Node before it reached the module. On 2026-10-22, 388 scripts covering quoting, expansions, control flow, redirections, sed, awk, jq, sort, grep and find gave the same stdout and exit status in this shell as in GNU bash 5.2.21 with GNU coreutils, gawk 5.2.1 and jq 1.7.

`Bash.create({ fallback: true })` is for a host that can run a script on a real machine instead, as Pyramid's workspace shell does with its Hand: a command this shell can't run (a missing program, `curl` or `wget` without `fetch`, Python without Pyodide, git's network commands, an option or command it doesn't implement) throws a `MachineError` past every pipeline, `||`, `xargs` and substitution, which ends the whole script with status 127 and the command's message (`(this simulated shell has no …)`, `network access is not available`), rather than failing alone while the rest of the script answers without it. Without it, `cargo build 2>&1 | tail -1`, `curl -s URL | jq .` and `rg --json x | wc -l` exited 0 with an answer a real machine would not give, so Pyramid, which falls back only on a non-zero status with such a message, kept them. On 2026-11-05 a differential run against bash 5.2.21, GNU coreutils, ripgrep 14.1.0 and jq 1.7.1, over the files of jqlang/jq, BurntSushi/ripgrep, rust-lang/cargo and tempoxyz/tempo and a synthetic data set, ran 20,000 generated pipelines in both. With `fallback`, 288 of them fell back. The same run fixed `"$@"` with no positional parameters, which had expanded to one empty word (`for x in "$@"` ran once). It also fixed the `--` that grep and rg write before a context group from another file, ignore-file whitelists (`!/.github/`) that rg ignored for hidden entries, rg's "No files were searched" error (exit 2) for paths given explicitly, which ripgrep only reports when no path is given, and a missing final newline that sed's `p` and cat's `-A`/`-E` added. Matching runs went from 18,955 to 19,082, and exit-0 differences from 607 to 503. Most of what is left depends on the environment (du, ls -l, column, the diff algorithm). The rest is small: Unicode widths, option precedence and awk's number formatting. The run's commands, outside this repository: `fixtures.py` copies the four repositories' tracked files and writes the synthetic set; `gen.py 20000` generates the pipelines (search, reading, data, construct and listing groups); `run.py c2.jsonl out.jsonl SRC` runs each in bash under bubblewrap (no network, fresh home, a sorted copy of the files) and in this shell under Bun 1.4.4 with the same files, then classifies stdout, stderr and the exit code. Before is 93863fd with fallback, after is this change.

`Bash.create({ machine: { commands } })` stands the shell in for a real machine whose answers it must give exactly, as Pyramid's workspace does for its hosted Hand's repositories (2026-11-05). It turns fallback on, and the environment is `env` alone, without the shell's defaults. A MachineError then ends the script for a command not in `commands`, for a builtin whose answer is the machine's (`type`, `which`, `hash`, `command -v` and `-V`, `printenv`, the listings of `env`, `set`, `declare`, `export`, `readonly` and `shopt`, `alias`, `ulimit`, `umask`, `times`, `jobs`, `kill`, `pushd`, `popd`, `dirs`, `history`, `help` and `time`), for a background job, a coproc or a timed pipeline, for a program run by its path, and for a read of the top-level standard input. `$$`, `RANDOM`, `SRANDOM`, `SECONDS`, `EPOCHSECONDS`, `EPOCHREALTIME` and `BASHPID` end the script, and so does a read of a variable that neither the script nor `env` set: `Vars` records each name the script sets or unsets. `OLDPWD`, `CDPATH`, `GLOBIGNORE` and the shell's own bookkeeping (`PIPESTATUS`, `LINENO`, `REPLY` and the like) count as unset. Each message reads `X: (this simulated shell has no answer for this machine's X)`, so a host that falls back on `(this simulated shell has no` falls back for these too. Without `machine`, nothing changes.

`HostFileSystem.create({ request, writes })` (`src/bash/HostFileSystem.ts`) is a FileSystem that a host serves while a command runs. `request` sends `{ op: 'list' | 'stat' | 'read' }` over the host's own transport and returns its reply and bytes. A listing gives each entry's kind, size, mode and mtime, and `absent` names what the listing proves missing (`true` for every unlisted name), so `stat`, `ls -l` and `find` cost no request for listed entries. The host sets `batchBytes` and `cacheBytes`: the second read in a directory also asks for its other small files (up to `batchBytes` in all, each at most a quarter of it), bodies stay cached up to `cacheBytes`, and a read in flight is shared. Pyramid passes 1 MiB and 32 MiB, so `rg` over a tree costs about two requests per directory. A `{ machine }` reply throws MachineError and stays in `fs.machine`, so the host learns of it even when a command catches the error (find prints it and exits 1). Writes answer EROFS, or end the script with `writes: 'machine'`. Before reads in flight were shared, `rg -l TODO src | wc -l` over 7,300 files and 40 MB through Pyramid's plugin and pyramid-core's host made 2,580 read requests for 2.5 GB, because rg reads many files at once and each one started its own batch. After, it read 40 MB once and took 0.23 to 0.27 s against 2.3 s (dev-georgios, 2026-11-05, 4 rounds each, load about 70).

`MemoryFileSystem.create({ lower })` is a working copy over a read-only FileSystem. A directory is filled from `lower` the first time a path reaches it, and a file is read when its bytes are first needed, so `stat` and `ls -l` read nothing. `changes()` answers what the copy holds against `lower`: the files written, new or moved, with their bytes, the files of `lower` that are gone, symlinks, files whose mode differs from `lower`'s (or from 644 for a new one) and empty directories. Pyramid's workspace runs its scripts on one over core's host and keeps the change set in one transaction. Without `lower`, nothing changes.

The differential runs against the hosted Hand's userland found these, each now as the Hand does (2026-11-05): `dirname -z` and `basename -z` end each name with NUL, sed truncates its `w` files when it starts, as GNU sed opens them, sed `-i` without input files exits 4 with `sed: no input files`, sed's `e` and `v` commands are not supported (with fallback, the machine runs them), and printf `%q` leaves the letters, marks and digits of every script unquoted, as bash 5.2 does in C.UTF-8.

On 2026-11-05 a second differential pass compared this shell, as Pyramid's workspace runs it (fallback on, through Pyramid's tools/runtime.ts and plugins/shell), with the hosted Hand's userland: an Ubuntu 24.04 image at Pyramid's hand.Dockerfile digest with bash 5.2.21, GNU coreutils 9.4, grep 3.11, sed 4.9, mawk 1.3.4 20240123 as awk (no gawk, no yq), findutils 4.9.0, diffutils 3.10, ripgrep 14.1.0, jq 1.7.1, tree 2.1.1 and util-linux 2.39.3 column, each script under bubblewrap as `/bin/bash -c` in `/workspace` with this shell's environment (HOME=/home/user, LANG=C.UTF-8). 60,000 cases: two corpora of 20,000 read-only commands over tokio-rs/tokio, BurntSushi/ripgrep, serde-rs/serde, expressjs/express and a synthetic repository of odd files (names with spaces, unicode, quotes and a leading dash, CRLF, no final newline, binary, BOM, Latin-1, long lines), and two of 10,000 scripts (loops, expansions, here-docs, functions, arithmetic, redirections, file writes) over a thread's files, comparing stdout, the exit code, whether stderr is empty and the files written. Environment-specific output (directory sizes, ls -l owners and dates, du, stat, diff header times, rg --json timings) is not compared. Matching runs went from 12,213 of 12,818 and 12,017 of 12,513 compared commands, and 9,362 of 9,564 and 9,346 of 9,546 scripts, before #181, to 13,336 of 13,337 and 13,362 of 13,365 commands and 9,628 of 9,628 and 9,603 of 9,604 scripts after #190 and this change. Of the 5 left, #192 to #194 fixed column -x's row fill, ls -C's tab alignment and comm's order on a binary file. A jq 1e1000 literal and yq, which the Hand lacks, remain. The fixes: #181 and #190 (grep and rg: POSIX and Unicode classes, empty matches, GNU's stray escapes and leading quantifiers, binary files, --files-without-match, smart case), #182 (diff ports GNU's analyze.c: discard_confusing_lines, the diag heuristic and shift_boundaries, side by side), #183 and #186 (text tools: sed's bracket escapes and l, column -t padding, base64 -w 0, comm's order check, od -j, cmp -b, tree's counts, cut and tr on bytes, wc -L, sort -V, coreutils' quoting of names in messages, and options agents use), #184 (syntax errors exit 2 with bash's message, `$0` and error prefixes as `/bin/bash -c`, cd with HOME and OLDPWD, jq 1.7.1's errors and `//`), #187 to #189 (awk as mawk: bytes, no gawk extensions, mawk's printf, regex dialect and messages) and #185 (rg --json and friends). With fallback, `file` now ends the script as a machine command: libmagic's types (`C source, ASCII text`, `JavaScript source`, a `#!`-looking Rust attribute as a script) can't be reproduced here, and Pyramid's Hand answers them. What is left: NaN signs and yacc error cascades in awk, jq's preserved number literals, '.' against invalid bytes in GNU grep, a cmp -l | head whose EOF line races SIGPIPE on the Hand. The harness and its notes are outside this repository, in Pyramid's /home/ubuntu/core/output/handless/diff on dev-georgios (NOTES.md: run.py, sim.ts, real.py, the corpora and every run's rows).

On 2026-11-05 a third pass, in the same image, fixed the three differences and checked rg's binary files against ripgrep 14.1.0. Each change:

- #192: column's fill modes skip whitespace-only lines, use display widths and read each file on its own, as util-linux 2.39 does. 1,200 generated cases went from 338 matches to 1,200.
- #193: ls -C, -x and -m use display widths, a column of at least 3 cells, two spaces for -w 0 and code point order, as coreutils 9.4 does. 2,200 cases went from 1,756 matches to 2,200.
- #194: comm compares lines as bytes, as glibc's C.UTF-8 collation does. 1,500 cases went from 825 matches to 1,500.

ripgrep searches binary data in three ways:

| Target | ripgrep reads it with | It stops |
|---|---|---|
| A file found by walking a directory | a 64 KiB line buffer | at the read that holds the first NUL, which it does not search |
| An explicit file, when at most 10 paths are given and all are files | a memory map | at the first printed line with a NUL, or at the first printed line if the first 64 KiB hold one |
| Other explicit files, stdin, and walked files with --binary or -uuu | the line buffer, with each NUL as a line terminator | at the first printed line after the read that holds the NUL |

The line buffer keeps the last max(-A, -B) + 1 lines for context and triples for a line that does not fit. #195 makes rg find the NUL in the raw bytes before it decodes. It models these reads and decodes only what it searches. Before, it decoded each file whole, and a binary file took the slow escape path.

- Differential: 12,000 generated invocations over tokio with planted binaries (NUL from 0 to 500,000 bytes) went from 9,932 to 11,772 matches and from 1,783 mismatches to 0. They use -a, --binary, -uuu, -c, -l, -o, --json and context over walks, explicit files and stdin.
- Not compared: 228 pipe cases (cat f | rg) whose NUL comes after 8 KiB. Which pipe read holds that NUL depends on the writer's timing.
- Speed: over facebook/react 76baa3b5 (7,300 files, 47 with a NUL), rg useState went from 189 to 117 ms of CPU. With 200 planted 100 KB binaries, it went from 596 to 143 ms (median of 20, Bun, one in-memory shell).
- Regression check: the second pass's 60,000 cases leave 2 mismatches, jq 1e1000 and yq.

The harness is in /home/ubuntu/core/output/handless/tsfix on dev-georgios.

The same day, three follow-ups closed differences that the third pass had left out of its corpora:

- #198: rg lets the last of conflicting flags win, as ripgrep's flag update functions do. This covers output modes, -n/-N, -H/-I, --heading, --column, case, -w/-x, -F and -v. -q quiets every mode, and a fourth -u fails. 5,000 generated cases went from 1,412 mismatches to 0.
- #199: sort compares bytes in C.UTF-8 in every path, and -V does too. -f folds ASCII only, -n reads no exponent and -g puts non-numbers first. -c with -u reports equal lines, and -m merges. 4,000 cases went from 605 mismatches and 373 fallbacks to 0.
- #200: rg prints context lines with -o and prints matches past -m in the trailing context. With -r, positions refer to the replaced line, $0 is the whole match, and -v context lines are replaced. 5,000 cases went from 1,599 mismatches to 0.

rg -rn keeps grep's meaning of -r (#162), and rg -q --json prints no stats summary. ripgrep reads -rn as a replacement of "n" and prints the summary.

`Bash.create({ webassembly })` says whether the runtime compiles WebAssembly, which decides whether `python`, `python3`, `pip` and `pip3` stay (they run on Pyodide unless `python.remote` is set). Unset, `create` probes by compiling an empty module, which in Bun starts JavaScriptCore's WebAssembly engine and with it a GC helper thread per CPU the process sees. A host that starts a fresh process for every command and knows its runtime, as Pyramid's workspace shell does (one Bun process per command, a hand-free command's whole cost), passes `webassembly: true` and skips the probe; `webassembly: false` removes the Python commands without probing. On 2026-11-05 on a 32-CPU host (dev-georgios, load 45-50, Bun 1.4.4), one Pyramid shell call (`cat a.txt | wc -l` through Pyramid's tools/runtime.ts, this bundle as Bun bytecode; 5 interleaved rounds of 40 calls, CPU as the children's user plus system time) ran 37 threads with the probe and 7 without. With Pyramid's runtime already writing its answer with `Bun.write` it went from 21.6 to 18.2 ms of CPU and from 24.5 to 21.3 ms of wall time per call; with the runtime as it was, from 29.9 to 26.9 ms of CPU. A process that only creates a shell and runs one command from `src/` (transpiled each run, 5 rounds of 30) went from 30 to 25 ms of CPU with 32 CPUs visible, and by about 1 ms, within the run's noise, under `taskset` to 8 CPUs.

jq's `parseStream` (`src/bash/internal/commands/jq.ts`) tries `JSON.parse` on the whole input first and uses its own scanner only when that throws: more than one document, `NaN`, `nan` or `-Infinity`, a byte-order mark, whitespace JSON doesn't allow (`\v`, no-break space), or a syntax error, whose message comes from the scanner. The scanner slices the rest of the input and runs a regex per token. Measured on 2026-11-05 under Bun 1.4.4, median of 3: `jq length` on a 6.3 MB array of 40,000 objects took 51 ms before and 16 ms after, and `jq -r .[].name` 55 ms before and 23 ms after. In-process (median of 7, interleaved, load 7) on the same file: `jq length` 50 to 6 ms, `jq -r .[].name` 53 to 12 ms, `jq -c .[0]` 46 to 6 ms. On 200,000 random valid documents (5 seeds; keys `__proto__`, `constructor`, integer-like and lone surrogates; numbers `-0`, `1e400`, `2.5e-324` and integers past 2^53), both parsers gave the same values, the same key order and `Object.is`-equal numbers. The one exception is a `"__proto__"` key: the scanner's assignment set the object's prototype and lost the key, so `jq -c .` printed `[{"__proto__":1}]` as `[{}]`. JSON.parse keeps it, as jq 1.7 does. The input of a failed `JSON.parse` is parsed twice: a large document followed by a second one costs one extra pass over the first. Against jq 1.7 on 14 inputs × 5 filters (`.`, `length`, `keys? // type`, `[.[]?] | length`, `tojson`), the fast path removed the two `__proto__` differences and added none. Documents in sequence, documents on one line, `NaN`, `nan`, a byte-order mark, duplicate keys and key order answered as before. Differences that remain on both paths: number literals jq 1.7 keeps (`1.0`, `-0`, `9007199254740993`, `1E+400`), the error text for truncated or trailing input (jq also prints the values before the error), and a lone `\ud800` escape, which jq rejects.

Text in the shell is UTF-8 with each byte that is not part of valid UTF-8 as U+DC80..U+DCFF (Python's surrogateescape), so pipes, variables and redirections carry binary data as strings and give back the same bytes: `encode` and `decode` in `src/bash/internal/Fs.ts` convert, with `TextEncoder` for well-formed text (`String.prototype.isWellFormed`, which replaced a lone-surrogate regular expression that took 120 ms per 5 MB in QuickJS), a strict `TextDecoder` for valid UTF-8 and a loop for the rest. `Bash.create({ codec })` (`Bash.Codec`: `encode(text)`, `decode(bytes)`, and `toBase64(bytes)` and `fromBase64(text)` with `btoa`'s output and `atob`'s forgiving input, throwing as it does) replaces them, `byteLength` (`wc -c`) and the `base64` command's conversions, for every shell in the realm: the mapping is the same, so a codec only makes it native for a host whose engine interprets JavaScript, such as nanocodexyz/nanocode-swift's QuickJS, where the loops took about 0.6 s per 5 MB of binary data each way. `/dev/urandom` and `/dev/random` decode their random bytes the same way. In nanocode-swift on 2026-10-28 (release library, Apple silicon) a native codec took `cat` of a 5 MB random file into another file from 3.1 s to 0.15 s, `head -c 5000000 /dev/urandom` from 3.8 s to 0.17 s, `cat | wc -c` from 2.3 s to 0.27 s `gzip -c` from 1.8 s to 0.32 s, `base64` from 0.23 s to 0.05 s and `base64 -d` of its 76-column output from 2.5 s to 0.43 s (`base64 -d` also removes line breaks and maps the URL alphabet with `replaceAll` instead of regular expressions, 7 ms instead of 87 ms per pass over 6.7 MB in QuickJS).

`mounted(base, { "/archive": fs })` (`src/bash/Mount.ts`, exported from `nanocode-ts/bash`) places other `FileSystem`s at absolute paths: `ls`, `cat`, `find`, `grep`, `rg`, Read and globs reach them like any directory, errors name the full path, `rename` across filesystems fails with EXDEV and a read-only backend answers EROFS ("Read-only file system"). A directory that exists only as the parent of a mount point, such as `/workspace` under a `/workspace/archive` mount, is created in the base filesystem before a `write`, `mkdir`, `writeMany`, `rename` or `symlink` below it. Before 2026-11-03 `stat` answered such a directory from the mount table, so `mkdirs` skipped it and every write under it failed with `/workspace: No such file or directory` on a new `SqlFileSystem`. A Cloudflare session with `workspace.files` then retried its first turn without end, and gakonst/opentempi's sessions could write no file in `/workspace`. `node output/mount-parents.ts` (a `Bash` over `SqlFileSystem` on `node:sqlite` with a memory mount at `/workspace/archive`) seeded `AGENTS.md`, wrote and read a file and created a directory after the change, and failed all three before. `createNanocode({ workspace: { mounts(authority, env) } })` on Cloudflare mounts them into each session's shell. A `FileSystem` may implement `search(dir, literals)`, answering the files under `dir` that may contain any of the literals ignoring case (a superset), or null: `grep -r` and `rg` extract one required literal per alternative of their patterns (`literals()`), ask once, and read and regex-match only those files in walk order, so output equals a full walk; `-v`, `-L`, `-c`, `--count-matches`, `--files` and patterns without a literal still walk. gakonst/opentempi mounts its D1 archive this way (trigram FTS5 `docs_grep`). On 2026-10-26, 26 grep/rg/ls/cat commands over 300 Greek documents gave identical stdout, stderr and status with and without `search`, reading 117-206 files instead of 300. `FileSystem.matches(dir, literals, ignoreCase)` answers exactly the files under `dir` containing any literal (case-insensitively when asked), or null: `grep -rl`/`-rq` and `rg -l`/`-q` whose patterns are all plain literals or alternations of them (no `-v`, `-w`, `-x`, `-c`, `-L`) print its answer in walk order without reading a file, so a D1 mount answers them from its index (2026-10-26, gakonst/opentempi docs_text). A pattern's required literals (`literals()` in `src/bash/internal/Prune.ts`) also come from a group that is not optional: a plain, non-capturing or named group (`(…)`, `(?:…)`, `(?<name>…)`, BRE `\(…\)`) that no `?`, `*`, `{0,…}` or BRE `\?`, `\{0,…\}` follows contributes one literal per alternative inside it, and each alternative keeps the run or group whose shortest literal is longest. Lookarounds, flag groups such as `(?i)` and optional groups contribute nothing, so every match still contains one of the literals. Before 2026-11-03 a group was skipped, so `.{0,250}(A|B|C).{0,200}`, which models write for context around a phrase, had no literal and `rg` and `grep -r` read every file under the path. `grep` and `rg` also skip the regex on a line that contains none of the literals (`scan`'s `require`, a regex of the literals with the main regex's `i`, `u` and `v` flags, so case folding is the same), except `rg -U`; with `-v` such a line counts as not matching. A single pattern without a top-level alternation also yields every required group and the widest gap between consecutive groups (`conjunction()`, a `FileSystem.Conjunction`: `A.{0,30}B` gives groups `[[A], [B]]` and gaps `[30]`, `A.{0,40}(B|C)` gives `[[A], [B, C]]` and `[40]`; a gap holds only atoms such as `.`, `\s`, `\w` or a bracket with `?`, `{n}` or `{m,n}`, and anything unbounded, a backreference or a BRE pattern makes it null), which `grep -r` and `rg` pass to `search` and `excerpt` as `every`, unless `rg -U` or `grep -z` lets a match span lines. A `FileSystem` may then answer only the files with a line, or a larger record holding whole lines, that contains a literal of every group with each gap respected, and one that ignores `every` still answers a valid superset. `Bash`'s operation-budget wrapper passes `every` on; build-f4285a7 dropped it there, so its conjunctions never reached a mount. `rg` takes its literals from the patterns after the grep-style `\|` rewrite, so `rg 'A\|B'` no longer looks for files containing the text `A|B`, which found none. On 2026-11-04 over gakonst/opentempi's archive, where gaps become FTS5 `NEAR` on its trigram index (`output/archive-bench/and.json` and `heavy.json`, a Durable Object in `wnam`, old build first), ten proximity searches took 43.0 s cold before and 8.4 s after, `rg -il -e 'ΤΗΛΕΔΙΟΙΚΗΣΗ.{0,40}(ΔΕΝ|ΕΚΤΟΣ)' /workspace/archive | head -40` 10.6 to 16.9 s and 0.29 to 0.54 s, and those and the 22 commands of `commands.ts` printed identical output. On 2026-11-03 over 25 Greek committee transcripts (8 M characters) in a `MemoryFileSystem`, 39 `rg` and `grep` commands (gakonst/opentempi `output/bench-archive/rgcmp.ts`: `-v`, `-c`, `-o`, `-w`, `-x`, `-l`, `-L`, `-C`, `-i`, `-F`, `-e`, `(?i)`, grep-style `\|`, `--count-matches`, `-U`, groups) printed identical output before and after, `rg -n -i -o '.{0,250}(…|…|…).{0,200}'` took 3,763 ms before and 38 ms after, `grep -rnE '.{0,100}(…|…).{0,100}'` 1,484 and 24 ms, and the 39 together 9.0 s and 0.88 s. `rg --include GLOB` is accepted as `-g GLOB`, since models write grep's flag for rg and the error made them rerun searches. `FileSystem.excerpt(path, literals)` may answer a file's text with every line that cannot contain a literal replaced by an empty line (the same number of lines), or null: `grep -r` and `rg` use it for candidates whenever the output needs no context lines or byte offsets, `-n` included, since line numbers are unchanged; gakonst/opentempi pads rows it did not fetch from per-row newline counts (`docs_rows`). Files named on the command line take the excerpt path too (BTrg -n TERM FILEBT), so a model reading many named documents in parallel does not pull each whole; before 2026-10-26 one Opus cell of eight BTrg -nBT/BThead | rgBT calls on committee transcripts spent 108 s reading them in full. `rg` reads a short-flag cluster with `r` whose other letters are all rg boolean flags, such as `-rl`, `-ril` or `-rn`, as grep's recursive `-r` (dropped, since rg always recurses) instead of `--replace` with that text: on 2026-10-26 Sonnet on gakonst/opentempi wrote `rg -rl "A|B" dir | head` in most turns, which real rg runs as replace-with-`l` over every matching line (481 s over the D1 archive instead of 0.3 s); `-r TEXT` and `-rTEXT` with other letters still replace, except that a separate `-r` is dropped when `-l`, `-c` or `-q` makes a replacement meaningless (`rg -r 'A|B' dir -i -l`, written by the same model, then searches for `A|B`). A pattern whose only alternations are written grep-style as `\|` (no bare `|`) is read with `|`, since models carry grep's syntax over and real rg would match a literal pipe and print nothing; that empty result sent Sonnet into ten calls of `ls` probing whether the archive existed. `pause()` (commands/util.ts) yields to a timer only once 25 ms have passed since the last yield; until 2026-10-26 it also yielded every 64 calls, so `find` over 81,200 files spent 1.6 of 1.9 s waiting on about 1,300 zero-delay timers (0.27 s now; `find | grep` 4.0 s to 0.23 s), and 20-60 s in a Worker. `find` and `rg` walks list the subdirectories of each directory they enter ahead of descending into them (`listings()` in `src/bash/internal/Prune.ts`, at most 64 listings in flight, the `ahead` read window), so a mount whose listing is a network round trip pays one round trip per level instead of one per directory; `find` keeps listing one directory at a time when its expression has `-prune`, `-quit`, `-delete`, `-exec`, `-execdir`, `-ok`, `-okdir`, `-fprint`, `-fprint0`, `-fprintf` or `-fls`, whose result depends on walking in order. `rg --files` and `rg -l` answered from `matches` write each path through `pause()` like `find`, so a long listing streams into the pipe: before 2026-11-04 `rg --files` wrote every path at once, and over gakonst/opentempi's archive (81,208 files) it failed with `pipe buffer exceeded 16000000 characters` after 57,743 lines. On 2026-11-04 in a Durable Object in `wnam` over that archive (`output/archive-bench`), a cold `find /workspace/archive -iname '*…*'` took 16 D1 round trips and 4.5 s before and 7 and 1.8 to 2.1 s after (with gakonst/opentempi's split-document listings folded into its subtree listings), and 22 grep, rg, ls, find, cat and du commands printed identical output.

`Chat` takes `tools` and `reasoning`, each `"expanded" | "collapsed" | "hidden"` (`Disclosure`): `tools: "collapsed"` shows each tool cell as its header line (name, title, duration) until clicked, with its nested calls (such as a Code Mode cell's bash commands) as header rows with their output line count, `"hidden"` leaves cells out; reasoning renders as `Thinking`, a disclosure with the summary as Markdown that streams open while live and collapses to "Thought for Ns" (from the entry's `started_at`/`ended_at`) when done (`"collapsed"`, the default), and `"expanded"` keeps it open.
