Before 2026-10-26 reasoning was one plain paragraph with `**` stripped. `nanocode-ts/cloudflare/transport`'s `model()` may answer `web_search`, which each turn sends; the Cloudflare runtime keeps it in the session descriptor, opens the runtime with `web_search` set to it, and reopens a resident runtime when it changes, so a page can turn the native web search tools on or off per conversation.

The shell also clones and runs WebAssembly (`src/bash/internal/commands/git.ts`, `wasm.ts`, `archive.ts`), as nanocodexyz/nanocode's browser shell did before its 2026-10-13 reduction (`js/packages/nanocodex-tools/src/shell.ts` and `repository-archive.ts` at 639b8995b). `git clone URL [DIR]` and `gh repo clone OWNER/REPO [DIR]` (`-b REF` for a branch, tag or commit) download a GitHub source archive through the shell's `fetch` (`https://codeload.github.com/O/R/tar.gz/REF`, then `api.github.com/repos/O/R/tarball/REF`), stream it through `DecompressionStream` and a tar reader that understands pax, GNU long names and base-256 sizes, strip its root, refuse unsafe paths and `.git`, skip symlinks, and leave files out instead of failing: a file over 8 MiB (`maxFileBytes`; its body is skipped in the stream, never buffered), files past 200 MiB written (`maxBytes`) or 50,000 files (`maxFiles`), and files the filesystem refuses as too large, each listed on stderr with a raw.githubusercontent.com URL to fetch it and recorded as `omitted` in `.git/nanocode.json` so `git status` does not report it; only an archive over 2 GiB (`maxDownloadBytes`) fails. On 2026-10-24 in Node, `git clone https://github.com/wevm/viem` (354 MB uncompressed, six test vectors of 13 to 84 MiB) took 8.9 s and wrote 3,175 files (48.6 MiB), where it had failed on its 84 MiB snapshot file, and vercel/ai (9,529 files, 86.7 MiB) took 3.1 s; the commit comes from the archive's pax comment, and two best-effort api.github.com reads add the default branch and the commit's author and message. Files reach the filesystem in batches of 4 MiB of archive (`BATCH_BYTES`, the size of the SQL journal's and `SqlFileSystem`'s staged transactions) through `FileSystem.writeMany(dirs, files)`, which `SqlFileSystem` runs as one transaction per batch of `INSERT OR IGNORE` directory rows and file rows with the clone's mtime, `MemoryFileSystem` and `mounted` forward, and other filesystems get as `writeEach` (mkdir, write and utimes per entry); the blob ids of a batch are hashed concurrently. Before 2026-10-27 each file was a stat of its directory, a stat of itself, a write and a stat for its mtime, with its SHA-1 awaited in between: a zyzz clone made 5,139 SQL transactions and a vercel/ai clone 44,939, now 31 and 69 (Node, `node:sqlite`), and on the live demos the clone took 2.1 s (zyzz) and 22.3 s (vercel/ai) on Cloudflare and 4.0 to 5.3 s and 38.2 s on Vercel. It writes `.git/HEAD`, `.git/config` and `.git/nanocode.json`, so `status` (by size and mtime), `log`, `show`, `rev-parse`, `branch`, `remote`, `ls-files` and `describe` answer for the snapshot; there is no history, commit, pull or push. A download that sends nothing for `stallMs` (30 s), before the response or between body chunks (time spent writing files does not count), or whose connection drops, is abandoned, its partial files removed, and retried once; a second failure ends the clone with exit 128 and `fatal: could not download https://github.com/OWNER/REPO at REF: <reason> (2 attempts)`. Before, the archive fetch had no timeout: on 2026-10-24, 15 concurrent sessions on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare each cloning wevm/viem in one cell finished in 5 to 16 s except three, whose codeload stream stalled until Workers reported `git: Network connection lost.` after 80, 345 and 345 s, holding the cell (and the 600 s workspace timeout) the whole time; from the hosted Hand 15 parallel curl downloads of the same archive all took 8.7 to 9.4 s, so GitHub's load alone does not explain it. `BashOptions.github` sets `archive(owner, repo, ref)` (a URL, such as a same-origin route, because codeload.github.com allows CORS only for render.githubusercontent.com, so browser hosts proxy the archive), `details`, `stallMs` and the caps. `githubArchives(fetch)` is a fetch that allows only GET and HEAD to those archive and commit URLs, adds `user-agent: nanocode-ts` outside browsers when a request has none (api.github.com answers 403 `Request forbidden by administrative rules` without one and Workers' fetch sends none, so before 2026-10-24 a cell's plain `fetch` of a repository's commits failed on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare and worked on nanocode.xyz/vercel), and refuses anything else with an error naming the method, the URL and what it reaches, so a model stops retrying (Codex Luna on nanocode.xyz/vercel spent 11 tool calls on a refused jsonplaceholder.typicode.com fetch before and 2 after); `nanocode-ts/cloudflare` gives it to sessions whose `codeMode` sets no `fetch`, so scripts get no network but `git clone` works. `wasm FILE ARGS`, `wasmtime [run] FILE ARGS` and running a file that starts with `\0asm` (`./prog.wasm`) run a WASI preview 1 program: a TypeScript `wasi_snapshot_preview1` host (args, environment, clocks, random, stdio, files and directories, `poll_oneoff` without sleeping; anything else answers ENOSYS) over an in-memory copy of the explicit paths, the working directory's subtree and `/` to depth 2 (`Wasm.Options.rootDepth`, which a shell over a real disk such as nanocodexyz/nanocode-swift's sets to -1 so a run does not read `/bin` and `/usr/bin`; 64 MiB, 20,000 entries; an implicit entry the file system refuses to stat, read or list, such as macOS's `/.file`, is left out instead of failing the run), written back when the program exits, with exit 134 for a trap and 124 for the time limit, checked on host calls. Modules compile with `WebAssembly.compile`, cached by SHA-256, wherever the host allows it (browsers, Node and so Vercel Functions, Deno, Bun). Cloudflare Workers refuse to compile WebAssembly at runtime ("Wasm code generation disallowed by embedder"), so when that fails, or with `BashOptions.wasm.force`, the shell runs the module in `BashOptions.wasm.interpreter`: `interpreter/`, a Rust crate around wasmi 2 that `scripts/build.mjs` builds into `dist/interpreter.wasm` (`nanocode-ts/interpreter.wasm`, which `nanocode-ts/cloudflare` imports precompiled like the main module). `src/bash/internal/commands/engine.ts` drives either engine behind one interface, so the WASI host and the cells' `wasm` global and `WebAssembly` shim are the same code; the interpreter meters fuel in slices of 10 million so the host checks the time limit and abort signal between them, and `fuel` and `memoryBytes` cap a program. The crate builds at `opt-level = 3` with `codegen-units = 1` (1.35 MB, smaller than the 1.37 MB `opt-level = "s"` build), a host call reads the instance's memory from the store's data instead of looking up its `memory` export and keeps its argument slots on the stack, and `engine.ts` reuses one `DataView` of the interpreter's memory and one view of the instance's memory until either moves. On 2026-10-25 against `opt-level = "s"` (best of 3 interleaved runs, Node 24 with the interpreter forced, then Miniflare's workerd): a C loop of 100M iterations 5.7 s to 5.9 s and 5.6 s to 4.8 s, 200,000 `clock_gettime` calls 260 to 216 ms and 327 to 248 ms, a 200,000-element `qsort` with `snprintf` 9.4 to 8.1 s and 8.8 to 8.0 s, `wasm-tools --version` 63 to 51 ms and 61 to 46 ms, and CPython 3.12 WASI `print(1)` 3.0 to 2.2 s and 2.6 to 2.1 s; native V8 runs the loop in 0.13 s, about 40 times faster. Wasm `tail-call` dispatch (`-C target-feature=+tail-call`, which wasmi's `auto-dispatch` then selects) overflowed the V8 stack on the loop, and disabling fuel saved only about 10%, so both stay as they are. A refused compile without an interpreter exits 126. `wat2wasm FILE.wat [-o FILE.wasm]` (`src/bash/internal/commands/wat.ts`, imported on its first run) assembles WebAssembly text in TypeScript, so it works wherever the shell does: flat and folded instructions with named locals, labels, functions, types and globals, the MVP numeric, memory and control opcodes plus `memory.copy`/`memory.fill`, imports (WASI's `fd_write` and the like), memory with data segments, globals, a funcref table with element segments, exports and start; it writes the module and warns when `WebAssembly.validate` rejects it. `BASH_SHELL_INSTRUCTIONS` points the model at it instead of writing module bytes by hand: on 2026-10-24 GPT-6 Luna on nanocode.xyz/vercel/chatgpt, asked for a WASI hello program, wrote the bytes from Python, miscounted a section length and gave up after four cells. WASI briefly ran only in browsers (aa84544, nanocodexyz/nanocode-ts#53); Georgios reversed that on 2026-10-24 and wanted WebAssembly on Workers and Vercel too. On 2026-10-23 the interpreter was 1,200,805 bytes (270,700 gzipped) and in Node a loop of 100 million iterations took 5.6 s interpreted and 0.1 s native; on 2026-10-24 a cell in the Cloudflare session Durable Object under Miniflare got `engine=interpreter add=42`. On 2026-10-23 in Node, `git clone https://github.com/nanocodexyz/nanocode-ts` took about 0.5 s (83 files), `gh repo clone wevm/zyzz` fetched 1,123 files (14.3 MiB), and a C program built with wasi-sdk read stdin, arguments, the environment and files and wrote a file back. On 2026-10-24 a differential harness ran 1,925 scripts agents write (shell core, find, grep, rg, sed, awk, jq, text tools, xargs, diff, archives) in GNU bash and in this shell, comparing stdout, the exit code and whether stderr was empty: the shell went from 1,459 to 1,880 matches. That pass added process substitution, associative arrays, traps, getopts, globstar/nullglob/dotglob, pushd/popd, `|&`, `$(< file)`, `/dev/null`, `/dev/zero` and `/dev/urandom`, rewrote find, grep, rg (ignore files, hidden and binary files skipped, no line numbers without `-n`), diff (Myers, GNU hunks, `-r`) and xargs after GNU, added `patch`, `bc`, `shuf`, `fmt`, `hexdump`, `numfmt`, `truncate` and `iconv`, and raised the loop and command limits to 1,000,000. Known gaps: pipelines buffer each stage (16 MB `maxBufferLength`), arithmetic uses doubles, awk runs about 2.5 s per million lines, background jobs run synchronously, and namerefs are missing. Over 5,000 files and 41 MB, `grep -r`, `rg -l` and `find | xargs grep` each took under 0.15 s in Node. `BASH_SHELL_INSTRUCTIONS` tells the model this and is part of `BASH_CODE_MODE_INSTRUCTIONS` and the bash tool's description.

Local git (2026-10-25, `src/bash/internal/commands/git.ts`): a clone records each file's git blob SHA-1 in `.git/nanocode.json` (`files`: size, mtime, id), and `local` in the same file holds branches, commits (parent, message, author, date and the paths each changed, as blob ids or null), the index as overrides of HEAD's tree, and a stat cache (size, mtime, id) so status re-hashes only files whose size or mtime moved. Blobs a commit or `git add` needs are stored raw under `.git/objects/<id>`; a clone's own files are not copied, and a checkout, reset or diff that needs one reads it from raw.githubusercontent.com at the snapshot's commit. `git init` makes the same file with no base. Supported: init, status (-s, --porcelain), add (-A, -u, paths), rm (--cached), mv, commit (-m, -a, --amend, --allow-empty), diff (worktree, --cached, REV, REV REV, A..B, --stat, --name-only, --name-status, --quiet, -- paths; hunks come from the shell's own `diff -u`), log (--oneline, -n, --format with %H %h %s %an %ae %ad, -p, --stat), show, branch (create, -d, -m, -a, --show-current), checkout/switch (-b/-c, branch, commit, -- paths, REV -- paths), restore (--staged, --source), reset (--soft, --mixed, --hard, paths), rev-parse (HEAD~N, ^, branches, short ids) and config. There is no fetch, pull, push, merge, rebase or stash. Before, every command but clone/status/log/rev-parse/branch/remote/ls-files/describe answered 'not supported', so a model asked to commit and diff fell back to cp -r and diff -r. Checked in Node on 2026-10-25 against sindresorhus/ky at v1.7.2: edit, delete and add files, status, diff --stat, add -A, commit, checkout -b, commit -a, diff HEAD~1, switch back and forth, show --stat, reset --hard HEAD~2 (restored a deleted file from raw.githubusercontent.com), and an init repository's commit, diff and restore.

Large archives and modules stay within a 128 MB isolate. `tar -x` and `tar -t` stream the archive through `DecompressionStream` in 64 KiB slices and `TarReader` (`untar` in `src/bash/internal/commands/archive.ts`), writing each entry as it is read instead of inflating the whole archive and then unpacking it. `SqlFileSystem.write` stages a file over 4 MiB under `//staging<path>` in transactions of at most 4 MiB and moves it to its path in one last transaction, so a write never holds a copy of every chunk at once and a failed write leaves the old file. The interpreter does not cache a module over 4 MiB (`release` in `wasm.ts` drops it after the run), and `engine.ts` forgets an interpreter instance whose memory grew past 16 MiB once no module or instance uses it, because wasmi's linear memory never shrinks. On 2026-10-24 in Node with the interpreter forced, over `node:sqlite`, extracting wasm-tools 1.240.0's wasm32-wasip1 release (3.8 MB, one 15.8 MB module) peaked at 60 MB of ArrayBuffers instead of 131 MB, `base64 -d` of its 5 MB base64 at 36 MB of heap instead of 129 MB (`Uint8Array.from` with a callback per byte), and `wasm wasm-tools.wasm --version` grew the interpreter to 42 MB. Bytes that pass through a Code Mode cell cost far more: in Miniflare the same `fetch` into a cell grew the nanocode module's memory from 3.3 MB to 64.5 MB and a following `bash.writeFile` to 135.5 MB (base64 strings in QuickJS and the logged nested calls), while `curl -sL -o` in `bash` left it at 3.7 MB, so hosts tell models to download with curl.

`python3`, `python`, `pip` and `pip3` (`src/bash/internal/commands/python.ts`) run CPython on Pyodide 314.0.7, as nanocodexyz/nanocode's browser shell did at 639b8995b (`js/tools/browser/browserPython.mjs`). Pyodide loads on the first call and stays for the process, one instance per loader: `BashOptions.python.load` returns a host's own instance, and otherwise Node imports the optional peer dependency `pyodide` (Node cannot import modules over HTTPS, and Pyodide's module reads its files through `node:fs`) and browsers import `pyodide.mjs` from jsdelivr (`PYODIDE_URL`, or `python.indexURL`). Each run copies the working directory's subtree, `/tmp` and the script into Pyodide's in-memory filesystem at the same paths (64 MiB and 20,000 entries unless `python.maxBytes` and `python.maxFiles` say otherwise); the copy walks breadth first, so when a cloned repository under the working directory fills that budget, the files beside it still make it in: on 2026-10-24 on nanocode.xyz/vercel, after cloning vercel/ai (87 MB) into /workspace, `python3 chart.py` failed twice with `FileNotFoundError: [Errno 44]` on /workspace/sizes.txt, which bash had written, because the depth-first copy had spent the budget inside the clone first, and in Node 14,000 5 kB files in /workspace/ai reproduced it), runs `-c`, a file, stdin or `-m` with `sys.argv`, the shell's environment and working directory, then writes created, changed and deleted files back; modules imported from the workspace leave `sys.modules` afterwards, so edits take effect, and runs share the instance one at a time. Imports of packages Pyodide ships (numpy, pandas, ...) load through `loadPackagesFromImports` before the run, and `pip install` goes through micropip, so pure-Python wheels from PyPI install for the life of the instance; package downloads use the global `fetch`, not the shell's. `SystemExit` sets the exit code. In Node with the default loader (no `python.load`, `python.indexURL` or `python.worker: false`) Pyodide runs in a `node:worker_threads` worker (`src/bash/internal/commands/pyworker.ts`, its source passed as `eval` code and reached through `process.getBuiltinModule`, so bundlers trace nothing): stdout and stderr come back as they are written, so CPU-bound Python never blocks the host's event loop. Since 2026-10-27 the worker keeps the files between runs, as the remote mirror does (`PythonOptions.mirror` names whose files they are; `nanocode-ts/vercel` passes the session's run ID, and without it each `pythonCommands` makes one): a run lists the roots by stat (`scan`, 256 MiB and 20,000 files unless `python.maxBytes` and `python.maxFiles` say otherwise), sends the worker the paths with their size:mtime (`sync`), and the worker prunes files and directories the listing lacks, wipes everything when the mirror name changed, and asks for the files whose stamp it does not hold, which alone cross. During the run the worker records every path Python opens for writing, writes, truncates, renames or deletes through the Pyodide filesystem's `trackingDelegate`, and afterwards returns only those, which the shell writes back and stamps (`stamps`), so later runs do not send them again; sync, run, write-back and stamps hold the worker for one session at a time. Before, each run copied the whole working directory (64 MiB at most) into the worker and the tree back: in Node with a vercel/ai clone in /workspace every `python3 -c 'print(1)'` took 1.24 to 1.42 s and Python could not see 695 files (24.1 MiB); now the first run takes 2.0 s and later ones 0.31 to 0.38 s, with every file visible. On Vercel a run that held the loop for 5 s or more (loading Pyodide, `import matplotlib`, a busy loop, `time.sleep`) left a durable-harness run-ahead write unacknowledged that long, its watchdog gave the delivery up (`demoted: durability stalled`) and another drive took the session over, so the turn ran twice and the cell came back as interrupted by a host crash (seen on nanocode.xyz/vercel on 2026-10-24), and in a shared Fluid instance every other session's stream stalled with it (up to 75 s). Measured in Node on 2026-10-24, the longest event-loop gap went from 2.4 s (first `python3`), 3.8 s (`import matplotlib`) and 20 s (a 20 s busy loop) to none over 300 ms. The time limit and the abort signal stop a running script through Pyodide's interrupt buffer: in the worker an `Int32Array` over a `SharedArrayBuffer`, and a worker that has not answered 3 s past the limit is terminated and replaced; elsewhere an object whose index 0 the interpreter reads between bytecodes. Its SIGINT handler raises only in workspace code, never inside the asyncio loop, whose escaping exceptions crash Node, and a script suspended in an `await` is cancelled instead. On the main thread a loop of `time.sleep` calls reaches few interrupt checks: on 2026-10-23 `for i in range(1000): time.sleep(0.01)` ran its full 11 s past a 1.5 s limit, while `while True: pass` stopped at 1.5 s; in the worker both stop at the limit (2026-10-24). `git clone` and Python's snapshot and write-back yield to the event loop every 40 ms (`breathe` in `src/bash/internal/Fs.ts`), because the filesystem calls they make can all complete synchronously. On hosts that cannot compile WebAssembly at runtime, such as Cloudflare Workers, a `Bash` leaves out `python`, `python3`, `pip` and `pip3` unless `options.commands` supplies them, so `command -v python3` finds nothing and running one answers 127 `bash: python3: command not found (Python is not available on this host)`: before 2026-10-24 `command -v` printed `/usr/bin/python3` there, and Codex Luna on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare checked it, wrote a script and lost a cell to exit 126 in three of 20 tasks. Other commands models reach for and this shell lacks (node, npm, deno, tsc, sqlite3, gcc, cargo and the like) say why in the same parenthesis after `command not found`; `BASH_CODE_MODE_INSTRUCTIONS` also says there is no node, npm or TypeScript compiler and JavaScript runs in the cell, because Codex and Claude on both demos ran `node fib.js` first in every run of a "write and run a Node script" task. On 2026-10-23 in Node 24 with the pyodide package, the first `python3 -c` took 1.9 to 2.6 s, later runs 3 to 20 ms, the first `import numpy` 1.1 to 1.3 s and `pip install cowsay` 0.6 s; in headless Chrome over jsdelivr the first run took 1.9 s, numpy 0.9 s and the pip install 0.6 s.

`PythonOptions.remote` (`PythonRemote`) runs `python3`, `python`, `pip` and `pip3` on another machine instead of Pyodide, for hosts that cannot compile WebAssembly: each run sends a `PythonRequest` (the mode, `argv`, the working directory, the environment, stdin, the time limit and the snapshot of the working directory's subtree, /tmp and the script, base64, at most `maxBytes`) and gets back a `PythonReply` with the exit status (negative for a timeout), stdout, stderr and the tree after the run, a file's bytes only when they changed, which the shell writes back as it does for Pyodide; the commands then exist even where WebAssembly cannot compile, and the workspace instructions say Python runs as CPython in a Linux container (`BASH_REMOTE_PYTHON_INSTRUCTIONS`). `nanocode-ts/cloudflare` takes it per session as `workspace.python(authority, env)`, with an 8 MiB snapshot limit so the base64 copies fit a Worker's 128 MB; nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare answers it from a Cloudflare container (CPython 3.13 with numpy, pandas, matplotlib, scipy, sympy, networkx and pillow) per visitor account. Measured on 2026-10-24 from gak-9 against that image under Docker (amd64 emulated on Apple Silicon): `python3 --version` 255 ms, a script 235 ms, a numpy and matplotlib chart written back to the workspace 3.5 s. With `PythonOptions.shared` the remote shares the shell's file system, as nanocodexyz/nanocode-swift's Pyodide in a WebView does over the app's own files: a run sends the request with `roots` (the working directory, /tmp and the script) and no files or mirror, nothing is scanned, uploaded or written back, and the reply's tree fields are ignored, so a 56 MB folder of 5,002 files costs nothing per run.

Remote Python keeps a mirror (2026-10-25): each `pythonCommands` (one per `Bash`) names a mirror (`PythonRequest.mirror`, a random id) and remembers which files the service holds (path to size:mtime). A run lists the roots by stat alone, sends only files that changed since the service last saw them, in `sync` requests of at most `batchBytes` (6 MiB) and then the run itself with the rest and `keep` (paths it expects the service to still have); the service answers `missing` when it lost any (a new container, another session's mirror, a file a run changed outside its roots) and the shell re-sends those. The reply lists every file, with bytes only for those the run changed, at most 6 MiB of them per reply and the rest named in `more`, fetched with `fetch` requests. `maxBytes` now bounds the whole mirrored tree (Cloudflare passes 256 MiB, `PYTHON_REMOTE_BYTES`); one file over twice `batchBytes` is left out. Every Python path (remote, Pyodide, worker) now says on stderr how many files Python could not see. Before, each run shipped the whole snapshot in one request capped at 8 MiB, so on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare on 2026-10-25 Python saw 941 of vercel/ai's 9,547 files and said nothing. A service without `mirror` support answers without echoing it: small directories still work (one request), larger ones fail with a message. Measured on 2026-10-25 against nanocode-cloudflare-durable's python image in Docker: first run in a vercel/ai clone 6.9 s and 125 MB over 16 requests, later runs 0.2-0.5 s with one request (0.8 MB, the keep list), a container restart re-sent everything, and a 10 MB output, deletions both ways, pip and a 40 s run all came back. Python and WebAssembly now get the time limit of the exec that runs them (`Bash.exec` passes its `timeoutMs` into the runner's limits; workspace calls default to 600 s) instead of `Limits.timeoutMs`' 30 s, which killed a 40 s python3 loop on 2026-10-25. Files over `batchBytes` cross in 6 MiB pieces both ways: uploads as `sync` requests with `append`, and a run's large outputs are named in `large` and read back with `fetch` and `range`, so no message nears the 32 MiB limit on Durable Object RPC values. Before, a Luna turn on nanocode.xyz/cloudflare/chatgpt that wrote 24 MiB from Python failed with 'Serialized RPC arguments or return values are limited to 32MiB' (2026-10-25); checked in Docker: a 24 MiB Python output and a 20 MB shell file round-trip with matching sha256, at most 8.4 MB per message.

A mirror outlives its shell (2026-10-29). `PythonOptions.mirror` names the remote mirror too (`nanocode-ts/cloudflare` passes `<session id>:<scope>`), and a shell that knows none of its mirror's files, such as one a restarted session object made, first sends `listing` (every path with its size:mtime stamp) and the service answers `missing` with the paths whose stamp it does not hold; uploads carry `stamps` (a file split into pieces only with its last), the service forgets a stamp when a run changes or deletes the file, and the shell sends the stamps of files it wrote back with its next request, so a later shell need not send them again. Before, every new `Bash` drew a random mirror, the service wiped its files and the shell uploaded the whole tree again. `pythonServer` also keeps the files of up to 32 MiB of other mirrors (`PARKED_BYTES`, least recently used dropped first) in JavaScript memory, out of Python's reach, and puts them back when their mirror returns, so two sessions on one `Python` object, or visitors whose objects share an isolate's Pyodide, no longer wipe each other's files on every switch; the Node worker does the same with 64 MiB, for a Fluid instance that serves several Vercel sessions. Its interrupt raises SIGINT at most every 50 ms past the deadline, as the main-thread Pyodide does: returning it on every check never stopped `while True: pass`. Measured in Node with Pyodide on 2026-10-29 (`pythonServer` behind a `Bash` with a remote, 2,001 files of 16.3 MB): the first run sent 16.3 MB in 3 requests, a new shell on the same mirror 0.16 MB in 2 (16.3 MB in 3 with a random mirror), a file the shell edited went up alone, and after a run in another session the first session's next run uploaded nothing; in the worker, returning to a 4,000-file session after another took 0.8 to 1.6 s instead of 2.9 s.

`preloadPython(options, packages)` loads the instance `python3` uses for those options and then `packages` (each once per instance), and `pythonHint(text)` answers the shipped packages a prompt names (`chart` or `plot` names matplotlib) when it mentions Python or one of them, else undefined, so a page starts Pyodide while the visitor types. Pyodide's files on jsdelivr are served `max-age=31536000` with brotli, so the browser's HTTP cache keeps them across visits. On 2026-10-24 on the deployed Cloudflare backend, a prompt typed at 40 ms a key that ran `python3 -c` took 94 ms in the tab instead of 2.06 s, and 3.5 and 4.9 s from Enter to output instead of 7.3 and 6.9 s.

A session with `config.workspace` (a directory such as a shell's `cwd`) gets its provider's own workspace tools from the SDK instead of a host-defined shell tool, and with `config.providers` each backend's agents get their own provider's, so a Claude root's Codex child gets Codex's (`open` gives each configured harness its own tool set, sharing the host tools, MCP tools and Code Mode host between them): Claude's `Bash`, `Read`, `Edit` and `Write` (`nanocode::claude::host_tools`; since SDK df3699a1f `Read` returns `.png`, `.jpg`, `.jpeg`, `.gif` and `.webp` files as images through `read_bytes`, as Claude Code does, where it read them as text and a 160 KB chart failed as larger than 256 KB) and Codex's `apply_patch`, `exec_command`, `write_stdin` and `update_plan` (`nanocode::codex_tools::host_tools`), with their trained names, schemas, validation, output formats and Code Mode policies, over `options.workspace`, a `WorkspaceHost` (`read(path)` answering the text or null, `write(path, contents)` creating parents, `remove(path)` and `run({ command, cwd, timeout_ms }, signal)` answering `{ stdout, stderr, exit_code, truncated }`). `shell.workspace()` is that host over a `Bash`, so `open({ ...config, workspace: shell.cwd }, { ...options, workspace: shell.workspace() })` gives the model the simulated shell and its files through the tools it was trained on. `config.workspace_tools` (`"all"`, `"files"` or `"none"`, or one per provider as `{ claude, codex }`) narrows that set: `"files"` keeps only Claude's `Read`, `Edit` and `Write` or Codex's `apply_patch` (`host_file_tools`). Its default is `"files"` for Codex in Code Mode and `"all"` otherwise: in a Code Mode session the cells' `bash` global runs the same shell as `exec_command`, `write_stdin` can only report that no session is running, and nothing renders `update_plan`, while Claude's `Bash` keeps `run_in_background`, which no cell can do, since a cell ends when its code returns. On 2026-10-25 with scripted providers (hosted-Hand `~/w/tooltrim/bench`), a Codex Code Mode session's first request fell from 9,947 to 8,629 o200k tokens (40,189 to 34,893 bytes) with this default and the SDK's root-only `submit_result`, and a Claude one from 7,459 to 7,210, or to 6,193 with `"files"` and 5,423 with `"none"`. The Cloudflare and Vercel adapters forward `workspaceTools`. Commands finish before `exec_command` returns, so `write_stdin` reports that no session is running. Claude's `Bash` takes `run_in_background` since fa19016c4bc1411e03b747c380e23c1a1f763abb: the SDK keeps the `shell_run` request pending as a background task and answers its task ID, `TaskOutput` reads or waits for its result and `TaskStop` cancels the request, which aborts the `run` signal; nothing streams its output or notifies the model, and the task lives as long as the runtime (a Vercel drive, a Durable Object's instance). On 2026-10-24 through `nanocodeHarness` on world-local with a scripted Claude model, top-level and in a cell, a background `sleep 2; echo bg-done` answered running at once and completed under a blocking `TaskOutput`, and `TaskStop` ended a background `sleep 30` at once; before, the call failed with `this executor does not support background commands`. On 2026-10-22 a Node run with mock providers wrote, edited, read and grepped a file through Claude's tools and added and updated one with Codex's `apply_patch` and read it back with `exec_command`, with a scratch script using the mock providers of `scripts/measure-node.ts`.

`nanocode-ts/cloudflare/python` is the `Python` Durable Object the Cloudflare host binds as `workspace.python`: Pyodide inside workerd, no container (Georgios, 2026-10-26: no sandboxes, Pyodide with numpy and matplotlib prebuilt, and pip working). Workers refuse to compile WebAssembly at run time, so `scripts/pyodide.mjs` (run by `build:ts`, skipped while `dist/pyodide/stamp` matches the pyodide version and the script) loads the `pyodide` devDependency in Node, imports numpy, matplotlib, pandas, Pillow, ctypes and sqlite3, pip-installs two pure wheels and adds every `addFunction` stub signature of up to three i32/i64/f32/f64 parameters, records every WebAssembly binary compiled on the way and writes them to `dist/pyodide/` with `modules.js` (exported as `nanocode-ts/pyodide-modules`), keyed by `src/internal/Fingerprint.ts`: 516 modules, 26.6 MiB, so a Worker that imports the object uploads about 9.0 MiB gzipped of the 10 MiB limit. `src/cloudflare/internal/Pyodide.ts` replaces `WebAssembly.Module`, `compile`, `instantiate` and `instantiateStreaming` with lookups in that table (a binary not in it, such as scipy's, fails with a message naming what is built in), serves `pyodide.asm.wasm` as a marker the table maps to the core module, strips the `integrity` option from package fetches (workerd refuses it) and checks the SHA-256 itself, and sets `process.browser`, `process.type` and `location` before importing `pyodide/pyodide.mjs` and `pyodide/pyodide.asm.mjs` (`createPyodideModule`), so neither takes its Node path; packages and wheels still download from jsDelivr. The host must install the exact `pyodide` version the modules were recorded from. `src/bash/internal/commands/pyserver.ts` (`pythonServer`) answers the same `PythonRequest` protocol the CPython container's `server.py` did (mirror, stamps, keep and missing, fetch and range, 6 MiB reply budget, large files), running each request through `HELPER`, with stamps as content fingerprints because a Worker's clock stands still during computation; since 2026-10-27 it fingerprints only files the run touched, recorded through Pyodide's `trackingDelegate` as in the Node worker, and checks `keep` against the stamps it holds instead of hashing every kept file, where it used to hash the whole mirrored tree three times per run (in Node with a vercel/ai clone, a run took 1.16 to 1.21 s before and 0.20 to 0.26 s after); one Pyodide serves every `Python` object in an isolate (Cloudflare packs several objects of a class into one 128 MB isolate, and on 2026-10-26 a visitor's first pandas run on the live demo reset because other visitors' Pyodides were resident beside it), its requests serialized and its files wiped whenever the mirror changes, and each object's successful `pip install`/`uninstall` argv lists are saved in its storage and replayed into the shared Pyodide before that object's next run (so visitors also see each other's installed wheels). Measured on Cloudflare on 2026-10-26: a cold boot 5.8 s, first matplotlib import with its wheels 7-10 s, then 0.2-0.3 s per call; the WebAssembly heap is 30 MiB after boot, 43 with numpy, 74 with matplotlib and 107 with pandas, and it never shrinks, so after a call that loaded all three the 128 MB isolate is reset: that call answers, the next boots again, the client re-uploads the workspace through `missing`, and pip packages come back from storage. A Python loop that never yields runs until the Worker's CPU limit, since interrupts and timers see the frozen clock.

The Cloudflare Python object boots from a memory snapshot (2026-10-27, `src/bash/internal/commands/pysnapshot.ts`): `scripts/pyodide.mjs` boots Pyodide in Node, imports numpy and matplotlib (`SNAPSHOT_PACKAGES`) and draws and saves a figure, and writes the WebAssembly heap below `sbrk` with Pyodide's `makeMemorySnapshot` header, the package files (`meta.files`) and the layout to `dist/pyodide/snapshot/part-<n>.bin` (8 MiB each, 88 MiB raw, 11 parts; `SNAPSHOT` in `nanocode-ts/pyodide-modules` lists them). A host serves that directory as Workers static assets bound as `PYODIDE_SNAPSHOT` in the Worker that exports `Python` (`"assets": { "directory": "<node_modules>/nanocode-ts/dist/pyodide/snapshot", "binding": "PYODIDE_SNAPSHOT" }`), and `loadWorkersPyodide` then restores instead of booting: it reads the parts with `arrayBuffer()` two ahead (gzip parts cost 1.7 to 2.3 s of CPU in `DecompressionStream`'s 22,000 small chunks, raw parts 0.13 to 0.16 s), preloads every extension module numpy and matplotlib load (29 libraries, from the precompiled modules by key) in `preRun`, where Emscripten's allocator is still the bump allocator, so they land at the same memory and table bases as at build time whatever Node or workerd ran before (`meta.layout`, checked, and the restore falls back to a normal boot with a logged reason when anything differs), with their constructors held back (matplotlib's `ft2font` constructor needs a running interpreter; at build time they run after `main`), grows memory, streams the heap in, finishes Pyodide's bootstrap, maps the `dlopen` handles, writes the package files (the libraries as empty placeholders, since they are loaded), reopens the font file matplotlib keeps open on fd 3 and marks the packages loaded. Measured on Cloudflare (a Worker running `pythonServer` directly, 2026-10-27): a cold `print(1)` took 0.87 to 1.9 s of CPU and 3.5 to 4.0 s of wall time while the assets were cold, a cold isolate's matplotlib chart 1.18 s of CPU and 2.0 s at the client, and warm runs 11 to 30 ms; without the snapshot the same Worker took 4.0 to 5.0 s for a cold `print(1)` (once 11.5 s), 1.85 s more for numpy and 7.9 s more for a matplotlib chart, and on the live demo a cold `print(1)` took 14.5 s and a chart 17.0 s. The restored heap is 63 MB against 78 MB after importing them, and the package files hold 28.7 MB without their libraries. pandas is not in the snapshot (its 30 MiB would not fit beside them in 128 MB) and loads as before.

`nanocode-ts/machines` (`src/machines/`) is machine payments: agents that pay for their model and tools from a visitor's Tempo Wallet in MACH over MPP. `Wallet` connects Tempo Wallet with the Accounts SDK (`accounts`, `mpp: false`), generates a secp256k1 access key and has it approved with MACH and USDC.e limits and scopes for MACH, the swapper and the TIP-1034 escrow, keeps it in localStorage under `nanocode.machines.wallet`, publishes it on chain before anything pays with it, and logs in through the device-code adapter inside X's player (`configure({ name, relay, websiteUrl })`); `Payments.create` is the mppx client that pays (MACH sessions first, then machine-token and MACH charges, 0.05 MACH opening deposits) with a spend ledger per host and per MCP tool taken from receipts and challenges; `Payment.receipts` meters the `payment-receipt` SSE events that MPP proxies append to metered streams and removes them from the stream the runtime reads, because the Claude Messages parser fails the turn on them ("Messages protocol: SSE event name disagrees with data type", reproduced on 2026-10-24 under `wrangler dev` with a proxy shim that appends them; Codex's Responses parser ignores them. The runtime stops reading a model stream at its final event (`response.completed`, `message_stop`), before a proxy's trailing receipt arrives, so once a final event has passed, cancelling the stream `receipts` returns drains the rest of the source for its receipts (for at most 10 s) instead of cancelling it; and mppx's `payment.response` observers get a clone (a tee) of the response, whose body `account` and `Payments.create` cancel at once, because an unread clone keeps the source from ever being cancelled and buffers the whole stream. Before both, under `wrangler dev` of a `createMachines` Worker with a proxy shim that sends its receipt 300 ms after the stream (the runtime closed the connection 843 ms into a request, before it), every model request's closing credit was lost: one Luna and one Haiku turn showed 5,000 and 2,000 base units for 4,000 and 3,000 spent, and the last request's credit never showed at all; after them, a Luna and a Haiku turn that each called gateway kv, bucket and compute tools matched the shim's ledger to the unit (36,200 and 33,200).); `Mcp` recognizes MCP servers and their `-32042` and `payment-required` answers and strips headers their CORS refuses; `Models` lists the MPP model endpoints (`openai.mpp.tempo.xyz`, `anthropic.mpp.tempo.xyz`), routes MCP requests through the paying fetch (`agent`, or `route` for API-key inference) and turns payment errors into next steps (`explain`); `Cloud` is the browser transport to `createMachines`'s Worker (`POST v1/keys`, `sessions/<id>/turns`, `/events`, steer, resume, `AbortSignal`, the `v1/me` spend meter. `Cloud.meter` lowers a row when a receipt credits it (a poll that sees less spend and the same call count used to be skipped, so the page showed 9,500 for 8,500 spent), and `Cloud.logout` sends `DELETE v1/keys`, which `useMachine`'s `/logout` sends before it asks Tempo Wallet to revoke the key, so the Worker forgets the key even when the visitor dismisses the wallet (before, `/logout` only dropped the page's cloud token and the Worker kept paying with the key until it expired).); `createMachine({ history, model, payment, suggestion })` builds the store and the transport that sends every turn to the `createMachines` Worker through `Cloud`, over the link (token and session) that `Cloud.enable` stores in localStorage and `Cloud.restore()` reads back. The agent always runs in the Worker's durable session: on 2026-10-27 Georgios dropped the in-tab agent (`nanocode-ts/machines/local`), the `/cloud` and `/local` switch and `runtime.cloud`, so a machine page has one runtime and its hands attach the way the Cloudflare demo's do (`/hand`, `Cloud.hand` mints the session's `nch_` token with `POST sessions/<s>/hands`, `Cloud.hands` lists them, and `useMachine`'s `hand` is a `useHand` state for `HandConnect`). Payment is configured per surface with `payment: { inference: "tempo" | "apiKey", tools: "tempo" | "off", servers }` (`Payment.resolve`; both default to `tempo`, and `servers` defaults to Mercator, https://mercator.sh/mcp, and the Tempo gateway, https://gateway.tempo-dev.workers.dev/mcp, whenever tools are `tempo`; `false` or a list overrides them). With `inference: "apiKey"` (Worker hosts: `createMachines` with the host's `egress`, and `createWallets`) the chat works without a wallet: tools go through `Payment.locked`, which sends free requests (MCP `initialize`, `tools/list`) unpaid and answers a paid call's `402`, `-32042` or `payment-required` with a tool result that starts `tempo_wallet_required` and tells the model the user must connect, and after `/login` the same session's tool fetch switches to the paying one, so the tools listed at open start working without reopening. `nanocode-ts/machines/react` (`src/react/Machines.tsx`) holds `useMachine` (login, the key's publication to the Worker, `/wallet`, `/fund`, `/hand`, `/clear`, `/logout`, the Worker's spend meter and `hand`) and headless parts the skin styles (`src/react/Machines.styles.ts` in the nanocode skin): `Spend` (`spend*`), `Meter` (`meter*`), `Prompts` (`prompt`), `SignIn`, `Account`, and for API-key hosts `Unlock` (`unlock`, a one-line Connect affordance while tools are locked) and `WalletCard` (`walletCard*`), which `walletCards(machine)` renders under each transcript entry whose tool output needs a wallet through `Chat`'s `after(entry, index)` slot: Connect Tempo Wallet with what it unlocks and the key's limits, then Retry once connected. `nanocode-ts/machines/cloudflare` (`src/machines/cloudflare/`, checked and emitted with the Cloudflare configs) is the Worker side: `createMachines({ instructions, payment, fallback, ...createNanocode options })` returns `{ AgentSession, Wallet, fetch }` and serves `POST /v1/keys`, `/v1/me`, `/v1/secrets`, `/v1/files`, `/sessions/*`, the device-code relay `/api/device/{register,token}` and `/api/github/:owner/:repo/:ref`; `Wallet` is the Durable Object per account (`WALLETS`, sealed under `KEY_SECRET`, storage keys `key`, `token:`, `secret:`, `file:`, `spent:`, `calls:`, `label:`, `seen:`, `channel:`) that holds the key and pays every request, and without a key answers `/pay` through `Payment.locked`; `egress` sends `api.openai.com` and `api.anthropic.com` to the MPP proxies when inference is `tempo` (an `apiKey` host passes its own `egress`). Hosts export the classes under the names their `wrangler` config binds (`export const { AgentSession, Wallet } = machines`). The server side is platform-neutral in `src/machines/Account.ts` (`nanocode-ts/machines/server`, web APIs only): `account({ store, secret, servers, rpc, scripts })` is the key holder of one account over a `Store` (async `get`, `put`, `delete`, `list(prefix)` and `update(key, change)`, a read-modify-write the store makes atomic) with the storage keys above, the key sealed with AES-GCM under an HKDF of the host secret (`sealer`), the mppx paying fetch with its session channels in the store (`send("model" | "pay", target, init)`, which answers `/pay` through `Payment.locked` while the account has no key, so a session's tools start paying the moment a key arrives, without reopening it), the spend ledger, secrets, files and `logout` (deletes the key and its tokens, keeps channels and the ledger); `verify` checks a posted key against Tempo's AccountKeychain at `rpc` (`RPC` by default); `keys({ holder, account, rpc, extra })` is the HTTP surface as Request → Response functions (`register`, `me`, `logout`, `principal`, `caller`): registration stores the key under the host's `account(request, env)` when it names one, and otherwise under the wallet address with a bearer token `<address>.<64 hex>`; `visitor(request)` turns a browser's `x-nanocode-visitor` header (64 hex that `Keyring.visitor()` keeps in localStorage) into the account `visitor:<first 40 hex of its SHA-256>` for hosts with anonymous visitors; `device` relays Tempo Wallet's device-code login. The Cloudflare `Wallet` Durable Object is that core over `ctx.storage.kv` (same class name, storage keys and sealing, so deployed objects need no migration; `/v1/me` now also names the `account`), `createMachines` takes `account`, and `createWallets({ account, payment, scripts })` is what an API-key host spreads into its own `createNanocode`: `{ Wallet, fetch, nanocode: { mcp, codeMode } }`, where `fetch` answers `POST`/`GET`/`DELETE /v1/keys`, `GET /v1/me` and `/api/device/*` (undefined for anything else) and each session pays its MCP servers from the Wallet object of its authority's account. With `scripts: false`, its default, cells keep `githubArchives()` and only MCP tools pay, and `Payment.unlocks`, `explanation` and `locked` leave "every MPP service a script fetches" out. `MachinesEnv` takes an optional `TEMPO_RPC`. Every server-side fetch a cell, its bash (`curl`, `wget`, `git clone`) or a paid fetch makes reaches only public http(s) hosts, through `src/internal/Egress.ts` (`guard(fetcher, { resolve })`), because cells run on the server and a Vercel Function could otherwise reach its own runtime API at 127.0.0.1 or a cloud metadata service: `publicAddress` refuses loopback, unspecified, private, carrier-grade NAT (100.64/10), link-local (including 169.254.169.254), benchmarking, documentation, multicast and reserved IPv4 and, for IPv6, loopback, IPv4-compatible and IPv4-mapped addresses (checked as their IPv4), NAT64 and 6to4 with a non-public IPv4 inside, Teredo, unique-local (including AWS's fd00:ec2::254), link-local, site-local and multicast; names that end in `localhost`, `.local`, `.localdomain`, `.internal` (`metadata.google.internal`), `.intranet`, `.lan`, `.home.arpa` or the reverse zones, and dotless names (`http://metadata/`), are refused without a lookup; decimal, octal and hex IPv4 forms (`http://2130706433/`, `http://0x7f.1/`, `http://0177.0.0.1/`) arrive normalized by the URL parser and are checked as the address. A hostname is resolved and refused when any of its addresses is not public, and `guard` follows redirects itself (`redirect: "manual"` on every hop, at most 20, with the fetch spec's method and body rewrite, and `authorization`, `cookie` and `proxy-authorization` dropped across origins), checking every hop, so a public URL that redirects to http://127.0.0.1 is refused; a refusal is a 403 with `{"error": "fetch refused: <host> resolves to <address>, which is not a public address"}`. On Node (`src/vercel/internal/Egress.ts`, `egress(fetcher?)`) names resolve with `dns.lookup` (all addresses), and without a host fetcher the connection goes through undici's `fetch` with an `Agent` whose `connect.lookup` resolves again and refuses non-public answers, so the address checked is the address connected to and DNS rebinding cannot swap it; `nanocode-ts/vercel`'s harness wraps every cell fetch in it (`sessionFetch`, the static `fetch`, or that pinned fetch when the host passes neither, while bash still gets network only when the host passes a fetch), and `nanocode-ts/machines/vercel`'s accounts pay through it (`src/machines/vercel.ts` is checked with Node's types for this). A host's own static fetch is checked and its redirects followed, but not pinned. Workers have no raw DNS, so the platform-neutral default resolves with DNS over HTTPS (`doh()`, A and AAAA from https://cloudflare-dns.com/dns-query, cached per isolate for the answers' TTL, between 5 s and 300 s) and cannot pin the connection; there Cloudflare's network is the backstop: a Worker's outbound `fetch` cannot reach the machine it runs on or private networks, and Cloudflare documents error 1000 (DNS points to prohibited IP) for a hostname that resolves to a non-public address, which has not been measured on these deployments. `account({ fetch })` sends every paid, MCP, model and locked (`Payment.locked`) request through `guard`; `paying` and the Cloudflare `paying` are marked as already guarded (`mark`, so wrapping them again costs nothing), `nanocode-ts/cloudflare`'s runtime wraps a host's `codeMode.fetch` in `guard`, and `githubArchives` (also marked) follows redirects itself on servers and refuses a hop outside its GitHub allowlist. `publicTarget(url)` is the literal-only check, still exported from `nanocode-ts/machines/server`. Checked on 2026-10-24 from gak-9 under Node 26 (`egress()`) and `wrangler dev` (workerd: `guard` with `doh()`, and `account().send("pay")` without a key): localtest.me, 127.0.0.1.nip.io, 169.254.169.254.nip.io, 100.64.0.1.nip.io, http://2130706433/, http://0x7f.1/, http://[::ffff:127.0.0.1]/, metadata.google.internal and httpbin.org redirects to http://127.0.0.1:8799/ and to localtest.me all came back 403 before any connection, the pinned undici fetch alone refused 127.0.0.1.nip.io at connect, and api.github.com and a github.com archive (redirected to codeload) still answered 200, and a Haiku 5.5 turn of nanocode-vercel-durable's `npm run cli` (its `sessionFetch: wallets.fetch`, no key registered) got the same 403s from a cell's `fetch` and its bash `curl` while `git clone` of nanocodexyz/nanocode-ts still downloaded; DNS over HTTPS added about 80 to 250 ms to a host's first fetch in an isolate. A paid fetch without a `user-agent` gets `nanocode-ts`, because GitHub answers 403 to archive downloads without one and Workers send none: on 2026-10-24 `git clone` through the Cloudflare demo's paying fetch failed with HTTP 403 until then. `nanocode-ts/machines/vercel` (`src/machines/vercel.ts`) keeps the same core in the durable-harness session's own log: `docStore(sessions, sessionId)` is a `Store` over the session document `nanocode.wallet` (`sessions.doc` read through a 1 s cache, `updateDoc` for writes, within DOC_MAX_BYTES), so on Vercel the account is the chat session and the page registers the key with each session it opens, and each session opens its own payment channel; `createWallets({ sessions, secret, authorize, rpc })` returns `GET`, `POST` and `DELETE` route handlers taking `?sessionId=`, `device`, and `mcp` for `nanocode-ts/vercel`'s `createNanocode`, whose harness takes `mcp: { servers, fetch(sessionId) }` and sends dispatches without a provider (MCP requests) through it. Its `scripts` option (false by default) makes the wallet's messages name script fetches too, and the returned `fetch(sessionId)` is the session's paying fetch, which `nanocode-ts/vercel`'s `sessionFetch(sessionId)` gives the session's cells and their bash (it wins over the static `fetch`), so a cell's `fetch` and `curl` pay MPP services from the key registered with the session; without a key a 402 comes back as `tempo_wallet_required` and other responses pass through. In the browser, `Keyring.keyring({ url, headers, scope })` registers the connected key with the host once per scope and key, one request at a time (`scope` adds query parameters such as the Vercel session, and `nanocode-ts/vercel/transport`'s `open()` starts the session before the first prompt), and `useUnlock(keyring, { servers, scripts })` in `nanocode-ts/machines/react` is the cloud-transport counterpart of `useMachine`'s API-key path: `/login`, `/logout`, `/wallet` and `/fund` (`commands`, `handles`, `command`), registration on connect, on reload and on every conversation change, and the `Keyed` shape that `Unlock`, `WalletCard`, `walletCards` and `Prompts` take, so both demos show the in-tab flow's locked line, inline card and Retry. `mppx`, `viem`, `ox` and `accounts` are dependencies, imported only by these subpaths, and `undici` only by `nanocode-ts/vercel` and `nanocode-ts/machines/vercel`.

A machine's access key goes on Tempo with its first payment, not at /login (2026-10-28). /login used to send a separate transaction that published the key with its fee in USDC.e from the visitor's wallet, so a wallet holding MACH and too little USDC.e failed there ("Publishing the key needs a little USDC.e for its fee"). Now `Wallet.connect` keeps Tempo Wallet's signed `keyAuthorization` with the key, `Keyring.register` sends it to `POST /v1/keys` as `key_authorization`, and `Account.verify` accepts a key that is not on Tempo yet when `AccessKey.verify` finds that the authorization names the key and this chain, has not expired and carries the root account's signature (`SignatureEnvelope.verify` over `KeyAuthorization.getSignPayload`: secp256k1, P256 and WebAuthn roots; a revoked key is refused). The record keeps the authorization, and both payers, the server's `pay()` and the browser's `Wallet.signer`, build the key's account with viem's `KeyAuthorizationManager` holding it, as `accounts` does for its own keys: viem's Tempo chain attaches it to every transaction mppx prepares (`prepareTransactionRequest`, which mppx's charge pull mode and session channel operations use) until `getMetadata` shows the key on Tempo, so the first payment installs the key and the paid service's fee payer pays that fee. That needs services on mppx 0.16.0 or newer (wevm/mppx#1129, 2026-10-28), which removed the `allowKeyAuthorization` policy that refused a sponsored transaction carrying a `keyAuthorization`; an older one answers `keyAuthorization is not allowed`, which `Models.explain` names. The payers themselves run mppx 0.16.0 since 2026-10-29 (0.13.6 before); `accounts` 0.21 still depends on mppx ^0.12.0 for its own provider payments, which `Wallet` does not use, so `npm ls mppx` lists both. Before that release, a first version of this change (b20abcd) published the key in a transaction of its own through Tempo Wallet's relay, which sponsors only a signed-in wallet session's own account, and tempoxyz/wallet#1169 proposed sponsoring such publications; both were dropped for the mppx change. On 2026-10-28 `node output/lazy-key-check.ts` (a throwaway secp256k1 root and key against rpc.tempo.xyz) verified a signed authorization and refused it under another root or key, registered the pending key with its authorization (`spendPolicy` `limited`), refused the key without one, and prepared a first transaction with the key's account that carried its `keyAuthorization`, with a fee payer and with its own fee. `npm run check:types` and both emitting builds pass.

Who pays for a session's model requests is the session's `payer` (2026-11-09, Georgios: a per-thread choice between the site's key, the visitor's Tempo Wallet paying for inference over MPP, and the visitor's plan). `Contracts.Payer` is `deployment`, `wallet` or `plan`. On Cloudflare `App.create` takes `payers` (by default `deployment`, and `plan` with `plans` or `subscription`) and a turn's or warm's `payer`: it refuses one it does not offer with 400, binds the first accepted one to the session (`payer:<scope>` in the metadata, as the model is, so another payer later answers 409 `session payer is immutable`), passes it to the egress as `ModelDispatch.payer` and `SocketDispatch.payer`, and keys the resident runtime on it. `deployment` and `wallet` never use a plan, so a signed-in visitor's subscription no longer pays for a session that chose otherwise; `plan` answers 403 unless the visitor's plan serves the model (`sign in to your Claude plan first, or choose another payer`); a session without a payer keeps the automatic rule, under which the plan pays when it is signed in and serves the model. `Wallets.create(...).egress(base)` (`nanocode-ts/machines/cloudflare`) wraps the deployment's egress and sends the `/model` and `/socket` requests whose dispatch says `wallet` to `machines/cloudflare/Egress.ts`, which pays them from the account's `Wallet` object through `openai.mpp.tempo.xyz` and `anthropic.mpp.tempo.xyz`; its socket answers 426, so Codex falls back to HTTPS. On Vercel `App.create` takes `payers` (by default `deployment`, `plan` with a subscription and `wallet` with `wallet`) and `payerHarnesses`, the models each payer serves, and sets the session's `config.nanocode.payer` at start; `Harness` uses no plan for `deployment` or `wallet`, fails a drive whose plan has signed out, and sends a `wallet` session's requests to `Gateway.walletEndpoint(provider)` through `wallet(sessionId)`, which `Wallets.create(...).model` (`nanocode-ts/machines/vercel`) provides: the session's key pays with `Account.send('model', ...)`, which forwards only `accept`, `content-type`, `anthropic-version` and `anthropic-beta` and refuses any other origin. The transports send `payer` (Cloudflare's `Model.payer` with turns and warms; Vercel's `Selection.payer` at session start, also part of the warm key). `Agent` (`nanocode-ts/react/agent`) takes `walletHarnesses` and puts `payer` in its selection: `/pay` and a Paid by button choose this deployment, Tempo Wallet or a plan; the models offered follow the payer (`harnesses`, `walletHarnesses`, or `planHarnesses` for the providers signed in); the payer is bound to a conversation with its model, so choosing another starts a new session; signing in to a plan makes it the payer of new sessions; choosing Tempo Wallet connects it first; and a turn paid by the wallet registers the key before it is sent (`before` in the clones). On 2026-11-09 in local workerd (durable-cloudflare's bundle, `output/payer-check.mjs` there, a scripted provider, no network) an unknown payer answered 400, `plan` without a sign-in 403, `deployment` completed with one provider request, a second payer in that session 409, `wallet` without a key failed with the wallet's `payment_error` (`no access key is registered for this account, or it expired: connect Tempo Wallet again`) and no provider request, a warm with `wallet` 204, and a turn without a payer completed as before; durable-cloudflare's `npm run drill` (`charge` and `waiting` with one charge each, `deploys`) and durable-vercel's (all eight runs) passed on this build.

Diagrams are Ondine's (wevm/ondine; Georgios, 2026-11-09: "make sure we're using ondine for our diagrams"). `Diagram` from `nanocode-ts/react/site` takes `svg`, an SVG that Ondine compiled from Mermaid, and shows it with the site's Ondine colors (`--ondine-bg` transparent, `--ondine-fg` the text color, `--ondine-line` the `middle` color), as nanocodexyz/nanocode-site's docs do; it used to take box-drawing text in a `pre`. The SVG is compiled when the host builds, so pages ship no Mermaid parser: durable-cloudflare imports `.mmd` files through an esbuild plugin around `ondine/compiler`, and durable-vercel renders its page's source with `ondine/compiler` while Next.js prerenders it.

A scoped key's authorization can be too heavy for the service's fee payer (2026-11-08). On gakonst/personal's production Wallet, a fresh key from nanocode-swift's `Wallet.connect` (secp256k1, MACH and USDC.e limits, seven call scopes) was refused on every payment with "fee-sponsored transaction gas exceeds sponsor policy": the first transaction carried the authorization and needed 9.6M gas for MAI-Transcribe-2's charge and 8.8M for OpenAI's session channel, over what microsoft-ai.mpp.tempo.xyz and openai.mpp.tempo.xyz sponsor, so the key never reached Tempo (`getMetadata` still answered the zero address). The server's `pay()` now lands a pending key itself before its first payment: when `getMetadata` doesn't show the key, `land` sends one transaction of its own that carries the authorization, `approve(spender, 0)` on the token of the key's approve scope (its first allowed recipient; MACH and the account itself for an unscoped key), paying its own fee in the first of pathUSD, USDC.e and MACH that the account holds and that works (at Tempo's 0.6 gwei base fee, 9.6M gas is about $0.006; Tempo answered a MACH fee with "insufficient liquidity in FeeAMM pool to swap fee tokens for user token" on 2026-11-08, so MACH comes last). A failure names the node's reason for each fee token. The landing runs when the first payment is made (both mppx methods' `createCredential` wait for it), not when the payer is built: in a68090e it ran in `pay()`, so a key that couldn't land failed every request the payer forwards, including the paid MCP servers' free `initialize`, and on gakonst/personal every new session's runtime failed to open ("MCP initialize failed: … HTTP 402") and its turns retried for 5 hours. A failed landing is tried again on the next payment. A key whose scopes allow no approve answers that it can't be landed. Later payments are sponsored as before, since viem stops attaching the authorization once `getMetadata` shows the key. `Wallet.signer` in the browser still relies on the fee payer.

An MPP session channel that reaches `maxDeposit` rolls over to a new channel (2026-11-09). mppx 0.16.0 refuses a voucher whose cumulative amount passes the session method's `maxDeposit` (`requested voucher amount 2010000 exceeds local maxDeposit 2000000`). It kept planning vouchers on the stored channel, so every later request to that payee failed with 402 `payment_error`, as gakonst/personal's OpenAI transcription fallback did on 2026-11-08. The payer's `onChallenge` now deletes a stored channel of the challenge's payee and token before mppx plans the credential, when the channel's cumulative amount plus the request's amount, or the server's `sessionSnapshot.requiredCumulative` for that channel, passes `maxDeposit`. mppx then opens a new channel with the 0.2 MACH opening deposit. The old channel keeps its unspent deposit in escrow until it is closed, and since top-ups stop at `maxDeposit` that is less than the refused request's amount. `output/mpp-rollover.ts` gave a payer a stored channel at 2,000,000 for one payee and one at 500,000 for another, and a mock service that answers a 0.01 session challenge for the first payee. With HEAD's Account.ts the payer answered 402 with that error and kept the channel. With this tree it deleted that channel, kept the other, and went on to open a new channel, which failed only at the unreachable RPC of the run.

A Tempo Wallet pays for every model the Tempo gateway serves, listed as nanocode's TUI lists them (2026-11-10, Georgios: "if a user has connected their tempo wallet they can access all the models we got in the gateway", "with the right way we have them in the nanocode tui code"). Wallet sessions sent Codex models to `openai.mpp.tempo.xyz` and Claude models to `anthropic.mpp.tempo.xyz`, and each host offered a fixed list of five. `nanocode-ts/machines` now has `Tempo`: `Tempo.gateway` (https://gateway.tempo-dev.workers.dev), `Tempo.endpoint` (`/v1`, Responses) and `Tempo.anthropic` (`/v1/messages`), which `Models.endpoint` and `Models.anthropic`, `Wallets.egress` on Cloudflare (`api.openai.com` and `api.anthropic.com` both go to the gateway, path kept), the Vercel `Wallets.model` dispatch and `Gateway.walletEndpoint` now name. `Tempo.list(models, { fetch, signal })` is a port of `bin/nanocode/src/tempo.rs` at nanocode 09bb9a5d5 (`gateway_offers`, `rows`, `alias`, `price`): it reads `/api/auction/models` (no payment, CORS `*`, `max-age=300`), skips `boundless/` ids as the TUI does (it reaches Boundless directly, which this port does not), keeps the first entry of each id, maps an upstream whose last segment is a direct Codex model or, with dots as dashes, a Claude model to that model's own id, prices it at its `vercel/` route, else its `openrouter/` one, and keeps one row per provider and model, preferring the one named for its provider. It throws the TUI's messages when the listing fails, is not a model list, names an entry without an id or provider, or lists nothing. The result is `Tempo.Harness[]`, the shape `Pickers.Harness` and nds's `Agent` take: Claude and Codex first with the catalog's models the gateway serves under their own ids (names, efforts and defaults from `nanocode-ts/models`), then `Tempo · <provider>` for every other model on the Codex harness, which the SDK runs as a custom model (every thinking level, `low` by default: `Tempo.efforts`), each with `$<input>/$<output> per 1M input/output tokens`. The TUI lets such a model follow the current harness; a picker entry has one harness, and the gateway's Responses route served each one tried. `Pickers.models` shows a model's `detail` and leaves out a name that repeats the id. On Vercel a `wallet` session without `payerHarnesses.wallet` takes any harness, model and thinking level (`served` in `App.ts`), so the gateway, not a copy of its list, decides what it serves. On 2026-11-10 `output/models-check.ts` listed 684 models in 476 ms (8 Claude, 4 Codex, 31 workers-ai, 376 openrouter, 265 vercel, ids unique), the same count as a Python port of `rows` run on the same listing, and `output/gateway-check.ts` (this build's module in Node 22.23, `lookup` as a host tool, requests paid by nanocode's MPP proxy from the TUI's wallet) completed a turn with a tool call on each of `gpt-6-luna`, `vercel/alibaba/qwen-3-14b`, `@cf/zai-org/glm-5.3-flash` and `openrouter/meta/muse-ember` on the Codex harness and `claude-haiku-5-5` on the Claude harness, all through the gateway.

The picker aligns each group's labels to that group's longest label (2026-11-10), where it used the longest label of the whole list. With the Tempo gateway's 684 models in one picker, the longest id (an `openrouter/` one) made the label column about 60ch wide, so on nanocode.xyz's home page at 1440 px the Claude rows showed `Claude Opus 5.5 · $4…`. Each run of choices between two headers now has its own width, so the Claude and Codex groups show their name, price and thinking levels, and the long-id groups their price (headless Chromium against the site's `pnpm preview`, `output/picker-shot.mjs` there).

A model that the SDK does not know runs on the Claude harness too (2026-11-10, Georgios: "Claude themed demo should also show the other models"). `Tempo.list(models, { harness })` puts the `Tempo · <provider>` groups on `harness` (`codex` unless set), so a Claude-only picker lists them. On the Claude harness each such entry carries `maxTokens: Tempo.outputTokens`, 32,768, the TUI's `Tempo::OUTPUT_TOKENS`, which it sets on a Claude request whose model has no known output limit: the SDK refuses such a model without one (`Unknown Claude model: configure max_tokens explicitly`). On Vercel a wallet session on the Claude harness whose model does not start with `claude-` gets that `max_tokens`. On 2026-11-10 `output/gc-claude-custom.ts` (`output/gateway-check.ts` with `MAX_TOKENS`) completed a turn with a tool call through the gateway's `/v1/messages` on `openrouter/meta/muse-ember`, `vercel/alibaba/qwen-3-14b` and `@cf/zai-org/glm-5.3-flash`, and without `max_tokens` the open failed with that error. `createMachines`' GitHub archive requests send the user agent `nanocode-ts`.

The module builds against nanocode 209406cc3 (2026-11-10, from 85d570598), whose Code Mode `image()` takes the array that Claude's `Read` returns for an image file and whose exec description says to show one with `image(await tools.Read({ file_path }))` (Codex: `tools.view_image({ path })`). On the demos a Python chart never reached the chat: Haiku 5.5 base64-encoded the PNG through `Bash`, whose nested output stops at 128 KiB, so the data URL ended early and the SDK answered `image content omitted because it could not be processed`. `output/image-check.ts` (this module, a scripted model, the shell with an 1800×1200 PNG in `/workspace`) showed the old path come back 131,072 characters long with `truncated: true`, and `image(await tools.Read(...))` reach the next Claude request as an image block; `output/gateway-check.ts` still completed tool-calling turns on `gpt-6-luna` and `claude-haiku-5-5` through the Tempo gateway. The SDK moved 66 commits; `Cargo.lock` takes their two new dependency edges.

A `git clone` into a `SqlFileSystem` with `sources` keeps each file's bytes out of the database (2026-11-10, Georgios: "things feel slow in the demos ... took like 30s to clone a simple thing this has to be a bug"). On the Vercel demo a vercel/ai clone (10,064 files, 90.9 MiB) took 15.0, 24.0 and 73.9 s in three turns that day, where the archive's download took 2.9 s: the clone waited for the journal, whose drive report read `batches: 18, batch_wait_ms: 44944` for that 73.9 s one, since each batch is a Workflow step and the brotli'd 37 MB uploaded at about 2 MB/s (brotli quality 3 to 6 and zstd all gave 35 to 37 MB, so compression could not fix it), and every later turn of the session replayed it (a `pwd` turn took 5.1 to 5.8 s after the clone and 3.8 to 4.1 s in a session without it). The clone knows each file's commit from the archive's pax header, so it now gives every `NewFile` a `source`, `github:<owner>/<repo>@<commit>/<path>` (`Sources.github`). A `SqlFileSystem` created with `sources` (`Sources.create({ fetch, archive, token, maxBytes, maxFileBytes })`) stores such a file as a row without data plus a row of `nanocode_file_sources` (path, source, size), and its stat, list and rename see that size and path; a read asks `sources.read(source, size)`, which serves the bytes the clone primed in this process, or downloads the commit's archive once (codeload, or the host's `archive`), keeps its files in a per-process cache of at most `GitHub.maxBytes` (200 MiB, the clone's own limit) that drops the least recently used archive, and refuses a file whose size differs from the one cloned. Writing or removing such a file drops its source; appending to it reads it first. Without `sources` a filesystem stores the bytes as before, so Cloudflare (whose 128 MB isolate could not hold an archive's files) and browsers keep eager clones; a database that holds sources cannot be read by an earlier build, which sees those files as empty. `nanocode-ts/vercel` creates both the session's and the file route's filesystem with `Sources.create({ fetch: Egress.guard() })` and the session's GitHub token. Measured on 2026-11-10 with `output/clone-bench.ts` (Node 22.23, `node:sqlite`, vercel/ai at eaeb37e): an eager clone took 3.0 to 3.7 s and wrote 92 MiB in 3,464 transactions, a lazy one 3.0 to 4.1 s and 1 MiB, `head` and `md5sum` of its `package.json` right after matched the eager copy without a download, and in a new process over the same database `find | wc -l` listed 10,068 files and the same `md5sum` came back after one archive download.

`git clone` speaks git's protocol v2 to github.com when the host names no `github.archive` (2026-11-10, Georgios: "fix all dem bugs slow = bug amke faster"). The Cloudflare demo's own prompt, Clone wevm/viem and list its five largest source files, took 69.0 s on 2026-11-10, 48.8 s of it in the clone: GitHub's archive of viem is 189 MB, mostly test vectors of 33 to 60 MiB that the clone then leaves out (it keeps 49.4 MiB of 3,224 files), and the Durable Object downloaded it in 15.5 s and spent the rest inflating it. A shallow fetch with `filter blob:limit=<maxFileBytes>` (`ls-refs` for the ref, then `fetch` with `deepen 1`, `ofs-delta`, `no-progress`, `done`, `src/bash/internal/GitPack.ts`) sends a pack of 11.4 MB for viem and 41.4 MB for vercel/ai (whose archive is 40.5 MB), with the same files. Pack objects are zlib streams that end where they end, which `DecompressionStream` does not report, so `src/bash/internal/Inflate.ts` inflates them in TypeScript (stored, fixed and dynamic blocks, a 10-bit first-level table, exact end offsets; 1,208 cases against `node:zlib` at levels 0, 1, 6 and 9, 605 MiB/s of text output) and `GitPack` resolves OFS and REF deltas with a base cache of one maximal file (`maxFileBytes`), names each object by its SHA-1 (which is the snapshot's blob id, so the clone no longer hashes files), keeps the pack in segments of `maxFileBytes` (joining two only for an object that straddles them), and walks the commit's tree once its trees have arrived, so blobs need not wait for the whole pack. Symlinks and submodules are skipped and counted, a blob the filter left out is listed as larger than the limit, and the commit object gives the snapshot's author, date and message, so the clone no longer asks api.github.com. `GitHub.archives` lets cells and the shell POST to `https://github.com/OWNER/REPO.git/git-upload-pack`, forwarding the body. Hosts with `github.archive`, such as nanocode.xyz's browser agent (github.com sends no CORS headers), keep the archive, and `Sources` re-reads lazy files through the same fetch. Checked on 2026-11-10 in Node 22.23 with `output/clone-compare.ts` (each repository cloned both ways through `GitHub.archives()`): viem 3.0 s against 10.8 to 15.0 s, nanocodexyz/nds 0.9 s against 1.9 s, vercel/ai 5.4 to 7.6 s against 4.9 to 6.6 s, and for each the same paths, sizes, blob ids and left-out files; a lazy viem clone read back its `package.json` (md5 b891a252…) in a new process after one 11.4 MB fetch. The machine ran at a load average of 110 to 190 from other builds, so these are upper bounds.

The Cloudflare `Python` object keeps mirrored files in its own SQLite and reads each one into Pyodide only when Python opens it (2026-11-10). With a vercel/ai clone (10,068 files, 90.9 MiB) in the working directory, `python3` on the Cloudflare demo failed after 88.1 s with `the Python service kept losing this workspace's files`: one Pyodide per isolate held every mirrored file in MEMFS beside its 63 MB heap, past the isolate's 128 MB, so the object reset on every upload. `pythonServer`'s state takes `files` (`PythonFiles`: `put`, `get`, `clear`, synchronous, which a Durable Object's `ctx.storage.sql` is), and with it `place` stores each uploaded file under a key of its own and makes its MEMFS node lazy: the node's `usedBytes` is the file's size, so `stat`, `os.walk` and `os.path.getsize` read no contents, and its `contents` property loads the bytes from the store on first use and becomes a plain property once Python writes the file. Entering another mirror clears the object's store; a parked mirror holds its own copy. `nanocode-ts/cloudflare/python` stores the files in a `mirrored` table in parts of `Sql.valueLimitBytes`. Checked on 2026-11-10 in Node with `output/pyserver-check.ts` (Pyodide 314.0.7, the shell's remote Python over `pythonServer`, a vercel/ai clone): an `os.walk` count and a `getsize` sort of every file read no file from the store, and reading `ai/package.json` read one.

The shell sends the Python service files as bytes (2026-11-10). A remote `python3` request marks itself `binary`, carries each file as a `Uint8Array`, which a Durable Object RPC clones as it is, and the service answers that request's files as bytes too; one without the flag (an older shell during a deploy) still sends and gets base64. Base64 had been the largest cost of the first `python3` in a big tree: with a vercel/ai clone in the working directory, `output/pyserver-check.ts` (Node 22.23, a load average of 75 to 95) spent 3.6 s of CPU in the shell's `base64` and 3.9 s in garbage collection, and that run took 5.0 s against 2.5 s with bytes.

The Python service keeps a mirror's first `PARKED_BYTES` (32 MiB, the budget it already parks mirrors in) in MEMFS and only the rest in the object's store (2026-11-10). On the Cloudflare demo the first `python3` in a vercel/ai clone took 20.6 to 26.0 s with every file going to SQLite, of which the `Python` object's RPC calls spent 15.6 s of wall time and 10.1 s of CPU (`wrangler tail`, 18 calls), so a small workspace no longer pays SQLite at all. `place` also creates each parent directory once per request and each lazy node through one `open`, and stamps a file from the bytes it received instead of reading it back: in Node `output/pyserver-check.ts` kept 3,130 of the clone's files in MEMFS and stored 6,945, and its first `python3` took 2.6 s.

The Python service keeps a mirror's first `PARKED_BYTES` (32 MiB, the budget it already parks mirrors in) in MEMFS and only the rest in the object's store (2026-11-10). On the Cloudflare demo the first `python3` in a vercel/ai clone took 20.6 to 26.0 s with every file going to SQLite, of which the `Python` object's RPC calls spent 15.6 s of wall time and 10.1 s of CPU (`wrangler tail`, 18 calls), so a small workspace no longer pays SQLite at all. `place` also creates each parent directory once per request and each lazy node through one `open`, and stamps a file from the bytes it received instead of reading it back: in Node `output/pyserver-check.ts` kept 3,130 of the clone's files in MEMFS and stored 6,945, and its first `python3` took 2.6 s.

`nanocode-ts/sandbox/vercel` and `nanocode-ts/sandbox/cloudflare` (`src/sandbox/`) are `WorkspaceHost`s over real Linux machines, for hosts whose agents outgrow the simulated `Bash` (no processes, interpreter-only WebAssembly on Workers, Pyodide-only Python): a host passes one as the runtime's `workspace` with `/workspace` as `config.workspace`, and its `instructions` replace the simulated shell's in Code Mode. Both run each command as `timeout --kill-after=5 <timeout_ms> bash -c <command>` in the request's directory, so a timeout or an aborted call (SIGTERM to `timeout`, which signals the command's process group) also stops what the command started, keep at most `outputLimit` bytes of each stream (4 MiB) and set `truncated`, and add `command timed out after N s` to stderr. `vercelSandbox({ name, credentials?, leaseMs, persistent, image, vcpus, env, files, networkPolicy })` opens the Vercel Sandbox (`@vercel/sandbox`, an optional peer dependency, with the Vercel OIDC token or `credentials`) named `name` with `Sandbox.getOrCreate`, creates and chowns `/workspace` and seeds `files` only when it creates it, keeps its timeout at least `leaseMs` (5 minutes) plus the command's timeout ahead with `extendTimeout`, every `leaseMs / 2` while a command runs, and is `persistent` by default, so a stopped or timed-out sandbox resumes with its files on the next call; `stop()` stops it. `containerWorkspace(ctx.container, { image, instance, setup, inactivityTimeoutMs, enableInternet, env, files })` runs in the Durable Object that owns the container (`containers` with `scheduling_policy: "durable_object"` on that class, which can be the session object itself): it starts `image` (`cloudflare/debian-trixie`) with `sleep infinity` when it is not running, sets the inactivity timeout (10 minutes; Cloudflare forgets it when the object restarts, so every boot sets it again), and runs `setup` once per instance, by default `apt-get install` of git, python3, pip, curl, procps and xz, which the managed image lacks; files go through `exec` (`cat`, `rm -rf`), since `exec` passes only `PATH` from the start options, every process gets `HOME=/root`, `LANG` and `DEBIAN_FRONTEND` plus `env`. Measured on 2026-10-24 from gak-9 through the compiled modules: on Vercel (Ubuntu 26.04, 2 vCPUs, 4 GB, git, Python 3.14, Node 24, passwordless sudo) creating a sandbox and running the first command took 1.9 s, a warm command 0.6 to 0.9 s, a full `git clone` of wevm/viem (432 MB of history) 12 s, `pip install --user` 1.1 s, installing wasmtime and running a WASI module 2.6 s, reading or writing a file 0.16 to 0.37 s, and a persistent sandbox resumed after `stop()` in 1.9 s with its files and pip packages; on Cloudflare (`standard-1`, Debian 13, 7.5 GB disk) a new instance started in 0.3 s, the default setup took 28.5 s, a warm command 6 to 80 ms, the same clone 41 s, a file read or write 6 to 56 ms, and a 2 s timeout returned 124 in 2.0 s with no `sleep` left running.
