A host that needs the tools at once builds its own image with them instead of the default setup.

`nanocode-ts/machines`'s `Wallet` and `Payments` load `accounts`, `viem`, `ox` and `mppx` with dynamic imports on first use (`Wallet.preload()`, which `useUnlock` and `useMachine` call on mount, fetches them when the page is idle), so a page that imports `nanocode-ts/machines` or `nanocode-ts/machines/react` ships none of them in its first bundle: with this and its tab hand loaded on demand, the Cloudflare demo's `app.js` and its static imports went from 2,522 KB to 325 KB (minified, 2026-10-24) and its first contentful paint on local workerd from 480-640 ms to 240-276 ms. `Wallet.reader`, `signer` and `fund` are async; `fundingUrl` builds the Tempo Wallet link itself.

`EventHub` keeps a tail of recent contiguous events for readers, wakes waiters and serves a finite or open Server-Sent Events stream from pages the host reads. `new EventHub(retained, retainedBytes)` bounds the tail by both the host's event count and the events' total encoded bytes from `published`, dropping the oldest events first, so a reader behind the tail pages from the log; one event above the byte bound leaves the tail empty. `node --expose-gc scripts/measure-events.ts` publishes 128 events of 300 B, 4 KiB, 64 KiB and 1 MiB into a hub with the bounds in `EVENT_PAGE_SIZE` and `EVENT_TAIL_BYTES` (128 and 4 MiB by default) and writes the heap each tail retains to `output/events-measurement.json`: on 2026-10-20 in Node 22.23, a count-only tail of 128 1 MiB events retained 134,266,928 bytes, more than an isolate's 128 MB, and 8,437,864 for 64 KiB events, while with the 4 MiB bound the 1 MiB tail kept 3 events in 3,146,448 bytes and the 64 KiB tail 63 in 4,152,544, and the 300 B and 4 KiB tails kept all 128 in 129,288 and 583,032 bytes; parsed event text took about one byte of heap per encoded byte. `Timings` aggregates span durations.

`config.live_deltas` (the Cloudflare runtime sets it unless `liveDeltas: false`) keeps streamed reply and tool-input deltas out of the session log: the SDK passes them to `HostOptions.live` with an `offset` and logs only `assistant.message` and `tool.call`, plus one merged delta when a run or attempt ends first. `EventHub.live` keeps each unfinished item's text (`a:<item_id>`, `t:<call_id>`) until its final or a merged delta is published or its turn ends, bounded at 4 MiB. A stream or watch socket opened with `live=1` gets them: on every pull, after the log events, one `event: live` frame per item with the text it has not sent, without `id` or `seq`, so a new connection first gets each unfinished item from offset 0 and the cursor stays on the log. Without `live=1` a stream carries only log events, as nanocode-cloudflare-durable's measure-faults chaos check expects. Both browser clients ask for it: `nanocode-ts/cloudflare/transport` and `nanocode-ts/machines`'s cloud mode (`src/machines/Cloud.ts`, its `turns` and `events` requests). `liveEvent` keeps the `offset`, maps `assistant.message` to a `text` event at offset 0 and gives no `seq` to unlogged events, and `Transcript` applies only the part of an item it has not received (saved as `received` in its state, from what it consumed), so replays, reconnects and reloads show each reply once; the Vercel harness drops `assistant.message` since it logs its deltas. A `seq` of 0 made `Transcript` drop live chunks of equal length as duplicates. Measured 2026-10-25 in Node (`output/rows.mjs`, 3 turns, 20 chunks 50 ms apart): rows per turn of 3 cells and a reply 89.7 to 49.7 (Claude) and 95.3 to 52.7 (Codex), a reply alone 32.3 to 22; Miniflare with the Cloudflare demo, 56 to 51.7 with unpaced replies. `WorkerInbox` also answers `live_progress` with true (2026-11-04), so `model.call.started` and `model.attempt.started` go to `HostOptions.live` and not to the log. No module or demo reads them, and `EventHub.live` skips events that are not streamed deltas, so clients no longer receive them. `model.call.completed` and the attempt failure and retry events stay logged. Measured with `output/vs-agents/rows-nc.ts` (Node 22.23, the agents-vs-nanocode mock, 20 turns each, SDK 40588ecad): a text turn wrote 17.1 rows instead of 19.1, and a turn with one cell and one tool call 27.3 instead of 31.3. `output/live-e2e.mjs` drives the real runtime, `EventHub` and `Transcript` with steady, reconnecting, reloading and late clients: 40 runs rendered every reply and cell once, and a stream broken mid-reply renders the same as with logged deltas.

Subagent deltas are live-only too (2026-11-07, SDK 838c13936). `TurnInbox` routed only the root agent's events, so with `live_deltas` every subagent `tool.input.delta`, `reasoning.summary.delta`, `assistant.delta` and its `model.call.started` and `model.attempt.started` were logged (coalesced at `event_coalesce_ms`): tempoxyz/gateway's Swarms lead f3a42cf87339 logged about 6,000 of 8,000 events in 20 minutes as subagent deltas, and its session hit the isolate's memory limit reopening (`GET /internal/events?after=97607`). Streams are now keyed by scope (the root, or a subagent's root session and agent ID): a subagent's reply and tool-input deltas go to `HostOptions.live` with an offset and its finals stay logged, as the root's do; its reasoning deltas go live and are logged as one merged delta per reasoning block right before the subagent's next final item or `model.call.completed`, so a reloaded transcript keeps the reasoning in place; a scope's boundary (its run end, a failed attempt) flushes only that scope's streams. The root's reasoning deltas stay logged. `EventHub` keys live streams by the same scope (`s:<root session>:<agent id>:` before the item key, `r:` for reasoning) and rewrites a subagent envelope's inner payload, so `events?live=1` carries subagent deltas as `event: live` frames; `/sessions/<s>/subagents/<n>/events`, which streams logged events only, shows a subagent's final items and merged reasoning but no streamed deltas. `output/swarm/run.ts` with `SCENARIO=swarm` (a Codex lead spawning 4 subagents that each make 6 model calls with 60 reasoning deltas and a 6 KB tool input in 40-byte deltas 8 ms apart, `event_coalesce_ms: 100`, `stream_tool_input: true`), build-0b6a628 against this build: 4,647 logged events (3,008 subagent `tool.input.delta`, 1,440 `reasoning.summary.delta`) against 163 (24 merged reasoning deltas), 221 `log_chunks` rows against 42, SQL writes 603 against 426 and 6.08 MB bound against 3.78 MB (the rest is subagent checkpoints), a 4.13 MB database against 1.57 MB; reopening and paging every event took the module from 3.9 MB to 7.6 MB against 6.2 MB and grew the process by 61 MB against 16 MB. Killed 50 s into a 40-step run and reopened: 5,065 events paged (wasm 8.7 MB, RSS +82 MB) against 137 (6.4 MB, +18 MB), against `RUNTIME_MEMORY_BUDGET_BYTES` of 64 MiB in tempoxyz/gateway.

The Rust crate is host glue around the SDK. `src/wasm/bridge.rs` is the ABI and its single-threaded `LocalPool` executor. The module imports `host.dispatch(id, pointer, length)`, `dispatch_body(id, pointer, length, body, body_length)`, `cancel(id)`, `clock(monotonic)` in milliseconds, `calendar_date(pointer, length)` as the year, month and day of today in an IANA timezone named by UTF-8 text, as one number (year × 10,000 + month × 100 + day) or 0 for an unknown timezone, `random(pointer, length)`, `reply(id, pointer, length)` and `panicked(pointer, length)`, and exports `memory`, `allocate`, `command`, `complete`, `complete_bytes` and `poll`; QuickJS's C is linked against wasi-libc, so the module also imports `wasi_snapshot_preview1`'s `clock_time_get`, `fd_write`, `fd_close`, `fd_fdstat_get` and `fd_seek`, which `AgentRuntime` answers with the host clock and discards output; requests and replies are JSON, and request bodies, model response chunks, socket frames and SQL replies are raw bytes. A `store` or `log` reply is the statements' rows as little-endian `u32` counts of statements, rows and cells, each cell a `u32` byte length (`u32::MAX` for null) followed by its UTF-8 text, so record text crosses without JSON escaping, unescaping or an intermediate `Value`: in `measure-cold.mjs` on 2026-10-20, opening a Claude session with a 2.1 MB checkpoint took a median 14.6 ms instead of 21 ms with its code compiled and 36 ms instead of 42.6 ms in a fresh process, and a Codex session with 3.9 MB of records 25.3 ms instead of 30.5 ms and 59 ms instead of 63 ms. A command is externally tagged, `{"<command>": {fields}}`, so serde reads its fields straight from the request instead of buffering the whole request into its `Content` tree to find an internal `op` tag; on 2026-10-20, built in one directory layout from SDK 139e30458, that removed 82 KB of symbolized `Content` code and shrank the module from 8,345,317 to 8,314,227 bytes (2,530,014 to 2,529,519 gzipped), with `measure-node.mjs` turn times unchanged within run-to-run variation. Each command runs as its own boxed future, so a command compiles only its own code: when one `execute` future inlined every command's future, the first reader command on a restored session made V8 compile 98,633 bytes of function bodies against 59,353 now, and on 2026-10-20 in `measure-cold.mjs` (20-turn sessions with 20 KB replies, 7 rounds, two interleaved runs) that first reader command took a median 3.5 and 3.2 ms instead of 4.3 and 4.1 ms for Claude and 3.3 and 3.3 ms instead of 4.2 and 4.1 ms for Codex, while the function bodies compiled before the first model request fell from 1,325,158 to 1,300,446 bytes for Claude and from 1,475,944 to 1,451,232 for Codex. `src/wasm.rs` is the command table (`configure`, `init`, `submit`, `append`, `turn`, `pending`, `cancel`, `interrupted`, `retry`, `finish`, `page`, `import`, `drive`, `attach`, `detach`, `settle`, `preconnect`, `settings`, `steer`, `interrupt_subagent`, `close_subagent`, `compact`, `completion_origin`, `replace`, `move_queued`, `dispose`, `catalog`, `admit`, `export_branch`, `export_page`, `import_branch`, `import_page`, `publish_branch`, `import_events`): `init` answers a configuration the SDK refuses as a rejection rather than an error, and it builds the runtime with `PLACEMENT_INSTRUCTIONS` when Code Mode has an executor or hands, opens the SDK's `TurnInbox` and maps the commands onto it and onto `SessionLog`. `src/wasm/store.rs` is the SDK's `Sql` host over the `store` and `log` host calls. `src/wasm/transport.rs` implements `nanocode::http::Sender` over `http_start` and `http_read` for both backends, passing `origin::current()` with `http_start`, `socket_open` and `socket_send`, Claude through `SenderTransport` and Codex HTTPS through `host::send_http`, each over its own `WorkerTransport` that names its `provider` in `http_start` and `socket_open`, with `src/wasm/mcp.rs`'s MCP requests over one that names none, and opens Codex sockets over `socket_open`, `socket_send`, `socket_next`, `socket_wait` and `socket_close`. `src/wasm/events.rs` is the SDK's `InboxHost`: it forwards logged batches over `events`, adds `host_timing`, asks for a finished turn's fields over `turn_finished` and selects each turn's hand with `select_prompt_hand`. `src/wasm/tools.rs` registers host tools as the SDK's `FunctionTool` with a `FunctionHandler` over `tool_call`, which sends the `ToolContext`'s `session_id`, `call_id`, `turn_id`, `model` and `instruction_revision` as `context`. `src/wasm/workspace.rs` implements the SDK's process contracts `FileHost`, `ForegroundExecutor` and `ShellExecutor` over `file_read`, `file_write`, `file_remove` and `shell_run`, limiting output with the SDK's `redact_and_limit`. `src/wasm/remote.rs` is the `RemoteLink` for the SDK's `RemoteClient` over `remote_catalog`, `remote_send`, `remote_poll` and `remote_close`; it marks batches that hold a request `RemoteFrame::dispatches` reports as starting work, reads catalogs with `RemoteCatalog`, reports as its `max_frame_bytes` the smaller of the host policy and the limit the hand's catalog publishes, turns a refused or oversized batch into the SDK's unsent error, and turns the frames a host refuses alone into `CodeModeHostError::refused`. Protocol, turn and event logic belongs in the SDK. Code Mode cells (`src/wasm/cells.rs` over the SDK's `LocalCells`, on the Cloudflare and Vercel runtimes alike) stream their `text()` and `console` output while they run as `tool.notification` events on the cell's call, each carrying only the output since the previous one (the client appends them), a stopped cell's output since it last returned is appended to the stopped `exec` or `wait` result the model reads, and a cell's top-level declarations are script globals, so `(0,eval)('name')` sees a top-level `const` (SDK 40039e3; before it, output arrived only when a cell yielded or ended and a stopped cell's output was lost). Since SDK 59c5cd9 Stop also terminates a session's first cell, which before kept running after its turn was cancelled because the executor counted its host as touched only once a cell returned, and a cell stopped mid-run no longer traps the module. SDK 59c5cd9 avoided the trap by leaking the stopped cell's QuickJS runtime (about 2.4 MiB per Stop for a cell holding 20,000 small objects: 50 stop/run cycles grew the module's memory from 3.0 to 130.6 MiB in Node 26); since SDK 6fecd99 the runtime is freed again, because rquickjs 0.14 bundles quickjs-ng 0.16, whose cycle collector no longer frees a suspended coroutine's captured locals twice (quickjs-ng 7955cfd49), and the same 50 cycles end at 9.8 MiB. Since SDK 4eb91c4 a cell's consecutive `text()` and `console` output is merged into one item in the engine and again when buffered, and the Claude backend drops text blocks past its 12,000-token tool-result budget: before, a cell running `for (let i = 0; i < 200000; i++) console.log('row ' + i)` crashed the nanocode.xyz/cloudflare Durable Object (Haiku 5.5, 2026-10-25), and in Node it grew the module to 263 MB and sent the model 200,000 blocks (9.1 MB); it now peaks at 24 MB and sends one 53 KB block. `Cargo.lock` pins rquickjs 0.14.0 to match the SDK. Since SDK 263e261 `SessionLog` and `SqlStore` record their storage formats in a `nanocode_formats` table and migrate on first use, so a Durable Object or Node database an older build wrote migrates in place (checked 2026-10-25 by opening `scripts/measure-node.ts`'s SQLite database written by the previous WASM release with the new module: both components recorded format 1, the history replayed and a third turn completed), and a build that finds a newer format refuses the storage with `StoreError::UnsupportedFormat` instead of reading it.

Install Rust 1.97 or newer, the `wasm32-unknown-unknown` target, libclang (bindgen generates QuickJS's bindings for this target; set `LIBCLANG_PATH` if it is not found) and Node. `scripts/build.mjs` compiles QuickJS with wasi-sdk: it uses `WASI_SDK_PATH` or downloads wasi-sdk 25 into `target/`, and sets `CC_wasm32_unknown_unknown`, `AR_wasm32_unknown_unknown`, `CFLAGS_wasm32_unknown_unknown`, `BINDGEN_EXTRA_CLANG_ARGS_wasm32_unknown_unknown` and `WASI_SYSROOT` unless set (the C flags force-include the SDK's `crates/runtime/code-mode-engine/wasm/quickjs-wasi.h`, which keeps QuickJS's stack limit on, and define its hash, because Cargo does not rebuild `rquickjs-sys` when only that header changes); a bare `cargo check --target wasm32-unknown-unknown` needs the same variables.js 22.18 or newer. Run `npm ci`, `cargo check --locked --lib --target wasm32-unknown-unknown`, `npm run check` and `npm run build`. Run SDK checks from `../nanocodex` with `cargo check --locked --lib` and `cargo check --locked --lib --target wasm32-unknown-unknown`. Dependency resolution is pinned by both lockfiles; after moving `sdk-revision.json`, refresh `Cargo.lock` with `cargo metadata --format-version 1` so `--locked` builds pass.

`npm run build` runs `scripts/build.mjs`, which builds the WASM from source and writes `dist/nanocode-wasm.json`, then `npm run build:ts`, which compiles `src/` into `dist/` with `tsconfig.build.json`, which rewrites the `.ts` import extensions to `.js`. The script fingerprints `Cargo.toml`, `Cargo.lock`, every `src` Rust file, the script itself, `../nanocodex/Cargo.toml`, `../nanocodex/Cargo.lock`, every file under `../nanocodex/crates`, toolchain and Cargo configuration files, `rustc --version --verbose` and the `CARGO_` and `RUST` environment, and skips Cargo when the fingerprint and the artifact's digest match `output/wasm-build-fingerprint`. It writes `dist/nanocode_wasm.wasm` only when its bytes change, so a consumer's watcher does not rebuild for unchanged WASM. Local compilation keeps incremental artifacts without debug information. The optimized build uses the `wasm` profile with `opt-level = "s"`: on a 110-turn Worker session with 200 deltas per reply, two runs each measured WASM CPU per turn at 24.0 and 30.5 ms with `"z"` (7.39 MB, 2.31 MB gzipped), 19.1 and 17.8 ms with `"s"` (8.01 MB, 2.48 MB) and 8.4 and 18.7 ms with `3` (10.51 MB, 3.39 MB); `"s"` keeps most of the CPU saving without the larger module every cold start of a hibernated session compiles. QuickJS's C code alone builds at `opt-level = 3` (`[profile.wasm.package.rquickjs-sys]`, which cargo passes to the cc crate's `OPT_LEVEL`): on 2026-10-27 in `bench/hands.ts --hand=local` a 10^7-iteration loop took 547 ms instead of 603, 100,000 objects through `JSON.stringify` and `JSON.parse` 147 instead of 171, a million string appends 127 instead of 162, a 1 MB regex replace and match 170 instead of 214 and a million small objects 322 instead of 366, for 370 KB more module (12,312,072 against 11,941,970 bytes, 88 KB more gzipped); `opt-level = 3` for the whole module gave 15,960,957 bytes (5.19 MB gzipped) and turns no faster beyond QuickJS (an empty cell's turn 8.9 against 8.7 ms). The profile builds without LTO: on 2026-10-24 on the hosted Hand, relinking after an SDK change took 16 s instead of 34 s with fat LTO and one codegen unit, the module grew from 11,222,053 to 12,674,874 bytes (3,484,092 to 3,852,862 gzipped), and `measure-cold.mjs --rounds 9` measured the same turn times within run-to-run variation (Claude warm turn 54 ms against 67 ms with fat LTO, Codex 54 against 49, cold opens 59 and 73 against 61 and 60 ms). It builds with one codegen unit: on 2026-10-25 on the hosted Hand against SDK 26864d07d, it shrank the module from 13,054,263 to 11,549,861 bytes (3,975,559 to 3,596,692 gzipped, 2,615,092 to 2,412,507 with Brotli), while 2 and 4 units gave 12,105,798 and 12,461,867 bytes and one unit for registry crates alone 12,986,322. In Miniflare's workerd (nanocode-cloudflare-durable `measure-cold-local.mjs`, 6-turn sessions, 5 interleaved runs of 3 rounds) the median cold turn reached its first model request in 99 ms instead of 105 ms for Claude and 120 ms instead of 137 ms for Codex, and runtime open took 44 and 66 ms instead of 47 and 74 ms, with warm turns unchanged. A full build took 63 s instead of 53 s, and relinking after touching `nanocode-backend-codex` took 29 s instead of 14.5 s. `scripts/build.mjs` runs Binaryen 133's `wasm-opt -O1` over both modules after Cargo (2026-11-07). It uses `BINARYEN_PATH`, or downloads the host's release into `target/` and checks its pinned SHA-256. It names the features Rust 1.97 emits for this target, because the stripped module has no `target_features` section, and `--all-features` emitted imports that V8 refused (`unknown import kind 0x7f`). The optimized module is cached beside the Cargo artifact, and its digest is recorded in `output/wasm-build-fingerprint`.

workerd compiles every WebAssembly module of a Worker when it loads the script, whether or not code imports it, and a dynamic `import()` does not defer that. With workerd 1.20261019.1 (median of 7 spawns), a Worker that answers `ok` served its first request 16 ms after spawn, 47 ms with the 13.9 MB module listed but never imported, 48 ms with it imported statically and 47 ms with it imported dynamically. So every isolate of a host Worker pays the module's decode and validation, agent routes or not, and `interpreter.wasm` adds 5.4 ms (single-threaded compile in Node). Measured on 2026-11-07 against the module of build-4e35cf7 (WASM build d4b4afd5b394bd57dd13) on an Apple M-series laptop at load average 30 to 65:

| | before | `-O1` | `-Os` | `-Oz` |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| bytes | 13,877,267 | 12,373,610 | 11,749,409 | 11,585,656 |
| gzip -9 | 4,240,799 | 4,040,208 | 4,243,531 | 4,215,753 |
| Brotli 11 | 2,758,408 | 2,706,862 | 2,897,354 | 2,908,652 |
| `wasm-opt` time | | 3.8 s | 17.2 s | 23.1 s |
| `new WebAssembly.Module`, `node --single-threaded`, median of 31 | 45.7 ms | 43.8 ms | 42.1 ms | 41.7 ms |
| function bodies Liftoff compiles in a `measure-cold.ts` round, Claude | 2,122,410 B, 5,837 functions | 1,892,208 B, 5,424 | | 2,150,392 B, 3,696 |
| the same for Codex | 2,212,796 B, 6,230 functions | 1,959,484 B, 5,783 | | 2,287,402 B, 3,895 |

The compiled bodies come from `--trace-wasm-compilation-times` in the round's child process, which is deterministic where timings on the loaded machine were not. `-O1` removes 11% of the bytes that the cold path compiles lazily and 200 KB of a host Worker's gzipped upload. `-Os` and `-Oz` add gzip or Brotli bytes, and `-Oz` inlines into fewer, larger functions, so the cold path compiles more bytes. Turn times in `bench/hands.ts --hand=local` and `measure-cold.ts` showed no difference outside run-to-run variation, and `scripts/measure-node.ts` and every `bench/hands.ts` cell passed with the `-O1` module. A Linux x86_64 container built the same `-O1` module byte for byte as macOS arm64. `interpreter.wasm` goes from 1,348,461 to 1,213,480 bytes (305,876 to 298,791 gzipped). `tracing` is built with `max_level_off`: the package installs no subscriber, so every span and event was already disabled at run time, and compiling them out saves 67 KB.

`node scripts/measure-size.ts` rebuilds the module with symbols into `target/size` and writes its size by crate and its 200 largest functions to `output/wasm-size.json`; it needs `twiggy` (`cargo install twiggy`). Compare sizes built in one directory layout: the checkout's location changes the crates' hashes and with them fat LTO's output, and on 2026-10-19 one Worker commit built in two sibling directories of equal path length measured 8,344,975 and 8,353,723 bytes. Built in each of those two layouts from Worker bc8ac90 and SDK 139e30458, moving the bridge from the Worker into this package and its host-tool adapter into the SDK's `FunctionTool` grew the module by 1,988 and 1,266 bytes, to 8,346,963 and 8,354,989, and by 1,266 and 577 bytes gzipped (`gzip -9`), to 2,530,435 and 2,533,418; at the earlier SDK revision the same move measured 1,971 and 1,987 bytes. The adapter's own code grew by about 300 bytes, since a call now awaits the handler's boxed future inside the tool's, and the rest is LTO output changing with the crate's new name. Moving the turn queue and event log into the SDK's `TurnInbox` and `SessionLog` earlier the same day added 98,798 bytes, 28,234 gzipped, against a build of the same SDK revision without them, and reading every image through `Cursor<&[u8]>` in the SDK removed two extra copies of each image decoder, 247 KB. Nearly all of the module is SDK code that the two providers, subagents and hands use: serde and serde_json code accounts for 1.53 MB of the 7.21 MB of attributed code and functions on serde's buffered `Content` path for tagged and untagged enums for 0.37 MB, image codecs that resize images arriving from hands for 0.45 MB, JSON Schema validation of subagent output contracts (`regress`, `url`, `idna`) for 0.32 MB and arc-swap instantiations for 0.24 MB. Fat LTO, which the profile used until 2026-10-24, removed the native-only crates of the `nanocode` facade, which contributed 2 KB, and schemars, which contributed nothing. SDK 596d189d2 parses the subagent tools' literal JSON schemas from text instead of expanding `json!` macros, which shrank the module from 8,365,079 to 8,307,405 bytes (2,549,074 to 2,543,220 gzipped) and the function bodies V8 compiles before a restored session's first model request from 1,383,876 to 1,318,292 bytes for Claude and from 1,562,804 to 1,497,194 for Codex.

`node scripts/measure-node.ts` runs the package in Node with `node:sqlite` as the SQL host and an in-process mock provider: for each backend it opens a session whose model runs one Code Mode cell per turn that calls the host tool `lookup`, checks that every request offers only `exec` and `wait` and carries no credential, that the tool's result reaches the model and, with `stream_tool_input`, that the logged `tool.input.delta` events of each call concatenate to its arguments, runs two turns, disposes the runtime, reopens the same database in a new instance, checks that the logged events survive and the next request replays the history, and writes timings, model requests, host tool calls and SQL calls to `output/`. On 2026-10-19 on an Apple Silicon laptop, across two runs of both backends, instantiating the compiled module took 0.3 to 0.7 ms, opening a new session 9 to 27 ms, reopening one 6 to 11 ms, a new session's first turn 33 to 50 ms and its second, each with one host tool call, 11 to 16 ms. `NANOCODE_WASM_PATH` selects another compiled module.

`node scripts/measure-cold.ts [--providers claude,codex] [--rounds 5] [--history 20] [--reply-bytes 20000]` measures the cold path step by step: it builds a session per backend with `--history` turns whose replies carry `--reply-bytes` of text, then in a fresh Node process per round compiles the module, instantiates it, runs a reader command, opens the runtime and runs a turn to its first model request and to completion, runs a warm turn, and repeats instantiation, reader, open and turn on a second instance of the same compiled module, so the difference between the two isolates V8's lazy compilation of the code the cold path runs. It records each store and log transaction the open runs and writes medians and rounds to `output/`. On 2026-10-20 on an Apple Silicon laptop under load from other builds, before the SQL reply and checkpoint reuse changes, a 10-turn Claude session with 20 KB replies took 10 ms to compile, 1.5 ms to instantiate, 4 ms for its first reader command, 28 ms to open and 22 ms more to its first model request, against 0.4, 0.2, 5.5 and 5.7 ms on the second instance; `--no-wasm-lazy-compilation` moved that cost into a 65 ms compile, and V8 compiled 1.38 MB of function bodies before the first Claude request and 1.57 MB before the first Codex one, spread over about 3,300 functions.

A runtime opens without work that grew with its shared tools (SDK 0f6420ac2 and b6675d29b, 2026-11-07). `open` added each shared tool (host tools, MCP tools, web search, image generation) to a provider's tool set with `ToolCollection::add_shared_tool`, which rebuilt the set and validated every tool in it again, and the SDK's Codex, Claude, subagent and runtime tool installs did the same, so a session with tempoxyz/gateway's 113 MCP tools validated about 6,600 tool definitions per provider, and Claude's `ClaudeTools` reinstalled its catalog once per tool; `ToolCollection::add_shared_tools` now adds them in one build, which reports the same first error, and every caller uses it. The MCP tool search index is built by the first `tool_search` instead of whenever a server's listing completes. `calendar_date` answers the UTC zones (the SDK's default `Etc/UTC` and its aliases) from `Date` instead of `Intl.DateTimeFormat`, whose first use loads ICU's time zone data, and keeps one formatter per other zone. In a CPU profile of 40 opens of an already compiled module in Node, `ToolsBuilder::build` under `add_shared_tool` took 440 of the 900 ms spent in WebAssembly, and in a local workerd profile of a cold turn `local_date` took 22 ms of the open. Measured on an Apple M-series laptop at load average 5 to 7 with a gateway-shaped session (both harnesses, a workspace, web search, subagent budgets and one MCP server answering the gateway's 117 KB, 113-tool `tools/list`), comparing modules built in one checkout before and after the SDK change: in Node, opening on an already compiled module took 14 to 15 ms instead of 24 to 27 for Codex and 9 to 10 instead of 20 for Claude, and a fresh process's cold open 75 to 79 instead of 88 to 90 ms and 61 to 64 instead of 75 to 83 (three interleaved runs each); in nanocode-cloudflare-durable's `measure-cold-local.mjs` with its local MCP server answering the same list (5 rounds, three interleaved runs), the cold runtime open took 86 to 92 ms instead of 114 to 124 for Codex and 68 to 72 instead of 92 to 98 for Claude, the cold turn's workerd CPU 160 to 170 ms instead of 200 to 220 and 140 to 150 instead of 170 to 190, and warm turns were unchanged. Most of what remains of a cold open is V8 compiling the functions it runs for the first time: with `--no-wasm-lazy-compilation` the cold Codex open in Node took 60 to 65 ms against 15 ms on a compiled module, and a cold Codex turn compiles about 2.7 MB of function bodies (`--trace-wasm-compilation-times` on a build with symbol names: 1.5 MB in the open, mostly the Codex backend, durability restore and the MCP catalog). `scripts/measure-node.ts`, every `bench/hands.ts --hand=local` cell, the durability restart scenarios (`plain`, `sleep`, `compute`, `yield`) and the MCP resume scenarios passed on the new module, and so did nanocode-cloudflare-durable's `npm run drill`.

`node scripts/benchmark-transfer.ts 65536 256` compares JSON byte-array round trips with the raw memory copy the bridge uses for HTTP bodies, for 16 MiB of data. It isolates serialization cost, not inference throughput or full application speed; chunk size and count are benchmark inputs.

Keep reports in ignored `output/` and build state in ignored `target/` and `dist/`. Host-specific measurements, such as Durable Object storage rows, workerd CPU and production latency, belong to the consuming host's repository.

`src/client/internal/Pause.ts` holds the abortable sleep that the Cloudflare and Vercel transports and `nanocode-ts/machines`'s cloud link used as three copies, and `bash/internal/commands/wasm.ts`, `zip.ts` and `Mount.ts` take `toBase64`, `fromBase64`, `transform` and `inside` from `Fs.ts`, `archive.ts` and `Prune.ts` instead of their own copies (2026-11-07). The cell WebAssembly host's base64 now goes through the shell's codec when a host sets one. Checked with `npm run check:types`, `npm run build:ts`, `scripts/measure-node.ts`, `bench/hands.ts --hand=local` (all 28 cells), a shell run of gzip, tar, zip, a mount and `wasm --invoke`, and both demos' `npm run drill` on this tree packed with `npm pack` (nanocode-cloudflare-durable: 3 scenarios clean, as on build-84af8f7; nanocode-vercel-durable: 4 of 4 clean with one charge each, the same as build-84af8f7). Installing the packed tarball with `npm install <tgz>` left `node_modules/nanocode-ts/node_modules/undici` empty, because the lock marks it `inBundle`, so the Vercel drill copied the packed `dist/` over an `npm ci` install instead.

`nanocode-ts/react/agent` (2026-11-09) is a whole demo chat in one component, so a host page writes only its transport: `Agent` takes `theme` (`codex`, `claude` or `nanocode`), the harnesses and models, an optional `planHarnesses` list, prompts, a footer, the Tempo Wallet `wallet` options and `connect({ headers, selection, session })`, which returns the host's `transport`, `keyring` and optional `plans` client. It keeps the model picked per conversation and its thinking in `localStorage` under `storage` (`<storage>:chatgpt` and `<storage>:claude` for the clones' histories, `<storage>` for the nanocode chat and `<storage>:token` for an access token), so pages that used the demos' own copies keep their conversations. `codex` and `claude` render the ChatGPT and Claude clones with the wallet button, and the composer's + opens a menu (`CloneProps.menu`, `Attach` in `clones/Shared.tsx`): add files, run on your machine (the hand command in `CloneProps.panel` above the composer) and use your ChatGPT or Claude plan when the host passes `plans`. `nanocode` renders `Chat` with `/model`, `/effort`, `/new`, `/hand`, the wallet commands, `PlanConnect`, `HandConnect` and `Unlock`, as the Cloudflare and Vercel home pages did. A hand resets only when the session changes from one id to another: on Vercel the hand request opens the session, and resetting on that first id closed the panel it had just opened. Checked on 2026-11-09 with a local pack in nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare under `wrangler dev` and nanocodexyz/durable-vercel under `next start`: the three themes rendered, the + menu opened, the hand panel showed its command on both hosts, the Claude plan sign-in opened on Cloudflare, and the nanocode chat showed the plan, hand and wallet rows. No model turn ran.

`Agent` warms its session at the first sign of a visitor (2026-11-09): the first `pointermove`, `pointerdown`, `touchstart` or `keydown` on the window calls `transport.warm()` once per page, in every theme. The nanocode chat warmed only when text was typed (the clones also on the composer's focus and pointer), so a prompt submitted at once, as a click on a suggestion is, sent its warm with its turn and paid the whole cold start: a new Durable Object, the runtime's open and the first request through the AI binding, which a trace of three new Haiku 5.5 sessions on the deployed Cloudflare demo that day put at 1.4 to 2.3 s to the turn's `queued` answer, 0.2 to 0.7 s to open the runtime and 2.7 to 3.8 s for a model call that took 0.6 s on the session's second turn with the same 10,831 cached tokens, while a session warmed first finished its first turn in 981 to 1,394 ms (`output/first-turn/trace.mjs` and `warm-trace.mjs` in nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare). The warm request itself took 2.2 to 4.3 s. Measured in headless Chromium against the deployed demo's sessions API (`output/first-turn/intent-ab.mjs` there, which serves the page built on a local pack of this change in place of the deployed one, alternating the two): a visitor who moved the pointer, waited 3 s and submitted "Say only the word hello." at once read the reply a median 1,139 ms after Enter (1,041 to 1,618, 6 rounds) instead of 4,043 ms (2,325 to 6,360), and with a 1 s wait 3,699 ms (4 rounds) instead of 4,040. A page nobody touches, such as one a crawler loads, sends no warm. The listener is removed after its first event, because `warmMs` (10 s) would otherwise let a moving pointer warm the session again and again.

The site header and the playground (2026-11-09). `nanocode-ts/react/site`'s `Header` lists Home, Experiments, Docs and GitHub, as nanocode.xyz's own navigation now does: Experiments is a list of posts at nanocode.xyz/experiments instead of a dropdown, and Playground and Prompts are gone (the prompts moved onto nanocode.xyz's home page). `Header`'s `experiment` prop still marks Experiments as the current section. `Demos` switches between Cloudflare and Vercel only, since nanocode-machines is a detail of the machine payments experiment rather than a demo of its own. `nanocode-ts/react/playground` (`Playground`, `Playground.styles.ts`) is removed with the playground pages of nanocode.xyz and nanocodexyz/durable-cloudflare.

The looks moved to nanocodexyz/nds (2026-11-09; Georgios: "move all the zyzz themes into nds, so that the story is easy demos, composing the headless with my own nds"). This package keeps behavior: the headless components and hooks of `nanocode-ts/react` and `nanocode-ts/machines/react`, which render `data-nc` elements and take a `Skin`, and the `Skin` contract of part names. nds, the nanocode design system, takes every Zyzz definition: `Theme.ts` (its `tokens`, `style`, `variants`, `vars` and keyframes are `nds`'s root), `Skin.ts` with `Skin.styles.ts` and `Machines.styles.ts` (`nds/skin`), `Themes.ts` with the ChatGPT, Claude and Gemini styles (`nds/themes`), `Site.tsx` with `Site.styles.ts` (`nds/site`), `Clones.tsx` with `clones/` (`nds/clones`) and `Agent.tsx` (`nds/agent`), and the stylesheet is `nds/zyzz.css`. `Agent` and the clones went with them because they import the skin and carry stylesheets of their own; in nds they import this package's public entrypoints (`nanocode-ts`, `/client`, `/machines`, `/machines/react`, `/react`, `/highlight`), so nothing they need is private here. The seven exports `./react/skin`, `./react/site`, `./react/theme`, `./react/themes`, `./react/agent`, `./react/clones` and `./react/zyzz.css` are removed, a breaking change for hosts, which import the same names from nds. `scripts/zyzz.mjs` and the `zyzz` dependency are gone, so `npm run build:ts` is `tsc` and the Pyodide step, `dist/react` holds plain modules with no `.css` or `.zyzz.json` beside them, and `sideEffects` is `false`. Checked on 2026-11-09: `npm run check:types` passed; nds at 700c3d5 built against build-07238e5's headless modules, its playground rendered a scripted turn under the nanocode and Claude themes, and durable-cloudflare at 4c96981 with its three imports pointed at the packed nds built and served its nanocode and Claude pages under `wrangler dev`.
- The repository has a README: what the package is in one paragraph, the host and module diagram of the guide, the install line and a table of six entrypoints. Georgios asked for it on 2026-11-09 ("durable-cloudflare and durable-vercel must include diagrams and be minimal otherwise, a very short README for nanocode-ts"), which this file's rule against README edits does not cover: that rule keeps a change from rewriting a README as a side effect. The diagram is a Mermaid fence rendered by Ondine (`ondine README.md --svg --out-dir assets`, wevm/ondine 0.0.x from nanocode-site's `node_modules`), which writes `assets/README-1.light.svg` and `.dark.svg` and keeps the source in a comment, so `ondine README.md --restore` returns the fence for an edit. Its alt text is the fence's `accTitle`.
- The README's diagram is one image, `assets/host.svg`, with a white background of its own (2026-11-09). The `<picture>` Ondine writes picks the dark image by the reader's operating system (`prefers-color-scheme`), not by GitHub's theme, and both images were transparent: Georgios saw white lines and black boxes on a white page, with no arrows visible. Checked in headless Chromium with the system dark and GitHub's page light: `currentSrc` was `README-1.dark.svg`. The source is `assets/host.mmd`, rendered with `ondine assets/host.mmd` (wevm/ondine 0.0.3), after which the SVG's `viewBox` grows by 16 units a side and a white `rect` goes under the drawing.

A session resolves its plan itself, and only when a plan could pay (2026-11-09), asked for by Georgios ("plans and egress are not optimized yet and must also work by default with MPP: minimal or zero overhead, everything warm, parallel, explicit state machines"). `App.create`'s `fetch` used to await `subscribes(account, env, 'claude')` and `('codex')` before every session request, two calls to the account's `PlanVault` object, which a new visitor's first request creates: on the live demo that was a median 878 ms (559 to 1,205, 8 new visitors) of `public_pre_session` before a deployment-paid first turn, and 800 ms before its warm (`output/egress/first.mjs` in durable-cloudflare). The Worker now sends `subscription` and `codex_subscription` false when a host has plans or a `subscription` option (and `MODEL_AUTH` as before when it has neither), and `AgentSession` resolves them: `subscribe(descriptor, provider, payer)` returns at once for the payers `deployment` and `wallet`, which `served` never gave a plan, and otherwise asks for the one provider the session runs on. A bound session resolves once per request after its metrics and events routes (`planned`, from its stored provider and payer); an unbound session resolves in its first turn, started beside the module's instantiation and awaited where the turn binds its payer, and in its warm. So a deployment-paid or wallet-paid session never reaches a vault, a plan-paid one makes one call where it made two, and a host's own `subscription` option is now called from the session object.
The warm request tells the egress who pays: its `x-nanocode-model` header carries `authority`, `subscription` and `payer` beside `provider` and `model`, so `Wallets.egress` routes a wallet session's warm to the wallet and nanocode-egress build-ec47028 (which this moves to) warms a plan session's vault, credential and relay container. Both used to warm the deployment's gateway, which those sessions do not use. `machines/cloudflare/Egress` answered `/warm` with 204 and did nothing: it now calls `Wallet.warm()`, which unseals the account's access key and builds its payer when a key is registered, so the first model request of a wallet session does not start the `Wallet` object. It sends no request and signs nothing: authorizing the key on chain and opening a payment channel stay with the first payment.
Checked on 2026-11-09 under `wrangler dev` with `CHAT_PUBLIC:1` on durable-cloudflare with this build packed: `GET /plans` and a session's hands answered, warms with the payers `wallet` (no key), `plan` (signed out) and none answered 204, a turn with the payer `plan` answered 403 "sign in to your Claude plan first, or choose another payer", a turn with the payer `wallet` was queued, and `npm run drill` passed there. Not checked: a wallet-paid or plan-paid model request, which need a funded Tempo Wallet key and a signed-in plan.

`Chat` takes `connections` (2026-11-09), asked for by Georgios ("the general nanocode-ts component could be improved on how it provides UX for connecting things or running", and the demo pages' own Connect picker was missing from the home page's chat, which uses `Chat` directly). A `Connection` is `{ id, label, detail, connect(), status }`, and `status` is one union: `idle`, `pending` with an optional `panel`, `connected` with what it is connected as, an optional `panel` and an optional `disconnect`, or `failed` with its error. Given any, `Chat` adds one `connect` action beside `history` (it reads `connecting…` or `connected (n)`), the `/connect` command, a picker that lists each connection with its state, the panel of whichever is pending below the transcript, and a row to try a failed one again. So a host no longer builds its own picker, tracks which panel to show, or renders idle rows. Three builders make connections from the existing hooks: `handConnection(useHand(...))`, `planConnections(usePlans(...))` and `walletConnection(useUnlock(...))` from `nanocode-ts/machines/react`. `usePlans` reports `asked`, the provider of the last sign-in or sign-out, so a plan's error marks that plan failed and not both. `HandConnect`, `PlanConnect`, `Prompts` and `extras` are unchanged for hosts that place them themselves. Checked on 2026-11-09 with this build packed into nds and durable-cloudflare, in headless Chromium at 1200 and 390 px: the action opened the picker with Tempo Wallet, ChatGPT plan, Claude plan and Your machine, and choosing Your machine showed its command panel. `npm run check:types` passed.
The payment libraries load on first use (2026-11-09, part of a pass Georgios asked for over "everything that sits on top of the Rust code"). `src/machines/Account.ts` imported viem, ox and mppx at module scope, and every host of `nanocode-ts/cloudflare` or `nanocode-ts/machines/server` evaluated them, with Zod behind mppx, when its isolate or process started, whether or not a wallet ever paid. The account's code now lives in `src/machines/internal/Core.ts`, and `Account.ts` keeps the types, `digest`, `json`, `sealer`, `visitor`, `keys`, `envelope`, `forward`, `paying` and `device`, with `create` and `verify` as wrappers that `import()` the core at their first call. `create` returns the same object, whose methods were all asynchronous already. Measured that day against nanocode-cloudflare-durable e5c372e with build-cd68d9e and with this change copied over it. In the Worker's bundle (`wrangler deploy --dry-run`), classing each module by whether esbuild wrapped it for lazy initialization, the source evaluated at startup fell from 3.04 MB to 1.46 MB: Zod 0.73 MB, viem 0.33 MB, mppx 0.30 MB and ox 0.17 MB left it, and the simulated shell's 1.01 MB is what remains. The bundle grew from 4,829,500 to 5,072,931 bytes for the wrappers (2,562,277 to 2,643,155 minified). In local workerd 1.20261012.1, a Worker with one SQLite Durable Object class that imports the whole bundle answered its first request to a new object 232 ms after spawn before and 209 ms after, against 67 ms for the same Worker without the import (medians of 15 interleaved spawns, twice), and 206 and 182 ms with both bundles minified. Wrangler's upload reported a Worker Startup Time of 54, 80 and 103 ms for that probe before and 14 and 17 ms after. On the deployed probes (`nanocode-probe-full` and `nanocode-probe-split` in Tempo Development Resources, deleted afterwards) a new object answered its Worker in 487 ms before and 478 ms after (medians of 40 interleaved rounds, ranges 210 to 763 and 174 to 749), so the gain there is inside the variation, and probes with only the two WebAssembly modules (294 ms) or only the JavaScript (295 ms) beside an empty one (190 ms) put half of the bundle's cost in the modules, which this does not touch. In Node 22, `import('nanocode-ts/machines/server')` took 767 ms before and 3 ms after, and the first `verify` then took 773 ms. `verify({})` answered the same 400 and a new account the same empty status before and after, nanocode-cloudflare-durable's `npm run drill` passed with the change, and `npm run check:types` passed.
Two candidates from the same pass were measured and left alone (2026-11-09). Loading the simulated shell's larger commands on first use: with `awk`, `jq`, `diff`, `sed`, `git` and the `extra` and `text` command tables replaced by stubs in nanocode-cloudflare-durable's minified bundle, the bundle fell from 2,582,933 to 2,255,788 bytes and the probe Worker's first request to a new object from 190 ms to 181 ms after spawn in local workerd (medians of 15 interleaved spawns, 66 ms for an empty Worker), so 9 ms is the most that change could give, and it would put every command's `streaming` marker and its tables behind a wrapper. And the `host.sleep` spans of a new session's first turn (7 of them, 150 to 1,800 ms in total, in `output/first-turn/trace.mjs` of nanocode-cloudflare-durable) are not waits: on WebAssembly the SDK's `nanocode_platform::timeout` races its future against `sleep`, so each span is a deadline's timer, from the MCP servers' startup timeout and the event stream's coalescing window, that ends when the work it guards does. In the same trace `wrangler tail` showed the session object's first turn using 419 to 524 ms of CPU against 19 to 32 ms for the second, which is the module's functions compiled at first use, and a `McpListings.read` of 560 to 826 ms beside it, the listings object waking with the same bundle, which the first model request does not wait for.

The shell's `wasm` command bounds every module it runs natively by the shell's deadline and memory budget (2026-11-10). The instrumenter puts its counter-and-check site at the start of every function as well as after every `loop`, and instruments every module, including one with no loop or no code, because a recursive call tree reaches no loop: a naive recursive `fib` ran past its `timeoutMs` until the process was killed. A module the instrumenter cannot parse, or whose instrumented copy does not compile, runs on the interpreter when the host gives one and is refused otherwise; the original bytes are compiled only to report an invalid module's own error. A module that already exports `__nanocode_check` is refused, since the appended export of that name made the instrumented copy fail to compile. Each memory, defined or imported, is checked against `limits.maxMemoryBytes` (or `Wasm.Options.memoryBytes` when that is smaller) in 64 KiB pages: a larger minimum is refused, and each defined memory gets a maximum no larger than the budget, so `memory.grow` past it returns -1. The budget is part of the compiled-module cache key. `fd_allocate` and `fd_filestat_set_size` return `FBIG` past the `maxBufferLength * 4` per-file size that `fd_write` already enforces, read their sizes as unsigned 64-bit values, and `fd_allocate` needs write rights like `fd_filestat_set_size`; the bytes held by the program's file buffers together stay within `limits.maxMemoryBytes`. Checked with `npm run check:types` and `output/wasm-limits.ts` (assembled with the shell's `wat2wasm`): recursive `fib 60` with a 1000 ms deadline exits 124 after about 1,005 ms with and without an unrelated loop, the reserved export and `(memory 16385)` (with or without code) exit 126, `memory.grow 16384` from one page returns -1, `fd_allocate` and `fd_filestat_set_size` of 3 GiB return 22, and 4,096 and 100 bytes still work. The function-entry check costs the most on call-bound code: `output/wasm-bench.ts`, `wasm --invoke fib fib.wasm 32` (7 million calls) in Node 24 on a Linux x86_64 VM, took a median 33 to 36 ms against 15 to 17 ms before (three interleaved runs of 7).
`/wallet` says what the key has paid on the site, not what the site's server paid with it (2026-11-10): a host whose `Keyring` keeps the key in the tab and pays from there, as the home page of nanocode.xyz now does with `Payments.create`, reports its own ledger through `status`, and the sentence named a server that host does not have. `npm run check:types` passed.
